<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:headline}
Let $U\in C^4([0,h_{\mathrm{a}}])$ with $U(0)=0$ and $U'>0$. Let the neutrally
stratified atmosphere $(\bar{\rho}_0,a)$ satisfy the regularity and
hydrostatic assumptions of \S\ref{subsec:hypotheses}. Let $k>0$ be a horizontal wavenumber such that the free wave speed $c_k$ at this wavenumber
lies in $U((0,h_{\mathrm{a}}))$, so that there is exactly one critical level
$z_c\in(0,h_{\mathrm{a}})$ with $U(z_c)=c_k$, and such that the wave-relative
flow $U-c$ is uniformly subsonic for $c$ near $c_k$. Assume
\begin{equation}
Q(z_c)=U''(z_c)+\frac{g}{a^{2}(z_c)}\,U'(z_c)<0 .
\end{equation}
Then there are $\varepsilon_0>0$ and $r_0>0$ with the following properties
for every air--water density ratio $\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_0)$. The linearized
problem has exactly one unstable phase-speed root $c_\varepsilon$ in the set $\{c\in\mathbb{C}:\ |c-c_k|<r_0,\ \operatorname{Im} c>0\}$, and the space of normal
modes at this root is one-dimensional. The root satisfies
$c_\varepsilon=c_k+\varepsilon c_1+O(\varepsilon^{2}(1+\log(1/\varepsilon)))$ with an
explicit $c_1$, and the growth rate is
$\gamma_\varepsilon=k\operatorname{Im} c_\varepsilon>0$. The number
$\lambda_\varepsilon=-\mathrm{i} kc_\varepsilon$ is an isolated, algebraically simple
eigenvalue of the closed, densely defined operator $\mathbb{L}_k$ of
\cref{thm:operator}, the linearized evolution operator at wavenumber
$k$.
If, in addition, the equation of state satisfies the thermodynamic regularity in Equation~\eqref{eq:thermo-domain}, then $\mathbb{L}_k$ is the linearization of the nonlinear two-phase problem in the sense of \cref{thm:operator}.
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
This is \cref{cor:monotone}.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- example-1: body_md -->
\label{ex:polytropic}
Consider an ideal polytropic gas with equation of state $P(\rho,S)=K(S)\rho^\gamma$, where $\gamma>1$ is the adiabatic exponent and $K(S_\ast)>0$.
At constant specific entropy $S_\ast$, hydrostatic balance gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:polytropic}
a^2(z)=a_0^2-(\gamma-1)gz,
\qquad
\rho_0(z)=\rho_0(0)\Bigl(\frac{a^2(z)}{a_0^2}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{\gamma-1}},
\end{equation}
where $a_0:=a(0)$.
We require $h_{\mathrm{a}}<a_0^2/((\gamma-1)g)$ so that the sound speed and density are positive on $[0,h_{\mathrm{a}}]$.

Both the sound speed and density decrease with height.
For air with $a_0\approx340\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$ and $\gamma=1.4$, the limiting height is approximately $29.5\,\mathrm{km}$.
Over the lowest $100\,\mathrm m$, the sound speed decreases by approximately $0.2\,\%$.
<!-- end example-1 -->
<!-- example-2: body_md -->
\label{ex:constant-sound-speed}
Consider the equation of state $P(\rho,S)=a_{\mathrm e}^2\rho+p_{\mathrm e}(S)$, where $a_{\mathrm e}>0$ is constant and $p_{\mathrm e}$ is smooth.
At constant specific entropy $S_\ast$, hydrostatic balance gives
\begin{equation}
a^2(z)=a_{\mathrm e}^2,
\qquad
\rho_0(z)=\rho_0(0)e^{-gz/a_{\mathrm e}^2}.
\end{equation}
The sound speed and density are positive on $[0,h_{\mathrm{a}}]$ for every finite $h_{\mathrm{a}}>0$.

The sound speed is constant, while the density decreases exponentially with scale height $a_{\mathrm e}^2/g$, the height over which it decreases by the factor $e$.
Thus varying $a_{\mathrm e}$ changes both the sound speed and the density scale height.
This equation of state admits a neutrally stratified atmosphere with constant sound speed.
For an ideal gas with constant adiabatic exponent, constant sound speed implies an isothermal atmosphere, which is stably stratified.
For uniform wind, the present model leads to a normal-mode equation with constant coefficients, as shown in \S\ref{sec:examples}.
<!-- end example-2 -->
<!-- hypothesis-1: body_md -->
\label{hyp:all}
\leavevmode
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(H\arabic*)},leftmargin=3.2em]
\item\label{hyp:reg} The base-state wind velocity $U$, normalized air density $\bar{\rho}_0$, and sound speed $a$ satisfy
\begin{equation}
U\in C^4([0,h_{\mathrm{a}}]),
\qquad
\bar{\rho}_0,a\in C^3([0,h_{\mathrm{a}}]).
\end{equation}
The density and sound speed are positive on $[0,h_{\mathrm{a}}]$ and satisfy the neutrality relation in Equation~\eqref{eq:neutral}.
\item\label{hyp:noslip} The horizontal velocity of the base wind vanishes at the interface,
\begin{equation}
U(0)=0.
\end{equation}
Because the water is at rest in the base state, this condition makes the base velocity continuous across the air--water interface.
\item\label{hyp:critical} The critical levels associated with the reference phase speed $c_k$ are the heights at which the base wind velocity satisfies $U(z)=c_k$.
Every such level in $[0,h_{\mathrm{a}}]$ lies in $(0,h_{\mathrm{a}})$ and satisfies $U'(z)\neq0$.
We call these \emph{regular critical levels} and, when present, denote them by $z_1<\dots<z_m$.
\item\label{hyp:subsonic} The wind velocity relative to a wave of real phase speed $c$ is uniformly subsonic near $c_k$.
More precisely, there exists a constant $\delta_0>0$ such that
\begin{equation}
a^2(z)-(U(z)-c)^2\ge\delta_0\,a^2(z)
\end{equation}
for every height $z\in[0,h_{\mathrm{a}}]$ and every real phase speed $c$ in a neighborhood of $c_k$.
We call this condition \emph{uniform subsonicity}.
\item\label{hyp:nonres} Let $w_\ast(z;c_r)$ denote the limiting vertical air-velocity amplitude constructed in \cref{prop:limit-solutions} as the complex phase speed $c=c_r+\mathrm{i}\delta$ approaches $c_r$ with $\delta\downarrow0$.
The solution is normalized at the upper boundary by
\begin{equation}
w_\ast(h_{\mathrm{a}};c_r)=0,
\qquad
\partial_z w_\ast(h_{\mathrm{a}};c_r)=1.
\end{equation}
We require its value at the air--water interface to satisfy
\begin{equation}
w_\ast(0;c_r)\neq0
\end{equation}
for every real phase speed $c_r$ in a neighborhood of $c_k$.
We call this condition \emph{non-resonance of the air boundary-value problem}.
It excludes a nontrivial limiting air solution, that is, one that is not identically zero, with both the interface and the upper boundary held fixed at the phase speeds under consideration.
\item\label{hyp:sigma} The surface tension coefficient at the air--water interface satisfies
\begin{equation}
\sigma\ge0.
\end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end hypothesis-1 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
These hypotheses allow us to determine the air-side pressure response near the free water-wave speed $c_k$ and use it to study the effect of wind on the wave.
The regularity of $U$, $\bar{\rho}_0$, and $a$ in Hypothesis \ref{hyp:reg} is needed to differentiate the coefficients of the air equations and to estimate how their solutions vary with the wave speed.
The neutrality relation ensures hydrostatic balance and gives $N^2=0$.
Since the water is at rest in the base state, the condition $U(0)=0$ in Hypothesis \ref{hyp:noslip} makes the horizontal air and water velocities equal at the undisturbed interface, excluding a jump in tangential velocity.
The critical levels in Hypothesis \ref{hyp:critical} are the heights where the wind speed matches the reference wave speed, $U(z_j)=c_k$.
The requirement $U'(z_j)\neq0$ ensures that these heights move smoothly as the wave speed varies.
It also ensures that the coefficient $\bar{\rho}_0 k^2+G/V$ in Equation~\eqref{eq:SL-intro} has at most a simple pole at each critical level, with residue proportional to $Q(z_j)$, which may be zero.
Hypothesis \ref{hyp:subsonic} prevents the denominator $a^2-(U-c)^2$ in the air equations from vanishing for real phase speeds near $c_k$.
Hypothesis \ref{hyp:nonres} concerns the limiting vertical velocity amplitude $w_\ast$, obtained by normalizing the air solution at the upper boundary.
Since $w_\ast(0;c_r)\neq0$, the solution can be rescaled to satisfy the kinematic condition at the moving water surface, and the resulting pressure response is finite.
If $w_\ast(0;c_r)=0$, the limiting air problem instead admits a nontrivial solution with the interface held fixed.
When this zero occurs at $c_r=c_k$, the uncoupled air and water problems share the phase speed $c_k$ at $\varepsilon=0$, which is the resonance that the present perturbation argument excludes (\cref{rem:determinant}).
Non-resonance still allows critical levels where $U(z)=c_r$.
Finally, Hypothesis \ref{hyp:sigma} includes both $\sigma=0$, corresponding to gravity waves, and $\sigma>0$, corresponding to gravity--capillary waves.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
\label{rem:hyp-axis}
For a complex phase speed $c$ with $\operatorname{Im} c\neq0$, the quantities $V=U-c$ and $a^2-V^2$ are nonzero at every height, so the air normal-mode equation has no singular coefficients (\cref{lem:regular-solve}).
Hypotheses \ref{hyp:reg}--\ref{hyp:noslip} are therefore sufficient to construct the holomorphic dispersion function $\mathfrak{E}$ of \cref{def:determinant} and identify its zeros with the eigenvalues $\lambda=-\mathrm{i} kc$ of the operator $\mathbb{L}_k$ of \cref{thm:operator} with $\operatorname{Re}\lambda\neq0$.
The additional Hypotheses \ref{hyp:critical}--\ref{hyp:nonres} are used to control the air solution as $c$ approaches a real phase speed near $c_k$.
This limiting analysis gives the estimates needed for the application of Rouch\'e's theorem that proves instability in \S\ref{sec:instability}.
In some cases, the non-resonance condition in Hypothesis \ref{hyp:nonres} follows from the other assumptions and need not be checked separately.
Specifically, if the critical-level values $Q(z_j)$ are all nonnegative or all nonpositive, with $Q(z_m)\neq0$ at the highest critical level, then \cref{lem:positivity} implies Hypothesis \ref{hyp:nonres}.
This criterion is used in \cref{cor:sufficient,cor:monotone,prop:suppression}.
Hypotheses \ref{hyp:subsonic} and \ref{hyp:nonres} need only be checked at $c_k$.
Since $[0,h_{\mathrm{a}}]$ is compact and $U$ and $a$ are continuous, Hypothesis \ref{hyp:subsonic} holds exactly when $(U(z)-c_k)^2<a^2(z)$ for every $z\in[0,h_{\mathrm{a}}]$.
Hypothesis \ref{hyp:nonres} holds when $w_\ast(0;c_k)\neq0$, because $w_\ast(0;c_r)$ depends continuously on $c_r$ near $c_k$ by \cref{prop:limit-solutions}, which does not use Hypothesis \ref{hyp:nonres}.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:linearized}
In the undisturbed air region $\Omega_{\mathrm a}=\{0<z<h_{\mathrm{a}}\}$, the variations satisfy
\begin{subequations}\label{eq:linear-air}
\begin{align}
&\partial_t r+U\partial_x r+\rho_0'w
 +\rho_0(\partial_xu+\partial_zw)=0,
 \label{eq:lin-cont}\\
&\rho_0\bigl(\partial_tu+U\partial_xu+U'w\bigr)+\partial_xq=0,
 \label{eq:lin-xmom}\\
&\rho_0\bigl(\partial_tw+U\partial_xw\bigr)+\partial_zq=-gr,
 \label{eq:lin-zmom}\\
&\partial_ts+U\partial_xs=0,
 \qquad q=a^2r+P_Ss.
 \label{eq:lin-ent}
\end{align}
\end{subequations}
In the undisturbed water region $\Omega_{\mathrm w}=\{-h_{\mathrm{w}}<z<0\}$, the variations satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{eq:linear-water}
\rho_{\mathrm{w}}\,\partial_t\mathbf v+\nabla q_{\mathrm w}=0,
\qquad
\nabla\cdot\mathbf v=0.
\end{equation}
The linearized kinematic and dynamic conditions at $z=0$ are
\begin{align}
&\partial_t\zeta
=w|_{z=0}
=\left.\mathbf v\cdot\mathbf e_z\right|_{z=0},
 \label{eq:lin-kinematic}\\
&\bigl(q_{\mathrm w}|_{z=0}-\rho_{\mathrm{w}} g\,\zeta\bigr)
-\bigl(q|_{z=0}-\rho_0(0)g\,\zeta\bigr)
=-\sigma\,\partial_x^2\zeta.
 \label{eq:lin-dynamic}
\end{align}
At the fixed top and bottom boundaries, impermeability gives
\begin{equation}
w|_{z=h_{\mathrm{a}}}=0,
\qquad
\left.\mathbf v\cdot\mathbf e_z\right|_{z=-h_{\mathrm{w}}}=0.
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
We first differentiate the governing equations for the air and water in Equations~\eqref{eq:air-nonlinear}--\eqref{eq:water-nonlinear} with respect to $\vartheta$, keeping the spatial coordinates and time fixed, and evaluate at $\vartheta=0$.
In the air phase, the base velocity is $(U(z),0)$, so transport by the base wind produces the operator $\partial_t+U\partial_x$.
The terms $\rho_0'w$ and $U'w$ arise from vertical advection of the base density and the base wind velocity, respectively.
The gravitational force contributes the term $-gr$ to the vertical momentum equation.
The base entropy is constant, so its gradient adds no term to the entropy equation.
Together, these observations give Equation~\eqref{eq:linear-air}.
In the water phase, the base velocity is zero and the density is constant, so the same differentiation gives Equation~\eqref{eq:linear-water}.

We next differentiate the kinematic conditions, taking the interface displacement into account.
The air-side kinematic condition in Equation~\eqref{eq:kinematic-nonlinear} is
\begin{equation}
\partial_t\eta(\vartheta)
+u_{\mathrm a}(\vartheta)\bigl(x,\eta(\vartheta),t\bigr)
 \,\partial_x\eta(\vartheta)
=w_{\mathrm a}(\vartheta)\bigl(x,\eta(\vartheta),t\bigr).
\end{equation}
At the laminar state, the interface is flat and the vertical air velocity is zero at every height.
Differentiation at $\vartheta=0$ therefore gives
\begin{equation}
\partial_t\zeta+U(0)\partial_x\zeta=w|_{z=0}.
\end{equation}
The corresponding calculation in the water phase, whose base velocity is zero, gives
\begin{equation}
\partial_t\zeta
=\left.\mathbf v\cdot\mathbf e_z\right|_{z=0}.
\end{equation}
Hypothesis \ref{hyp:noslip} sets $U(0)=0$, so the air-side condition reduces to $\partial_t\zeta=w|_{z=0}$.
Together with the water-side condition, this shows that the vertical air velocity perturbation $w|_{z=0}$ and the vertical water velocity perturbation $\left.\mathbf v\cdot\mathbf e_z\right|_{z=0}$ both equal the time derivative of the interface displacement, which gives Equation~\eqref{eq:lin-kinematic}.
If $U(0)\neq0$, these two vertical velocity perturbations instead differ by $U(0)\partial_x\zeta$, as in the vortex-sheet setting discussed in \cite[\S2]{BSWZ2016}.

We then differentiate the dynamic condition in Equation~\eqref{eq:dynamic-nonlinear}, which imposes the pressure balance at the moving air--water interface.
Because the base pressures vary with height, their values at the interface change when the interface moves.
The pressure variation on each side is therefore the sum of the Eulerian pressure perturbation and the base pressure gradient multiplied by $\zeta$.
Differentiating Equation~\eqref{eq:dynamic-nonlinear} at $\vartheta=0$ yields
\begin{align}
\begin{aligned}
q_{\mathrm w}|_{z=0}
+p_{\mathrm w,0}'(0)\zeta
-q|_{z=0}
-p_0'(0)\zeta
&=\sigma\left.\partial_\vartheta\kappa(\vartheta)\right|_{\vartheta=0}\\
&=-\sigma\,\partial_x^2\zeta,
\end{aligned}
\end{align}
where $\kappa(\vartheta)$ is the curvature of the interface $\eta(\vartheta)$.
The last equality follows from the curvature convention in Equation~\eqref{eq:dynamic-nonlinear}.
Substituting the hydrostatic gradients
\begin{equation}
p_{\mathrm w,0}'(0)=-\rho_{\mathrm{w}} g,
\qquad
p_0'(0)=-\rho_0(0)g
\end{equation}
gives Equation~\eqref{eq:lin-dynamic}.
We call the combinations $q-\rho_0(0)g\zeta$ and $q_{\mathrm w}-\rho_{\mathrm{w}} g\zeta$ the \emph{Lagrangian pressure variations} at the interface.
Each one includes both the pressure perturbation at a fixed height and the change in base pressure caused by the interface displacement.

Finally, the top and bottom boundaries are fixed, so differentiating Equation~\eqref{eq:rigid} gives the stated impermeability conditions directly.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
\label{rem:spaces}
By instability at wavenumber $k$, we mean that the closed, densely defined operator $\mathbb{L}_k$ of \S\ref{subsec:operator} has an eigenvalue $\lambda$ with $\operatorname{Re}\lambda>0$.
This notion of instability concerns only the eigenvalues of $\mathbb{L}_k$, as in \cref{thm:operator}.
By \cref{thm:pointspec}, these eigenvalues correspond exactly to growing normal modes of the form given by Equation~\eqref{eq:normalmode}, with $\lambda=-\mathrm{i} kc$ and $\operatorname{Re}\lambda=k\operatorname{Im} c>0$.
The spatial regularity of each mode depends on the regularity of the coefficients.
Its temporal factor $e^{-\mathrm{i} kct}$ gives exponential growth at rate $k\operatorname{Im} c$ in every $L^2$-based norm in which the mode amplitude is finite.
Here, for an interval $I$, $L^2(I)$ denotes the space of square-integrable functions $\psi$ on $I$, with the norm $\|\psi\|_{L^2(I)}:=\bigl(\int_I|\psi|^2\,\mathrm{d} z\bigr)^{1/2}$.
For an integer $m\ge1$, the Sobolev space $H^m(I)$ consists of the functions in $L^2(I)$ whose weak derivatives up to order $m$ lie in $L^2(I)$, with the norm $\|\psi\|_{H^m(I)}:=\bigl(\sum_{i=0}^{m}\|\psi^{(i)}\|_{L^2(I)}^2\bigr)^{1/2}$.
The weak derivative $\psi'$ is the function, when it exists, that satisfies $\int_I\psi'\varphi\,\,\mathrm{d} z=-\int_I\psi\varphi'\,\,\mathrm{d} z$ for every smooth function $\varphi$ that vanishes near the endpoints of $I$, and higher weak derivatives are defined by repeating this step.
A mode amplitude or a state lies in $L^2$ or $H^m$ when each of its component functions does, where the air components are defined on $[0,h_{\mathrm{a}}]$ and the water components on $[-h_{\mathrm{w}},0]$.
The $L^2$-based norms are the norms of these spaces.
The analysis does not establish semigroup generation, well-posedness of the initial-value problem, or nonlinear instability.
Semigroup generation would mean that the linear evolution in which the time derivative of the state is its image under $\mathbb{L}_k$ has exactly one solution for every initial state in the domain of $\mathbb{L}_k$, and that the norm of each solution at time $t$ is at most the norm of its initial state times an exponential function of $t$ that is the same for all initial states.
Well-posedness means that solutions exist, are unique, and depend continuously on the initial state.
For well-posedness results in related settings, see \cite{Denisova2000} for the viscous two-phase problem and \cite{Lindblad2005,Trakhinin2009,CoutandShkoller2012,LuoZhang2026} for inviscid one-phase compressible problems.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop:reduction}
Assume Hypotheses \ref{hyp:reg} and \ref{hyp:noslip}, and let $k>0$ and $\operatorname{Im} c\neq0$.
Every nontrivial normal mode of the form given by Equation~\eqref{eq:normalmode} has $\hat\zeta\neq0$.
Moreover, a nontrivial normal mode with wavenumber $k$ and phase speed $c$ exists if and only if the air system in Equation~\eqref{eq:mode-air} has a solution on $[0,h_{\mathrm{a}}]$ that satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:air-bcs}
w(h_{\mathrm{a}})=0,
\qquad
w(0)=\mathrm{i} kV(0),
\end{equation}
and whose pressure satisfies the \emph{dispersion relation}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dispersion0}
\rho_{\mathrm{w}}\Bigl[kc^2\coth(kh_{\mathrm{w}})-g-\frac{\sigma k^2}{\rho_{\mathrm{w}}}\Bigr]
=q(0)-\rho_0(0)g.
\end{equation}
The second condition in Equation~\eqref{eq:air-bcs} is the normalization $\hat\zeta=1$.
In terms of the vertical displacement amplitude $\xi:=w/(\mathrm{i} kV)$ of the air, it reads $\xi(0)=1$.
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Since $U$ is real and $\operatorname{Im} c\neq0$, we have $c\neq0$ and $V(z)\neq0$ for every $z\in[0,h_{\mathrm{a}}]$.
By Hypothesis \ref{hyp:noslip}, $U(0)=0$, so $V(0)=-c$ and the kinematic conditions in Equation~\eqref{eq:lin-kinematic} become
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mode-kinematic}
w(0)=\hat v_z(0)=-\mathrm{i} kc\,\hat\zeta=\mathrm{i} kV(0)\,\hat\zeta.
\end{equation}

We first show that a mode with $\hat\zeta=0$ is trivial.
In this case, Equation~\eqref{eq:mode-kinematic} gives $w(0)=0$ and $\hat v_z(0)=0$.
Since $\hat v_z''-k^2\hat v_z=0$ and $\hat v_z(-h_{\mathrm{w}})=\hat v_z(0)=0$, we have $\hat v_z\equiv0$.
Incompressibility in Equation~\eqref{eq:water-mode-eqs} with $k>0$ then gives $\hat v_x\equiv0$, and the horizontal momentum equation gives $\mathrm{i} k\,\hat q_{\mathrm w}\equiv0$, so $\hat q_{\mathrm w}\equiv0$.
No free constant remains in the water pressure, because an amplitude that is constant in $z$ still multiplies $e^{\mathrm{i} k(x-ct)}$ and therefore has the nonzero horizontal gradient $\mathrm{i} k\,\hat q_{\mathrm w}e^{\mathrm{i} k(x-ct)}$ unless $\hat q_{\mathrm w}=0$.
The dynamic condition in Equation~\eqref{eq:lin-dynamic} with $\hat\zeta=0$ then gives $q(0)=\hat q_{\mathrm w}(0)=0$.

In the air, since $V\neq0$, Equation~\eqref{eq:m-ent} gives $s\equiv0$ and hence $r=q/a^2$.
Equation~\eqref{eq:m-xmom} then determines $u$ in terms of $w$ and $q$.
Substituting $r$ and $u$ into Equations~\eqref{eq:m-cont} and \eqref{eq:m-zmom} gives a first-order linear system for $(w,q)$.
Its coefficients are continuous on $[0,h_{\mathrm{a}}]$ by Hypothesis \ref{hyp:reg}, because the only quantities we divide by are $V$, $a^2$, and $\rho_0$, and none of them vanishes on $[0,h_{\mathrm{a}}]$.
This elimination is carried out in \cref{prop:xiq}, and it does not use any subsonicity assumption.
Since $w(0)=q(0)=0$, uniqueness for the initial-value problem gives $(w,q)\equiv0$.
Then $u$, $r$, and $s$ also vanish, and since the water fields vanish as shown above, the whole mode is trivial.

Now let $\hat\zeta\neq0$.
Since the system is linear, we may rescale the mode so that $\hat\zeta=1$.
Then Equation~\eqref{eq:mode-kinematic} gives $w(0)=\mathrm{i} kV(0)$, and impermeability at the top gives $w(h_{\mathrm{a}})=0$, so the air amplitudes satisfy Equations~\eqref{eq:mode-air} and \eqref{eq:air-bcs}.
For a mode, $-\sigma\partial_x^2\zeta=\sigma k^2\zeta$, so the dynamic condition in Equation~\eqref{eq:lin-dynamic} with $\hat\zeta=1$ reads
\begin{equation}
\bigl(\hat q_{\mathrm w}(0)-\rho_{\mathrm{w}} g\bigr)-\bigl(q(0)-\rho_0(0)g\bigr)=\sigma k^2.
\end{equation}
Inserting Equation~\eqref{eq:water-trace} and rearranging gives Equation~\eqref{eq:dispersion0}.

Conversely, suppose that the air amplitudes solve Equation~\eqref{eq:mode-air} and satisfy Equations~\eqref{eq:air-bcs} and \eqref{eq:dispersion0}.
Set $\hat\zeta=1$, define $\hat v_z$ and $\hat q_{\mathrm w}$ by Equation~\eqref{eq:water-profile} with $\hat v_z(0)=-\mathrm{i} kc$, and define $\hat v_x$ by incompressibility.
These water fields satisfy Equation~\eqref{eq:water-mode-eqs} and the bottom condition.
The kinematic conditions hold by Equation~\eqref{eq:air-bcs} and $U(0)=0$, and the dynamic condition holds by Equation~\eqref{eq:dispersion0}.
The resulting normal mode is nontrivial because $\hat\zeta=1$.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{def:air-response}
Let $\operatorname{Im} c>0$.
By \cref{lem:regular-solve} below, the system in Equation~\eqref{eq:mode-air} has a unique solution with $w(h_{\mathrm{a}})=0$ and $w'(h_{\mathrm{a}})=1$.
We denote it by $w(\cdot;k,c)$ and call it the \emph{top-normalized solution}.
Its value $w(0;k,c)$ at the interface is the \emph{interface trace}.
Here and below, a dot marks the free argument and the arguments after a semicolon are parameters, so $w(\cdot;k,c)$ is a function of the height $z$ and $w(0;k,\cdot)$ is a function of $c$.
For the limiting solution $w_\ast(\cdot;c_r)$ of \cref{prop:limit-solutions}, the wavenumber $k$ is omitted from the arguments.
As in Equation~\eqref{eq:air-bcs}, $\xi=w/(\mathrm{i} kV)$ is the vertical displacement amplitude of the air, and $\xi(0)$ is the interface displacement of the solution.
Where the interface trace does not vanish, the \emph{air-side pressure response} is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Za-def}
Z_{\mathrm{a}}(k,c):=\frac{\bar{q}(0)-\bar{\rho}_0(0)\,g\,\xi(0)}{\xi(0)},
\qquad
\bar{q}:=\frac{q}{\varepsilon\rho_{\mathrm{w}}},
\end{equation}
where $q$ is the pressure of the top-normalized solution.
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:operator}
Assume Hypotheses \ref{hyp:reg} and \ref{hyp:noslip}.
For parts \ref{it:op-frechet} and \ref{it:op-realized}, assume in addition the thermodynamic regularity in Equation~\eqref{eq:thermo-domain}, which requires an open set $\mathscr U$ whose closure $\overline{\mathscr U}$ is a compact subset of $(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}$ and which contains the base range $\{(\rho_0(z),S_\ast):z\in[0,h_{\mathrm{a}}]\}$, with $P\in C^5(\overline{\mathscr U})$ and $\inf_{\overline{\mathscr U}}P_\rho>0$.
Here $P\in C^5(\overline{\mathscr U})$ means that the partial derivatives of $P$ of order at most five are continuous on $\mathscr U$ and extend continuously to $\overline{\mathscr U}$.
Then the following statements hold.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item\label{it:op-frechet}
In the flattened formulation of \cref{subsec:flattening}, the two-phase residual, whose components are the right-hand sides of the flattened evolution equations and the expressions set to zero in the flattened constraint equations, is continuously Fr\'echet differentiable, that is, with a derivative that depends continuously on the state, on an open neighborhood of the laminar state.
After the change of variables to the good unknown in Equation~\eqref{eq:goodunknown}, its derivative at the laminar state is the linearized system of \cref{prop:linearized} (\cref{prop:residual,prop:goodunknown}).
\item\label{it:op-realized}
For each $k>0$, take the part of the linearized evolution in \ref{it:op-frechet}, written in terms of the good unknown, that is proportional to $e^{\mathrm{i} kx}$.
Use pressure rather than density as the thermodynamic state variable, recover the water pressure by solving Laplace's equation in the water, and restrict to the linearized constraint space.
The resulting evolution defines the closed, densely defined operator $\mathbb{L}_k$ of \cref{def:modal-operator}, which acts on $L^2$.
Precisely, $\mathbb{L}_k$ is the graph closure of this evolution on the $H^3$ core $\mathfrak D_k$ in the constraint space (\cref{prop:realization}).
\item\label{it:op-spectrum}
The operator $\mathbb{L}_k$ itself is defined under Hypotheses \ref{hyp:reg} and \ref{hyp:noslip} alone, without the thermodynamic regularity in Equation~\eqref{eq:thermo-domain}.
For $\operatorname{Im} c\neq0$, $\lambda=-\mathrm{i} kc$ is an eigenvalue of $\mathbb{L}_k$ if and only if the holomorphic dispersion function $\mathfrak{E}(k,c,\varepsilon)$ of \cref{def:determinant} vanishes, and the eigenspace is then one-dimensional (\cref{thm:pointspec}).
At a simple zero of $\mathfrak{E}(k,\cdot,\varepsilon)$, the eigenvalue is an isolated point of the spectrum of $\mathbb{L}_k$ and is algebraically simple.
In this case, the resolvent satisfies an explicit bound and the Riesz projection has rank one (\cref{prop:resolvent,cor:algsimple}).
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lem:entropy}
Let $\operatorname{Im} c\neq0$ and let $(u,w,r,q,s)$ solve Equation~\eqref{eq:mode-air} on $[0,h_{\mathrm{a}}]$ with $S_0'\equiv0$.
Then $s\equiv0$, and the Lagrangian pressure and density variations satisfy the barotropic relation
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lagrangian}
\Delta p:=q-\rho_0g\,\xi
=a^2\Delta\rho,
\qquad
\Delta\rho:=r+\rho_0'\xi .
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
Since $V\neq0$, the entropy equation $\mathrm{i} kVs=0$ gives $s\equiv0$.
To obtain the Lagrangian relation, use the hydrostatic relation $p_0'=-\rho_0g$ and the identity $p_0'=a^2\rho_0'+P_SS_0'$, and compute $\Delta p-a^2\Delta\rho =(q-a^2r)+(p_0'-a^2\rho_0')\xi =P_S(s+S_0'\xi)=0$, by the equation of state and $s=S_0'=0$.

The same Lagrangian relation also holds when $S_0'\neq0$.
Indeed, the linearized entropy equation is then $\mathrm{i} kVs+S_0'w=0$, which gives $s=-S_0'\xi$, so the Lagrangian entropy perturbation still vanishes.
In that case the reduction includes the Taylor--Goldstein-type $N^2$ terms discussed in \cref{rem:general-strat}.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:xiq}
Under the assumptions of \cref{lem:entropy}, with $b=g/a^2$ and the neutrality relation in Equation~\eqref{eq:neutral}, the pair $(\xi,q)$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:xiq}
\xi'=b\,\xi+\frac{a^2-V^2}{\rho_0\,a^2V^2}\,q,
\qquad
q'=-b\,q+\rho_0k^2V^2\,\xi ,
\end{equation}
and conversely $(u,w,r,s)$ are recovered from $(\xi,q)$ by $w=\mathrm{i} kV\xi$, $s=0$, $u=-\bigl(U'w+\mathrm{i} kq/\rho_0\bigr)/(\mathrm{i} kV)$, $r=q/a^2-\rho_0(\rho_0'/\rho_0+b)\xi=q/a^2$.
For a general stratified base state with arbitrary $S_0'$, the same elimination uses $s=-S_0'\xi$ and Equation~\eqref{eq:lagrangian}, as in the proof of \cref{lem:entropy}, and yields
\begin{equation}\label{eq:xiq-general}
\xi'=b\,\xi+\frac{a^2-V^2}{\rho_0a^2V^2}\,q,
\qquad
q'=-b\,q+\rho_0\bigl(k^2V^2-N^2\bigr)\,\xi .
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
We derive the stratified system first and then impose neutrality.
Solving Equation~\eqref{eq:m-xmom} for $u$ gives $\mathrm{i} ku=-(U'w+\mathrm{i} kq/\rho_0)/V$.
Substitute this expression and $w=\mathrm{i} kV\xi$, $w'=\mathrm{i} k(U'\xi+V\xi')$ into Equation~\eqref{eq:m-cont}.
The two shear terms $\rho_0U'\xi$ cancel, and division by $\mathrm{i} kV$ gives
\begin{equation}
r+\rho_0'\xi+\rho_0\xi'-\frac{q}{V^{2}}=0 .
\end{equation}
Now eliminate $r$ using Equation~\eqref{eq:lagrangian} in the form $r=q/a^2-\rho_0(\rho_0'/\rho_0+b)\xi$.
The $\rho_0'\xi$ terms cancel, leaving the first equation of the system in Equation~\eqref{eq:xiq-general}.

For the second equation, substitute $w=\mathrm{i} kV\xi$ into Equation~\eqref{eq:m-zmom} to obtain $q'=-gr+\rho_0k^2V^2\xi$.
The same expression for $r$ gives $-gr=-bq-\rho_0N^2\xi$, where $N^2=-g(\rho_0'/\rho_0+b)$ as in Equation~\eqref{eq:neutral}.
This proves Equation~\eqref{eq:xiq-general}.
Under neutrality, $N^2=0$ and $\rho_0'/\rho_0+b=0$, so the system reduces to Equation~\eqref{eq:xiq}.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{prop:SLform}
Under the assumptions of \cref{prop:xiq} the following hold.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item\label{it:chi} $\chi=\mathrm{i} k\,q$, and $(w,\chi)$ satisfies the first-order system
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:wchi}
  w'=\frac{U'+bV}{V}\,w+\frac{\chi}{AV},
  \qquad
  \chi'=-b\,\chi+\rho_0k^2V\,w .
  \end{equation}
\item\label{it:SL} $w$ satisfies the \emph{compressible Rayleigh equation} in Sturm--Liouville form,
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:SL}
  \bigl(A\,w'\bigr)'
  =\Bigl(\rho_0k^2+\frac{G}{V}\Bigr)w,
  \qquad
  G:=\bigl(A(U'+bV)\bigr)'+b\,A(U'+bV) .
  \end{equation}
  For real $c$, the coefficient $\rho_0k^2+G/V$ has at most a simple pole at each critical level, with residue proportional to $Q(z_j)$ (\cref{prop:Q}).
  Under Hypothesis \ref{hyp:subsonic}, it has no other singularity on $[0,h_{\mathrm{a}}]$.
\item\label{it:X} The mass-weighted displacement $X:=\rho_0\xi$ satisfies the divergence-form equation
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:Xform}
  \bigl(FX'\bigr)'=k^2\,\frac{V^2}{\rho_0}\,X,
  \qquad
  F:=\frac{a^2V^2}{\rho_0\,(a^2-V^2)},
  \qquad
  q=\rho_0F\,X' .
  \end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
As $a\to\infty$ with $\rho_0\to\mathrm{const}$, Equation~\eqref{eq:SL} reduces to the classical Rayleigh equation $V(w''-k^2w)=U''w$ and Equation~\eqref{eq:Xform} to $(V^2\xi')'=k^2V^2\xi$.
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
The first equation of the system in Equation~\eqref{eq:xiq} gives $q=\rho_0a^2V^2(\xi'-b\xi)/(a^2-V^2)$.
Substituting $\xi=w/(\mathrm{i} kV)$ and expanding its derivative yields $q=\chi/(\mathrm{i} k)$.
Rewriting Equation~\eqref{eq:xiq} in these variables gives Equation~\eqref{eq:wchi} and proves \ref{it:chi}.

To obtain \ref{it:SL}, differentiate $\chi=A[Vw'-(U'+bV)w]$ and use Equation~\eqref{eq:wchi}.
The expansion $(AVw')'=V(Aw')'+U'Aw'$ shows that the terms containing $w'$ cancel, leaving $V(Aw')'=\bigl(G+\rho_0k^2V\bigr)w$.
Division by $V$ gives Equation~\eqref{eq:SL}.

For \ref{it:X}, the choice $X=\rho_0\xi$ absorbs the density gradient.
By neutrality, $\rho_0'=-b\rho_0$, and therefore the first equation of the system in Equation~\eqref{eq:xiq} gives $X'=\rho_0(\xi'-b\xi)=\rho_0\,\dfrac{a^2-V^2}{\rho_0a^2V^2}\,q =\dfrac{q}{\rho_0F}$.
Thus $q=\rho_0FX'$.
Substitute this into $q'+bq=\rho_0k^2V^2\xi$ and use the second neutrality cancellation, $(\rho_0F X')'+b\rho_0FX'=\rho_0(FX')'$.
The result is $(FX')'=k^2(V^2/\rho_0)X$, as required.
The stated incompressible limits follow directly as $a\to\infty$.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{prop:Q}
Let $z_c$ be a regular critical level, $U(z_c)=c\in\mathbb{R}$, $U'(z_c)\neq0$.
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Gc}
A(z_c)=\rho_0(z_c),
\qquad
G(z_c)=\rho_0(z_c)\,Q(z_c),
\qquad
Q:=U''+\frac{g}{a^2}U'
\;=\;\rho_0\Bigl(\frac{U'}{\rho_0}\Bigr)' ,
\end{equation}
the last identity using the neutrality relation in Equation~\eqref{eq:neutral}.
Moreover the logarithmic basis solution of Equation~\eqref{eq:SL} at $z_c$ (\cref{prop:local}\ref{it:basis-log}) has the local form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:frobenius}
w(z)=w_c\Bigl[1+\frac{Q(z_c)}{U'(z_c)}\,(z-z_c)\,
\Lambda(z)\Bigr]
+O(|z-z_c|),
\qquad
\Lambda(z):=\operatorname{Log}\frac{U(z)-c-\mathrm{i}0}{V_{\mathrm{ref}}},
\end{equation}
where $w_c:=w(z_c)$, $\operatorname{Log}(\cdot-\mathrm{i}0)$ denotes the boundary value from $\operatorname{Im} c>0$ of the principal logarithm in Equation~\eqref{eq:Log-def}, given explicitly in Equation~\eqref{eq:branch}, and $V_{\mathrm{ref}}>0$ is a reference speed.
Changing $V_{\mathrm{ref}}$ changes only the $O(|z-z_c|)$ term.
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
Since $V(z_c)=0$, the definition of $A$ gives $A(z_c)=\rho_0(z_c)$.
Differentiate $\log A=\log\rho_0+\log a^2-\log(a^2-V^2)$.
At $z_c$, the terms involving $a^2$ cancel because $(V^2)'(z_c)=0$, and neutrality gives $A'(z_c)/A(z_c)=\rho_0'/\rho_0=-b$.
Consequently,
\begin{align*}
G(z_c)&=A'(U'+bV)\big|_{z_c}+A\,(U''+b'V+bU')\big|_{z_c}
        +bA(U'+bV)\big|_{z_c}\\
&=\rho_0(z_c)\bigl[-bU'+U''+bU'+bU'\bigr](z_c).
\end{align*}
This proves Equation~\eqref{eq:Gc}.
The logarithmic expansion in Equation~\eqref{eq:frobenius} is established in \cref{prop:local}\ref{it:basis-log}, where the coefficient is derived from the residue at $z_c$ of the singular integrand in the local representation of the first-order system in Equation~\eqref{eq:wchi}, computed with Equation~\eqref{eq:residue-r}.
That construction uses the reference speed $V_\ast$ of \S\ref{subsec:local} and gives a remainder $h\in C^1$ vanishing at $z_c$, hence the stated $O(|z-z_c|)$ remainder.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
\label{rem:leeslin}
The density correction in Equation~\eqref{eq:Gc} differs from that in the classical Lees--Lin coefficient.
To see the source of this difference, repeat the reduction of \cref{prop:xiq,prop:SLform} for a base state with $N^2=0$, without imposing hydrostatic neutrality.
The resulting self-adjoint equation for $X_{\mathrm{gen}}:=e^{-\int_0^zb}\,\xi$ has the leading coefficient $F_{\mathrm{gen}}:=e^{2\int_0^zb}\,\rho_0a^2V^2/(a^2-V^2)$ and the critical-layer coefficient
\begin{equation}
Q_{\mathrm{gen}}
:=U''+\Bigl(\frac{2g}{a^2}+\frac{\rho_0'}{\rho_0}\Bigr)U'
\end{equation}
(\cref{app:thermo}).
For a temperature-stratified gas with $g=0$, one has $N^2=0$ automatically, while $\rho_0'$ is arbitrary.
In this case, $Q_{\mathrm{gen}}=U''+(\rho_0'/\rho_0)U'=(\rho_0U')'/\rho_0$ is the \emph{generalized inflection point} quantity of compressible boundary-layer theory \cite{LeesLin1946,LeesReshotko1962}.
Its role in the jump of the Reynolds stress across the critical layer, that is, of the vertical flux of horizontal momentum transported by the wave, is classical \cite[\S9.3]{Mack1984}.

For a neutral hydrostatic atmosphere, instead, $\rho_0'/\rho_0=-g/a^2$ and $Q_{\mathrm{gen}}=U''+(g/a^2)U'=\rho_0(U'/\rho_0)'$, as in Equation~\eqref{eq:Gc}.
The opposite density corrections in $(\rho_0U')'$ and $\rho_0(U'/\rho_0)'$ arise because gravity contributes both to the buoyancy of the pressure perturbation and to the base-state density gradient.
In meteorological variables, the relevant quantity is the specific vorticity $U'/\rho_0$, whose critical-level gradient controls momentum deposition in compressible wave--mean-flow theory \cite{EliassenPalm1961,AndrewsMcIntyre1978,Buhler2014}.
With the opposite curl convention, the specific vorticity is $-U'/\rho_0$, but $Q$ and the instability criterion remain unchanged.
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- remark-5: body_md -->
\label{rem:general-strat}
When $N^2\neq0$, Equation~\eqref{eq:xiq-general} gives $(F_{\mathrm{gen}}X_{\mathrm{gen}}')'=e^{2\int_0^zb}\rho_0\bigl(k^2V^2-N^2\bigr)X_{\mathrm{gen}}$, with $X_{\mathrm{gen}}$ and $F_{\mathrm{gen}}$ as in \cref{rem:leeslin}.
The coefficient on the right side no longer vanishes at $z_c$, and the Frobenius exponents become $-\tfrac12\pm\sqrt{\tfrac14-\mathrm{Ri}_c}$, where $\mathrm{Ri}_c=N^2(z_c)/U'(z_c)^2$.
This is the compressible Taylor--Goldstein structure.
Its critical-layer theory, including the absorption factors $e^{-2\pi\sqrt{\mathrm{Ri}_c-1/4}}$ of \cite{BookerBretherton1967} and the compressible Miles--Howard bound $\mathrm{Ri}\ge\frac14$ of \cite{Chimonas1970}, lies outside the present paper.
<!-- end remark-5 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lem:flux}
Let $(\xi,\bar{q})$ solve Equation~\eqref{eq:xiq}.
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:flux-identity}
\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d} z}\,\operatorname{Im}\bigl(\bar{q}\,\xi^{\ast}\bigr)
=k^2\bar{\rho}_0\,\operatorname{Im}(V^2)\,|\xi|^2
-\operatorname{Im}\!\Bigl(\frac{a^2-V^2}{\bar{\rho}_0 a^2V^2}\Bigr)|\bar{q}|^2 ,
\end{equation}
the $b$-terms canceling identically.
Consequently the following hold.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item\label{it:flux-real} For $c\in\mathbb{R}$, $\mathcal{J}$ is constant on every interval free of critical levels.
\item\label{it:flux-herglotz} For $\operatorname{Im} c=\delta>0$, assume $\xi(0)=1$ and $w(h_{\mathrm{a}})=0$.
Then $X=\bar{\rho}_0\xi$ satisfies $X(0)=1$ because $\bar{\rho}_0(0)=1$, and
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:herglotz}
  \operatorname{Im}\bar{q}(0)
  =2\delta\int_0^{h_{\mathrm{a}}}
  \bigl(U-\operatorname{Re} c\bigr)
  \Bigl[\frac{a^4\,|X'|^2}{\bar{\rho}_0\,|a^2-V^2|^2}
  +\frac{k^2}{\bar{\rho}_0}\,|X|^2\Bigr]\,\mathrm{d} z .
  \end{equation}
  In particular $\operatorname{Im}Z_{\mathrm{a}}(k,c)=\operatorname{Im}\bar{q}(0)$ has the sign of a weighted average of $U-\operatorname{Re} c$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
Differentiate $\bar{q}\,\xi^{\ast}$ using Equation~\eqref{eq:xiq}.
Since $b$ is real, the terms $-b\bar{q}\,\xi^{\ast}$ and $+\bar{q}\,b\,\xi^{\ast}$ cancel, leaving $\bar{\rho}_0 k^2V^2|\xi|^2 +\bigl((a^2-V^2)/(\bar{\rho}_0 a^2V^2)\bigr)^{\!\ast}\,|\bar{q}|^2$.
Taking imaginary parts gives Equation~\eqref{eq:flux-identity}.
For real $c$ away from critical levels, both imaginary parts vanish, which proves \ref{it:flux-real}.

For \ref{it:flux-herglotz}, multiply Equation~\eqref{eq:Xform} by $X^{\ast}$ and integrate by parts over $(0,h_{\mathrm{a}})$.
The top boundary term vanishes because $w(h_{\mathrm{a}})=0$ and $V(h_{\mathrm{a}})\neq0$ imply $X(h_{\mathrm{a}})=0$.
Hence
\begin{equation}
-F(0)X'(0)X^{\ast}(0)
=\int_0^{h_{\mathrm{a}}}\Bigl[F|X'|^2+k^2\frac{V^2}{\bar{\rho}_0}|X|^2\Bigr]\,\mathrm{d} z .
\end{equation}
By \cref{prop:SLform}\ref{it:X}, $\bar{q}=\bar{\rho}_0 FX'$ and thus $\bar{q}(0)=F(0)X'(0)$.
The coefficient identities $\operatorname{Im} F=-2\delta(U-\operatorname{Re} c)\,a^4/\bigl(\bar{\rho}_0|a^2-V^2|^2\bigr)$ and $\operatorname{Im} V^2=-2\delta(U-\operatorname{Re} c)$ now give Equation~\eqref{eq:herglotz} upon taking imaginary parts.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label{def:determinant}
For $k>0$, $\varepsilon\ge0$, and $\operatorname{Im} c\neq0$ set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Edet-def}
\mathfrak{E}(k,c,\varepsilon):=\bigl[D_{\mathrm{w}}(k,c)+\varepsilon g\bigr]\,w(0;k,c)
-\varepsilon\,V(0;c)\,\bar{\chi}(0;k,c),
\end{equation}
with $(w,\bar{\chi})(\cdot;k,c)$ the top-normalized solution with the values at $z=h_{\mathrm{a}}$ given in Equation~\eqref{eq:canonical}.
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lem:determinant}
Let $k>0$ and $\varepsilon>0$.
Then the following hold.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item\label{it:det-hol} $\mathfrak{E}(k,\cdot,\varepsilon)$ is holomorphic on the open upper half-plane and on the open lower half-plane, with the reflection symmetry $\mathfrak{E}(k,c^{\ast},\varepsilon)=\mathfrak{E}(k,c,\varepsilon)^{\ast}$.
\item\label{it:det-modes} For $\operatorname{Im} c\neq0$, the linearized problem has a nontrivial normal mode of the form given by Equation~\eqref{eq:normalmode} at $(k,c)$ if and only if $\mathfrak{E}(k,c,\varepsilon)=0$.
\item\label{it:det-nonvanish} If $\mathfrak{E}(k,c,\varepsilon)=0$ with $\operatorname{Im} c\neq0$, then $w(0;k,c)\neq0$.
Wherever $w(0;k,c)\neq0$,
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:Edet-factor}
  \mathfrak{E}(k,c,\varepsilon)=w(0;k,c)\,\bigl[D_{\mathrm{w}}(k,c)-\varepsilonZ_{\mathrm{a}}(k,c)\bigr]
  =w(0;k,c)\,D(k,c,\varepsilon),
  \end{equation}
  so for $\operatorname{Im} c\neq0$ the zeros of $\mathfrak{E}(k,\cdot,\varepsilon)$ and of $D(k,\cdot,\varepsilon)$ coincide, with multiplicity.
\item\label{it:det-eps0} $\mathfrak{E}(k,c,0)=D_{\mathrm{w}}(k,c)\,w(0;k,c)$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
Holomorphy in \ref{it:det-hol} follows from \cref{lem:regular-solve}.
For the reflection symmetry, the coefficient matrix $\mathcal M(z;c)$ of Equation~\eqref{eq:wchi} and the values at $z=h_{\mathrm{a}}$ in Equation~\eqref{eq:canonical} are rational in $c$ with real $z$-dependent coefficients, so $\mathcal M(z;c^{\ast})=\mathcal M(z;c)^{\ast}$, and likewise for these values.
Hence $z\mapsto(w,\bar{\chi})(z;k,c)^{\ast}$ solves the system at $c^{\ast}$ and satisfies Equation~\eqref{eq:canonical} at $c^{\ast}$, and uniqueness gives $(w,\bar{\chi})(z;k,c^{\ast})=(w,\bar{\chi})(z;k,c)^{\ast}$.
Finally $D_{\mathrm{w}}(k,c^{\ast})=D_{\mathrm{w}}(k,c)^{\ast}$ since $D_{\mathrm{w}}$ is a real polynomial in $c$.

We next prove \ref{it:det-nonvanish}, which is needed for the mode correspondence.
Suppose $w(0;k,c)=0$.
Then $\mathfrak{E}(k,c,\varepsilon)=-\varepsilon V(0)\bar{\chi}(0;k,c)$.
Since $V(0)=-c\neq0$ and $\varepsilon>0$, the equality $\mathfrak{E}=0$ would imply $\bar{\chi}(0;k,c)=0$.
Uniqueness for the regular system with $(w,\bar{\chi})(0)=(0,0)$ would then force $(w,\bar{\chi})\equiv0$.
This contradicts Equation~\eqref{eq:canonical}, whose second component $\bar{A}(h_{\mathrm{a}})V(h_{\mathrm{a}})$ is nonzero for $\operatorname{Im} c\neq0$ by \cref{lem:regular-solve}.
Substitution of Equation~\eqref{eq:Za-formula} into Equation~\eqref{eq:Edet-def} gives the factorization in Equation~\eqref{eq:Edet-factor}.
Since $w(0;k,\cdot)$ is nonzero on the open set under consideration, the two dispersion functions have the same orders of vanishing there.
Part~\ref{it:det-eps0} follows by setting $\varepsilon=0$ in Equation~\eqref{eq:Edet-def}.

To prove \ref{it:det-modes}, let a nontrivial normal mode be given.
By \cref{prop:reduction}, it has $\hat\zeta\neq0$ and $w(0)=\mathrm{i} kV(0)\hat\zeta\neq0$, since $c\neq0$.
Its air component solves Equation~\eqref{eq:wchi} with $w(h_{\mathrm{a}})=0$.
This solution space is one-dimensional by \cref{lem:regular-solve}, so the air component is a nonzero multiple $\mu\,(w,\bar{\chi})(\cdot;k,c)$ of the top-normalized solution.
The dynamic condition in Equation~\eqref{eq:dispersion0}, written for general $\hat\zeta$ in normalized variables, is $D_{\mathrm{w}}\hat\zeta=\varepsilon\bigl(\bar{q}(0)-g\hat\zeta\bigr)$ with $\bar{\rho}_0(0)=1$.
Inserting $\hat\zeta=\mu\,w(0;k,c)/(\mathrm{i} kV(0))$ and $\bar{q}(0)=\mu\,\bar{\chi}(0;k,c)/(\mathrm{i} k)$ and multiplying by $\mathrm{i} kV(0)/\mu$ gives $\mathfrak{E}(k,c,\varepsilon)=0$.

Conversely, if $\mathfrak{E}(k,c,\varepsilon)=0$ then $w(0;k,c)\neq0$ by \ref{it:det-nonvanish}.
Set $\hat\zeta:=w(0;k,c)/(\mathrm{i} kV(0))\neq0$ and reconstruct the water fields by Equation~\eqref{eq:water-mode}.
Then the two kinematic conditions and the dynamic condition hold by construction, and \cref{prop:reduction} produces the mode.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- remark-6: body_md -->
\label{rem:determinant}
The zeros of the interface trace and of the dispersion function have different meanings.
A zero of $w(0;k,\cdot)$ is a pole of $Z_{\mathrm{a}}(k,\cdot)$, because $\bar{\chi}(0;k,c)\neq0$ there by uniqueness of the top-normalized solution and the quotient in Equation~\eqref{eq:Za-formula} diverges.
For $\operatorname{Im} c\neq0$, a zero of $\mathfrak{E}$ instead corresponds to a normal mode and cannot be a zero of the trace, by \ref{it:det-nonvanish}.
Proposition~\ref{prop:reduction} excludes the possibility of a decoupled air mode with $\hat\zeta=0$ for $k>0$ and $\operatorname{Im} c\neq0$.

At $\varepsilon=0$, the factorization \ref{it:det-eps0} explains the non-resonance assumption in Hypothesis \ref{hyp:nonres}.
If this assumption fails at $c_r=c_k$, the free wave speed $c_k$ is a zero of the first factor $D_{\mathrm{w}}(k,\cdot)$ and also of the limit $w_\ast(0;\cdot)$ of the second factor as the imaginary part of $c$ tends to zero.
The proof of \cref{thm:main} uses $D_{\mathrm{w}}(k,c)-\varepsilonZ_{\mathrm{a}}(k,c)=0$, and therefore requires division by the interface trace.
It does not apply at such a common zero.
Near such a common zero, the function $\mathfrak{E}$ is still holomorphic for $\operatorname{Im} c\neq0$, but we do not study its behavior at real phase speeds and leave the resonant case open.
Under Hypothesis \ref{hyp:nonres}, the roots of $D$ near $c_k$ are equivalently the zeros of $\mathfrak{E}$.
<!-- end remark-6 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:regular-solve}
For $\operatorname{Im} c\neq0$ the coefficients of Equation~\eqref{eq:wchi-recall} are continuous on $[0,h_{\mathrm{a}}]$ and holomorphic in $c$, because $V$ has no zero, and neither does $a^2-V^2$, since $\operatorname{Im}(V^2)=2\,\operatorname{Re} V\,\operatorname{Im} V$ can vanish only where $\operatorname{Re} V=0$, and there $a^2-V^2=a^2+(\operatorname{Im} c)^2>0$.
No subsonicity assumption is needed in this case.
The top-normalized solution $(w,\bar{\chi})(\cdot;k,c)$ of \cref{subsec:determinant}, with the values at $z=h_{\mathrm{a}}$ given in Equation~\eqref{eq:canonical}, exists and is unique.
This solution spans the solution space of Equation~\eqref{eq:wchi-recall} subject to $w(h_{\mathrm{a}})=0$ and is holomorphic in $c$ on each open half-plane.
Consequently $Z_{\mathrm{a}}(k,\cdot)$ is meromorphic on the open upper half-plane, with poles exactly at the zeros of $w(0;k,\cdot)$.
Moreover,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Za-formula}
Z_{\mathrm{a}}(k,c)=\frac{V(0)\,\bar{\chi}(0;k,c)}{w(0;k,c)}-\bar{\rho}_0(0)\,g .
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
For $\operatorname{Im} c=\delta\neq0$ we have $|V|\ge|\delta|$ on $[0,h_{\mathrm{a}}]$, and $a^2-V^2\neq0$ as shown in the statement.
Hence the coefficients of Equation~\eqref{eq:wchi-recall} are continuous in $z$ and holomorphic in $c$, and the solvability and analyticity claims follow from the standard theory of linear ODEs with parameters \cite[Ch.~1]{CoddingtonLevinson1955}.
The solution space with $w(h_{\mathrm{a}})=0$ is one-dimensional, because a solution is determined uniquely by its values $(w,\bar{\chi})(h_{\mathrm{a}})$, which can be prescribed arbitrarily in $\mathbb{C}^2$, so the solutions with $w(h_{\mathrm{a}})=0$ are the multiples of the solution with $(w,\bar{\chi})(h_{\mathrm{a}})=(0,1)$.
If $w(0;k,c)=0$ then $\bar{\chi}(0;k,c)\neq0$, since otherwise $(w,\bar{\chi})\equiv0$ by uniqueness, contradicting Equation~\eqref{eq:canonical}.
Thus every zero of $w(0;k,\cdot)$ is a pole of $Z_{\mathrm{a}}$.
Equation~\eqref{eq:Za-formula} restates Equation~\eqref{eq:Za-def} using $\bar{q}=\bar{\chi}/(\mathrm{i} k)$, $\xi(0)=w(0)/(\mathrm{i} kV(0))$.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:representation}
Let $\delta>0$ and let $(w,\bar{\chi})$ solve Equation~\eqref{eq:wchi-recall} on $I_j$.
Then there exist unique constants $(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathbb{C}^2$ such that, for all $z\in I_j$,
\begin{subequations}\label{eq:rep}
\begin{align}
\bar{\chi}(z)&=e^{-B(z)}\beta
+\int_{z_j}^{z}e^{B(s)-B(z)}\,\bar{\rho}_0 k^2V(s)\,w(s)\,\,\mathrm{d} s,
\qquad B(z):=\int_{z_j}^z b,
\label{eq:rep-chi}\\
w(z)&=\alpha\,V(z)E(z)-\frac{E(z)f(z)\,\bar{\chi}(z)}{U'(z)}
+V(z)E(z)\int_{z_j+\varsigma}^{z}
\Bigl[\frac{g_1(s)\,\bar{\chi}(s)}{V(s)}+g_2(s)\,w(s)\Bigr]\,\mathrm{d} s .
\label{eq:rep-w}
\end{align}
\end{subequations}
Conversely, for any $(\alpha,\beta)$ and any $\delta\ge0$, a continuous pair $(w,\bar{\chi})$ on $I_j$, with $w'$ taken on $I_j\setminus\{z_j\}$ when $\delta=0$, satisfying Equation~\eqref{eq:rep} solves Equation~\eqref{eq:wchi-recall} away from $V=0$.
Moreover $\beta=\bar{\chi}(z_j)$, and at $\delta=0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:wc-beta}
w(z_j)=-\frac{f(z_j)\,\beta}{U'(z_j)}
=-\frac{\beta}{\bar{\rho}_0(z_j)\,U'(z_j)} .
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
Equation~\eqref{eq:rep-chi} is the variation-of-constants solution of the $\bar{\chi}$-equation with $\beta:=\bar{\chi}(z_j)$.
Since $V$ appears in the $\bar{\chi}$-equation only in the numerator, this equation is regular at $z_j$ even when $\delta=0$, so we can integrate it from $z_j$ itself, whereas the integral in Equation~\eqref{eq:rep-w} starts at $z_j+\varsigma$.
For Equation~\eqref{eq:rep-w}, the homogeneous $w$-equation $w'=(V'/V+b)w$ has solution $VE$, so variation of constants gives $w=VE\bigl[\tilde\alpha+\int_{z_j+\varsigma}^{z}\bar{\chi} f/V^2\,\,\mathrm{d} s\bigr]$ for some constant $\tilde\alpha$.
The integrand is $\bar{\chi}/(\bar{A} V)$ divided by the homogeneous solution $VE$, and its factor $f/V^2$, which multiplies the unknown $\bar{\chi}$, is the kernel of this integral.
One integration by parts using $1/V^2=-(1/V)'/U'$ turns the double pole into a boundary term and a simple pole,
\begin{equation}
\int\frac{\bar{\chi} f}{V^2}\,\,\mathrm{d} s
=-\Bigl[\frac{\bar{\chi} f}{U'V}\Bigr]
+\int\Bigl(\frac{\bar{\chi} f}{U'}\Bigr)'\frac{\,\mathrm{d} s}{V},
\end{equation}
and eliminating $\bar{\chi}'$ with the $\bar{\chi}$-equation, $(\bar{\chi} f/U')'=(-b\bar{\chi}+\bar{\rho}_0 k^2Vw)f/U'+\bar{\chi}(f/U')' =g_1\bar{\chi}+g_2\,Vw$, whose second term cancels the pole entirely.
Absorbing the boundary term at $z_j+\varsigma$ into $\alpha$ yields Equation~\eqref{eq:rep-w}.
This determines $\alpha$ uniquely in terms of the values $(w,\bar{\chi})(z_j+\varsigma)$.
For the converse, differentiate Equation~\eqref{eq:rep-w} and use Equation~\eqref{eq:rep-chi}, and the terms reassemble into Equation~\eqref{eq:wchi-recall}.
The two coefficient identities this requires,
\begin{equation}
-\Bigl(\frac{Ef}{U'}\Bigr)'+\frac{bEf}{U'}+Eg_1
+\Bigl(\frac{U'}{V}+b\Bigr)\frac{Ef}{U'}-\frac1{\bar{A} V}=0,
\qquad
-\frac{Ef}{U'}\,\bar{\rho}_0 k^2V+VEg_2=0,
\end{equation}
hold pointwise.
The first reduces, after expanding $g_1$ and $E'=bE$, to $Ef=1/\bar{A}$, and the second restates the definition of $g_2$.
Equation~\eqref{eq:wc-beta} follows by evaluating Equation~\eqref{eq:rep-w} at $z=z_j$ where $V(z_j)=0$, $E(z_j)=1$, and using Equation~\eqref{eq:residue-r}.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lem:fixedpoint}
There exist $\varsigma_0>0$ and $C_\ast\ge1$, depending only on the quantities listed at the beginning of this section, such that for every $\varsigma\in(0,\varsigma_0]$, every $\delta\in[0,\delta_1]$ with $\delta_1\le m_U\varsigma$, and every $(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathbb{C}^2$ the system in Equation~\eqref{eq:rep} has a unique solution $(w,\bar{\chi})=(w^{\alpha,\beta,\delta},\bar{\chi}^{\alpha,\beta,\delta}) \in C^0(I_j)^2$, depending linearly on $(\alpha,\beta)$, with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fp-bounds}
\|w\|_{C^0(I_j)}+\|\bar{\chi}\|_{C^0(I_j)}\le C_\ast(|\alpha|+|\beta|),
\qquad
\mathrm{Lip}(\bar{\chi})\le C_\ast(|\alpha|+|\beta|),
\end{equation}
and, for $\delta=0$, $w\in C^1(I_j\setminus\{z_j\})$ with $|w'(z)|\le C_\ast(|\alpha|+|\beta|)\bigl(1+\bigl|\log(|z-z_j|/\varsigma)\bigr|\bigr)$.
For $\delta>0$ these are exactly the solutions of Equation~\eqref{eq:wchi-recall} in the coordinates of \cref{lem:representation}.
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
In the system in Equation~\eqref{eq:rep}, the first equation gives $\bar{\chi}$ in terms of $w$, so we substitute it into the second equation and apply the contraction argument to $w$ alone.
For given $w\in C^0(I_j)$, Equation~\eqref{eq:rep-chi} defines a function $\bar{\chi}[w]\in C^1(I_j)$ with $\bar{\chi}[w](z_j)=\beta$, and, since $|V|\le|\tau|+\delta\le C\varsigma$ on $I_j$ (using $\delta\le\delta_1\le m_U\varsigma$),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:chi-of-w}
\|\bar{\chi}[w]\|_{\infty}\le C\bigl(|\beta|+\varsigma^{2}\|w\|_\infty\bigr),
\qquad
\mathrm{Lip}(\bar{\chi}[w])\le C\bigl(|\beta|+\varsigma\|w\|_\infty\bigr),
\end{equation}
the second bound because $\bar{\chi}[w]'=-b\,\bar{\chi}[w]+\bar{\rho}_0 k^2V\,w$.
Substituting $\bar{\chi}[w]$ into Equation~\eqref{eq:rep-w} defines the affine map $\mathcal B:C^0(I_j)\to C^0(I_j)$,
\begin{equation}
\mathcal B[w]:=\alpha\,VE-\frac{Ef\,\bar{\chi}[w]}{U'}
+VE\int_{z_j+\varsigma}^{z}
\Bigl[\frac{g_1(s)\,\bar{\chi}[w](s)}{V(s)}+g_2(s)\,w(s)\Bigr]\,\mathrm{d} s ,
\end{equation}
and a pair $(w,\bar{\chi})\in C^0(I_j)^2$ satisfies Equation~\eqref{eq:rep} if and only if $w=\mathcal B[w]$ and $\bar{\chi}=\bar{\chi}[w]$.
Here affine means that $\mathcal B[w]$ is the sum of a linear map of $w$ and a function that does not depend on $w$.
The map $\mathcal B$ is well defined on all of $C^0(I_j)$, including at $\delta=0$, because the simple pole is integrated against $g_1\bar{\chi}[w]$, which is Lipschitz by Equation~\eqref{eq:chi-of-w} and $g_1\in C^1$, so the decomposition in Equation~\eqref{eq:logextract} applies with $\psi=g_1\bar{\chi}[w]$.
The map $\mathcal B$ requires only continuity of $w$ itself.
At $z=z_j$ we define the singular term of $\mathcal B[w]$ by continuity, since the prefactor $V(z)E(z)$ multiplies the logarithm and $V\operatorname{Log}V\to0$.

For the contraction, let $w,\tilde w\in C^0(I_j)$ and set $\Delta\bar{\chi}:=\bar{\chi}[w]-\bar{\chi}[\tilde w]$.
Both functions take the value $\beta$ at $z_j$, so $\Delta\bar{\chi}(z_j)=0$, and by Equation~\eqref{eq:chi-of-w}, $\|\Delta\bar{\chi}\|_\infty\le C\varsigma^{2}\|w-\tilde w\|_\infty$ and $\mathrm{Lip}(\Delta\bar{\chi})\le C\varsigma\|w-\tilde w\|_\infty$.
In
\begin{equation}
\mathcal B[w]-\mathcal B[\tilde w]
=-\frac{Ef\,\Delta\bar{\chi}}{U'}
+VE\int_{z_j+\varsigma}^{z}
\Bigl[\frac{g_1\Delta\bar{\chi}}{V}+g_2\,(w-\tilde w)\Bigr]\,\mathrm{d} s
\end{equation}
the decomposition in Equation~\eqref{eq:logextract} applied to $\psi=g_1\Delta\bar{\chi}$ has vanishing residue coefficient, $\psi(z_j)=0$, so no logarithm arises in the difference, only the remainder, bounded by $C\varsigma\,\mathrm{Lip}\bigl((\psi/U')\circ\tau^{-1}\bigr)\le C\varsigma^{2}\|w-\tilde w\|_\infty$.
Collecting the three terms,
\begin{equation}
\|\mathcal B[w]-\mathcal B[\tilde w]\|_\infty
\le C_5\,\varsigma^{2}\,\|w-\tilde w\|_\infty
\le\tfrac12\,\|w-\tilde w\|_\infty
\qquad\bigl(\varsigma\le\varsigma_0:=(2C_5)^{-1/2}\bigr),
\end{equation}
uniformly in $\delta\in[0,\delta_1]$, in $(\alpha,\beta)$, and in the parameters.
Banach's fixed-point theorem gives a unique fixed point $w$.
Setting $\bar{\chi}:=\bar{\chi}[w]$ produces the unique solution of Equation~\eqref{eq:rep}, linear in $(\alpha,\beta)$ since $\mathcal B[w]$ and $\bar{\chi}[w]$ depend linearly on $(w,\alpha,\beta)$ jointly.

For the bounds, $\mathcal B[0]$ consists of $\alpha VE$, of $-Ef\bar{\chi}[0]/U'$ with $\bar{\chi}[0]=e^{-B}\beta$, and of the singular term with $\psi=g_1e^{-B}\beta$, whose logarithmic part is multiplied by the prefactor $VE$ and is bounded, since $\sup_{z\in I_j}|V(z)|\bigl(1+\bigl|\log|V(z)|\bigr|\bigr) \le C\varsigma\log(1/\varsigma)$.
Thus $\|\mathcal B[0]\|_\infty\le C(|\alpha|+|\beta|)$.
Hence $\|w\|_\infty\le2\|\mathcal B[0]\|_\infty\le C_\ast(|\alpha|+|\beta|)$, and Equation~\eqref{eq:chi-of-w} converts this into Equation~\eqref{eq:fp-bounds}.
The derivative bound for $\delta=0$ follows by differentiating Equation~\eqref{eq:rep-w}, all terms being bounded except the product of $(VE)'$ with the integral in Equation~\eqref{eq:logextract}, which is logarithmic.
The last statement is \cref{lem:representation}.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- proposition-6: body_md -->
\label{prop:local}
Let $\delta=0$ and denote by $(w^{(1)},\bar{\chi}^{(1)})$ and $(w^{(2)},\bar{\chi}^{(2)})$ the fixed points of \cref{lem:fixedpoint} with $(\alpha,\beta)=(1,0)$ and $(0,1)$ respectively.
Then the following hold.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item\label{it:basis-reg} $w^{(1)}\in C^1(I_j)$, $\bar{\chi}^{(1)}\in C^2(I_j)$ are real-valued, with $w^{(1)}=V(z)E(z)\bigl(1+O(\varsigma)\bigr)$, $w^{(1)}(z_j)=0$, and $\bar{\chi}^{(1)}(z)=O(|z-z_j|^{3})$.
No logarithm occurs.
\item\label{it:basis-log} $w^{(2)}(z_j)=-1/(\bar{\rho}_0 U')(z_j)\neq0$, and for $z\in I_j$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:local-expansion}
w^{(2)}(z)
=w^{(2)}(z_j)\Bigl[1+\frac{Q(z_j)}{U'(z_j)}\,(z-z_j)\,
\operatorname{Log}\bigl(\tau(z)-\mathrm{i}0\bigr)\Bigr]
+h(z),
\end{equation}
where the remainder satisfies $h\in C^{1}(I_j)$ and $h(z_j)=0$.
In particular $w^{(2)}$ is continuous.
When $Q(z_j)\neq0$ its one-sided derivatives diverge logarithmically at $z_j$, so we use instead the \emph{renormalized} one-sided derivatives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:renorm-deriv}
\mathcal D_\pm:=\lim_{z\to z_j^\pm}
\Bigl[w^{(2)\prime}(z)
-\frac{Q(z_j)}{U'(z_j)}\,w^{(2)}(z_j)\,\log\bigl|\tau(z)\bigr|\Bigr],
\end{equation}
which exist and satisfy the jump formula
\begin{equation}\label{eq:wjump}
\mathcal D_+-\mathcal D_-
=\lim_{s\downarrow0}
\bigl[w^{(2)\prime}(z_j+s)-w^{(2)\prime}(z_j-s)\bigr]
=\mathrm{i}\pi\,\frac{Q(z_j)}{|U'(z_j)|}\,w^{(2)}(z_j),
\end{equation}
where the middle expression equals $\mathcal D_+-\mathcal D_-$ because the logarithmic terms at $z_j+s$ and $z_j-s$ cancel as $s\downarrow0$.
\item\label{it:basis-wronskian} The modified Wronskian $\Xi:=w^{(1)}\bar{\chi}^{(2)}-w^{(2)}\bar{\chi}^{(1)}$, the determinant of the two solution vectors of the first-order system in Equation~\eqref{eq:wchi-recall}, satisfies $\Xi(z)=\varkappa\,V(z)$ on $I_j$ for a constant $\varkappa=1+O(\varsigma^2)$, in particular $\varkappa\neq0$ for $\varsigma$ small.
Hence $\{(w^{(1)},\bar{\chi}^{(1)}),(w^{(2)},\bar{\chi}^{(2)})\}$ is a basis of the solution space on each of $I_j^\pm:=I_j\cap\{\pm(z-z_j)>0\}$, and every solution on $I_j\setminus\{z_j\}$ arising as a limit of $\delta>0$ solutions is a unique combination $\alpha(w^{(1)},\bar{\chi}^{(1)})+\beta(w^{(2)},\bar{\chi}^{(2)})$ globally on $I_j$.
\item\label{it:quadratic} For any such solution, $u_1:=|w|^2$ is continuous on $I_j$ and $\mathcal{W}=\operatorname{Im}(\bar{A} w'w^{\ast})$ extends continuously to each of $I_j^\pm$ with the jump
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Wjump}
\mathcal{W}(z_j^+)-\mathcal{W}(z_j^-)
=\pi\,\frac{\bar{\rho}_0(z_j)\,Q(z_j)}{|U'(z_j)|}\,u_1(z_j),
\end{equation}
the limit taken from $\operatorname{Im} c>0$.
For $\operatorname{Im} c<0$ the sign reverses.
Moreover $u_1(z_j)=0$ if and only if $\beta=0$, in which case the solution is a complex multiple of the real solution $(w^{(1)},\bar{\chi}^{(1)})$, hence $C^1$ across $z_j$ with $\mathcal{W}\equiv0$ on $I_j$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end proposition-6 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
For \ref{it:basis-reg}, with $\beta=0$, Equation~\eqref{eq:rep-chi} and the bound $\|w^{(1)}\|_\infty\le C_\ast$ give $|\bar{\chi}^{(1)}(z)|\le C\!\int_{z_j}^z|\tau|\le C(z-z_j)^2$.
Then the singular integrand $g_1\bar{\chi}^{(1)}/V$ is $O(|z-z_j|)$, so the residue in Equation~\eqref{eq:logextract} vanishes ($\psi(z_j)=0$) and no logarithm arises in Equation~\eqref{eq:rep-w}, whose derivative $(VE)'\!\int[\cdot]+E[g_1\bar{\chi}^{(1)}+Vg_2w^{(1)}]$ is then continuous.
Hence $w^{(1)}\in C^1(I_j)$ with $w^{(1)}=VE+O(\varsigma)\,O(|z-z_j|)$.
Substituting this into Equation~\eqref{eq:rep-chi} improves the bound to $\bar{\chi}^{(1)}=O(|z-z_j|^3)$, and $\bar{\chi}^{(1)\prime}=-b\bar{\chi}^{(1)}+\bar{\rho}_0 k^2Vw^{(1)}\in C^1$ gives $\bar{\chi}^{(1)}\in C^2$.
For $\delta=0$, all kernels are real, as are the constants $(\alpha,\beta)=(1,0)$, so the solution is real.

For \ref{it:basis-log}, $w^{(2)}(z_j)$ is given by Equation~\eqref{eq:wc-beta} with $\beta=1$.
Apply the decomposition in Equation~\eqref{eq:logextract} to $\psi=g_1\bar{\chi}^{(2)}$.
By Equation~\eqref{eq:residue-r} the residue coefficient is
\begin{equation}
\frac{(g_1\bar{\chi}^{(2)})(z_j)}{U'(z_j)}
=-\frac{f(z_j)Q(z_j)}{U'(z_j)^3}\,\beta
=\frac{Q(z_j)}{U'(z_j)^2}\;w^{(2)}(z_j),
\end{equation}
so Equation~\eqref{eq:rep-w} becomes
\begin{equation}
w^{(2)}
=VE\,[\,\text{const}\,]-\frac{Ef\bar{\chi}^{(2)}}{U'}
+VE\,\Bigl[\frac{Q(z_j)}{U'(z_j)^{2}}\,w^{(2)}(z_j)\,
\operatorname{Log}(\tau-\mathrm{i}0)+\mathcal R[\psi]\Bigr]
+VE\!\int_{z_j+\varsigma}^{z}\!g_2\,w^{(2)} .
\end{equation}
Every term except the explicit logarithm is $C^1(I_j)$.
The first is smooth.
The second is $C^1$ because $\bar{\chi}^{(2)}\in C^1(I_j)$, its derivative $-b\bar{\chi}^{(2)}+\bar{\rho}_0 k^2Vw^{(2)}$ being continuous.
The last is $C^1$ because its integrand is continuous, and $VE\,\mathcal R[\psi]$ is $C^1$ because $\psi=g_1\bar{\chi}^{(2)}\in C^1(I_j)$, so that Equation~\eqref{eq:remainder-C1} gives $\mathcal R[\psi]\in C^1(I_j)$.
Replacing the prefactor $VE$ of the logarithm by its linearization $U'(z_j)(z-z_j)$ changes the expression by $[VE-U'(z_j)(z-z_j)]\operatorname{Log}(\tau-\mathrm{i}0) =O\bigl((z-z_j)^2\log|z-z_j|\bigr)$, which is again $C^1$ with derivative vanishing at $z_j$.
The constant term $w^{(2)}(z_j)$ in the bracket of Equation~\eqref{eq:local-expansion} is the value of $-Ef\bar{\chi}^{(2)}/U'$ at $z_j$, by Equation~\eqref{eq:wc-beta}.
Collecting all $C^1$ terms into $h$ gives Equation~\eqref{eq:local-expansion} with $h\in C^1(I_j)$, $h(z_j)=0$.

Differentiating Equation~\eqref{eq:local-expansion},
\begin{equation}
w^{(2)\prime}(z)
=\frac{Q(z_j)}{U'(z_j)}\,w^{(2)}(z_j)
\Bigl[\operatorname{Log}\bigl(\tau(z)-\mathrm{i}0\bigr)
+\frac{(z-z_j)\,U'(z)}{\tau(z)}\Bigr]+h'(z),
\end{equation}
where $(z-z_j)U'(z)/\tau(z)\to1$ from both sides, while by Equation~\eqref{eq:branch} the difference $\operatorname{Log}(\tau-\mathrm{i}0)-\log|\tau|=-\mathrm{i}\pi\mathbf 1_{\{\tau<0\}}$ is constant on each side of $z_j$.
Hence the renormalized limits in Equation~\eqref{eq:renorm-deriv} exist, each equal to $(Q/U')(z_j)\,w^{(2)}(z_j)\,(1-\mathrm{i}\pi\mathbf 1_{\{\tau<0\}})+h'(z_j)$ with the indicator evaluated on the corresponding side.
Crossing $z_j$ upward, $\mathbf 1_{\{\tau<0\}}$ drops from $1$ to $0$ if $U'(z_j)>0$ and rises from $0$ to $1$ if $U'(z_j)<0$.
In both cases $\mathcal D_+-\mathcal D_- =\pm\mathrm{i}\pi\,\tfrac{Q}{U'}(z_j)\,w^{(2)}(z_j) =\mathrm{i}\pi\,\tfrac{Q(z_j)}{|U'(z_j)|}\,w^{(2)}(z_j)$.
The formula for the difference evaluated at $z_j+s$ and $z_j-s$ follows since $\log|\tau(z_j+s)|-\log|\tau(z_j-s)|\to0$ as $s\downarrow0$ and $h'$ is continuous.
This is Equation~\eqref{eq:wjump}.

For \ref{it:basis-wronskian}, differentiating $\Xi$ and using Equation~\eqref{eq:wchi-recall} gives $\Xi'=(V'/V)\,\Xi$ on $I_j\setminus\{z_j\}$, because the $b$- and $k^2$-terms cancel between the two solutions.
Hence $\Xi/V$ is constant on each side of $z_j$.
Equivalently, from Equation~\eqref{eq:A-def}, $\Xi=\bar{A} V\,(w^{(1)}w^{(2)\prime}-w^{(2)}w^{(1)\prime})$, so $\Xi/V$ is the $\bar{A}$-weighted Wronskian of Equation~\eqref{eq:bvp}, which is constant on each side.
By \ref{it:basis-reg}, $w^{(1)}\in C^1(I_j)$ vanishes at $z_j$ and $\bar{\chi}^{(1)}(z)=O(|z-z_j|^{3})$, while $w^{(2)}$ and $\bar{\chi}^{(2)}$ are continuous, so $\Xi/V$ has the same limit from both sides of $z_j$, and $\Xi=\varkappa\,V$ on $I_j$ with a single constant $\varkappa$.
To compute $\varkappa$, we evaluate $\Xi$ at the endpoint $z_j+\varsigma$, where the integral in Equation~\eqref{eq:rep-w} is empty.
There $w^{(1)}=VE-Ef\bar{\chi}^{(1)}/U'$ and $w^{(2)}=-Ef\bar{\chi}^{(2)}/U'$, so the terms with $Ef/U'$ cancel and $\Xi=VE\bar{\chi}^{(2)}$.
Equation~\eqref{eq:rep-chi} with $\beta=1$ and $E=e^{B}$ then gives $\varkappa=E(z_j+\varsigma)\,\bar{\chi}^{(2)}(z_j+\varsigma)=1+\int_{z_j}^{z_j+\varsigma}E\,\bar{\rho}_0 k^2V\,w^{(2)}\,\,\mathrm{d} s$.
The integral is $O(\varsigma^2)$, because $|V|\le\varsigma\max_{I_j}|U'|$ on $I_j$ and $w^{(2)}$ is bounded, so $\varkappa=1+O(\varsigma^2)$.
The combination is unique on all of $I_j$ because $(\alpha,\beta)$ are determined linearly and invertibly by the values at $z_j+\varsigma$, and the representation in Equation~\eqref{eq:rep} extends the solution across $z_j$.

For \ref{it:quadratic}, $u_1=|w|^2$ is continuous since $w$ is.
For $\mathcal{W}$, write the solution as $\alpha(w^{(1)},\bar{\chi}^{(1)})+\beta(w^{(2)},\bar{\chi}^{(2)})$.
By \ref{it:basis-reg} and \ref{it:basis-log} it has the form of Equation~\eqref{eq:local-expansion} with $w^{(2)}(z_j)$ replaced by $w(z_j)=\beta w^{(2)}(z_j)$ and a $C^1$ remainder, so its renormalized derivatives $\mathcal D_\pm$, defined as in Equation~\eqref{eq:renorm-deriv} with $w^{(2)}$ replaced by $w$, exist and satisfy Equation~\eqref{eq:wjump} with $w(z_j)$ in place of $w^{(2)}(z_j)$.
Set $\varpi:=(Q/U')(z_j)\,w(z_j)$ and split
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{W}(z)=\operatorname{Im}\bigl(\bar{A} w'w^{\ast}\bigr)
=\bar{A}(z)\,\log|\tau(z)|\;\operatorname{Im}\bigl(\varpi\,w^{\ast}(z)\bigr)
+\operatorname{Im}\Bigl(\bar{A}(z)\bigl[w'(z)-\varpi\log|\tau(z)|\bigr]w^{\ast}(z)\Bigr).
\end{equation}
As $z\to z_j^\pm$ the second term converges to $\operatorname{Im}\bigl(\bar{A}(z_j)\,\mathcal D_\pm\,w^{\ast}(z_j)\bigr)$.
The first term vanishes in the limit although $\log|\tau|$ diverges, because the coefficient $(Q/U')(z_j)$ is real and $w(z_j)\,w^{\ast}(z_j)=|w(z_j)|^2$, so $\operatorname{Im}(\varpi\,w^{\ast}(z_j))=0$, while $w^{\ast}(z)-w^{\ast}(z_j)=O(|z-z_j|\log|z-z_j|)$ by Equation~\eqref{eq:local-expansion}, making the first term $O(|z-z_j|\log^2|z-z_j|)\to0$.
In words, the logarithmically divergent part of $\bar{A} w'w^{\ast}$ is real and drops out of the imaginary part.
Hence $\mathcal{W}$ extends continuously to each of $I_j^\pm$ and
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{W}(z_j^+)-\mathcal{W}(z_j^-)
=\operatorname{Im}\bigl(\bar{A}(z_j)\,(\mathcal D_+-\mathcal D_-)\,w^{\ast}(z_j)\bigr)
=\bar{A}(z_j)\,\pi\,\frac{Q(z_j)}{|U'(z_j)|}\,|w(z_j)|^2 ,
\end{equation}
which is Equation~\eqref{eq:Wjump} since $\bar{A}(z_j)=\bar{\rho}_0(z_j)$ and $u_1(z_j)=|w(z_j)|^2$.
Finally $u_1(z_j)=0\iff w(z_j)=0 \iff\beta=0$ by Equation~\eqref{eq:wc-beta}, and then the solution is $\alpha\,(w^{(1)},\bar{\chi}^{(1)})$ with $w^{(1)}$ real, so $\mathcal{W}=|\alpha|^2\operatorname{Im}(\bar{A} w^{(1)\prime}w^{(1)})=0$ on $I_j$.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- remark-7: body_md -->
\label{rem:mack-compare}
Equation~\eqref{eq:local-expansion} shows that the compressible critical layer has the same type of singularity as Rayleigh's equation, with $U''$ replaced by $Q$.
For the zero-gravity, temperature-stratified gas the same computation, with the unified coefficient of \cref{rem:leeslin}, reproduces the classical local expansions of compressible boundary-layer theory \cite[Eqs.~(9.13)--(9.14)]{Mack1984}, including the $-\mathrm{i}\pi$ branch rule.
The derivation makes the physical origin of the two pieces of $Q=U''+bU'$ transparent.
The curvature $U''$ enters through the curvature of the profile in the variation-of-constants kernel, and $bU'$ through the hydrostatic decay $\bar{\chi}'=-b\bar{\chi}+O(V)$ of the scaled pressure variable across the layer.
<!-- end remark-7 -->
<!-- proposition-7: body_md -->
\label{prop:convergence}
Let $(w_\delta,\bar{\chi}_\delta)$ denote the fixed point of \cref{lem:fixedpoint} at the parameter $\delta\in[0,\delta_1]$ with constants $(\alpha,\beta)$.
Here and below, a subscript $\delta$ marks the value of a quantity at the parameter $\delta$, for example $V_\delta=\tau-\mathrm{i}\delta$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item\label{it:conv-fixed}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:delta-rate}
\|w_\delta-w_0\|_{C^0(I_j)}+\|\bar{\chi}_\delta-\bar{\chi}_0\|_{C^0(I_j)}
\;\le\;C_\ast\,(|\alpha|+|\beta|)\;
\delta\bigl(1+\bigl|\log(\delta/V_\ast)\bigr|\bigr),
\qquad \delta\in(0,\delta_1],
\end{equation}
uniformly on $I_j$, including at $z_j$.
\item\label{it:conv-matrices} Let $\mathcal E^{\pm}_{\delta}:(\alpha,\beta)\mapsto (w_\delta,\bar{\chi}_\delta)(z_j\pm\varsigma)$ be the endpoint evaluation matrices.
Here $\|\cdot\|$ denotes the operator norm of a linear map from $\mathbb{C}^2$ to itself, where $\mathbb{C}^2$ carries the norm $|\alpha|+|\beta|$ chosen at the beginning of this subsection, and the operator norm of a linear map $\mathcal G$ is the maximum of the norm of $\mathcal G(\alpha,\beta)$ over the $(\alpha,\beta)$ with $|\alpha|+|\beta|=1$.
Then $\|\mathcal E^{\pm}_{\delta}\|\le C$ and $\|\mathcal E^{\pm}_{\delta}-\mathcal E^{\pm}_{0}\|= O\bigl(\delta(1+|\log(\delta/V_\ast)|)\bigr)$ by \ref{it:conv-fixed}.
For every $\delta\in[0,\delta_1]$,
\begin{equation}
\det\mathcal E^{\pm}_{\delta}
=\varkappa_\delta\,V_\delta(z_j\pm\varsigma)
\end{equation}
with a constant $\varkappa_\delta$ independent of the endpoint, $|\varkappa_\delta-1|\le C\varsigma^2$ for every $\delta\in[0,\delta_1]$, and $|\varkappa_\delta-\varkappa_0|\le C\,\delta\bigl(1+|\log(\delta/V_\ast)|\bigr)/(m_U\varsigma)$.
Hence, for $\varsigma$ small, $|\varkappa_\delta|\ge\tfrac12$ for every $\delta\in[0,\delta_1]$, and the inverses exist with $\|(\mathcal E^{\pm}_{\delta})^{-1}\|\le C$ uniformly in $\delta\in[0,\delta_1]$.
\item\label{it:conv-transfer} The maps $T_\delta:=\mathcal E^{-}_{\delta}\circ(\mathcal E^{+}_{\delta})^{-1}$, which for $\delta>0$ coincide with the solution maps $(w,\bar{\chi})(z_j+\varsigma)\mapsto(w,\bar{\chi})(z_j-\varsigma)$ of the regular system in Equation~\eqref{eq:wchi-recall}, are defined for every $\delta\in[0,\delta_1]$, are bounded with bounded inverses uniformly, and converge in norm to $T_0$ at rate $O\bigl(\delta(1+|\log(\delta/V_\ast)|)\bigr)$.
Moreover $T_0$, together with the critical values $w_0(z_j)$, depends $C^1$ on $c_r$ on compact sets of regular values, with uniformly bounded derivatives.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end proposition-7 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
Since the contraction constant of $\mathcal B$ in \cref{lem:fixedpoint} is $\tfrac12$ uniformly in $\delta$, it suffices to bound $\|(\mathcal B_\delta-\mathcal B_0)[w_0]\|_\infty$, where $\mathcal B_\delta$ denotes the map of \cref{lem:fixedpoint} at the value $\delta$.
Then $\|w_\delta-w_0\|_\infty\le2\|(\mathcal B_\delta-\mathcal B_0)[w_0]\|_\infty$, and the $\bar{\chi}$-component follows from $\|\bar{\chi}_\delta[w_\delta]-\bar{\chi}_0[w_0]\|_\infty \le C\varsigma^{2}\|w_\delta-w_0\|_\infty+C\delta$, the kernel of Equation~\eqref{eq:rep-chi} being Lipschitz in $\delta$.

The map $\mathcal B_\delta$ depends on $\delta$ in two ways.
It contains $V$ explicitly, in the prefactor $VE$, in the kernel of Equation~\eqref{eq:rep-chi}, and in the simple pole $1/V$.
It also contains the coefficients $f_\delta=1/(\bar{A}_\delta E)$, $g_{1,\delta}$, and $g_{2,\delta}$, which depend on $\delta$ through $V^2$ in $\bar{A}_\delta$.
Since $V_\delta^2-V_0^2=-2\mathrm{i}\delta\tau-\delta^2$, these coefficients are Lipschitz in $\delta$ in the norms we need, namely
\begin{equation}\label{eq:coef-delta}
\|f_\delta-f_0\|_{C^2(I_j)}
+\|g_{1,\delta}-g_{1,0}\|_{C^{0,1}(I_j)}
+\|g_{2,\delta}-g_{2,0}\|_{C^0(I_j)}\le C\delta .
\end{equation}
To see this, note that $f_\delta-f_0=(2\mathrm{i}\delta\,\tau+\delta^{2})/(\bar{\rho}_0 a^2E)$ exactly.
The numerator has $C^2(I_j)$ norm $O(\delta)$, because its derivatives up to order two involve only $\tau$, $U'$, and $U''$, and the denominator does not depend on $\delta$ and is bounded away from zero.
Hence $\|f_\delta-f_0\|_{C^2}\le C\delta$.
The definitions in Equation~\eqref{eq:g-def}, linear in $f$, then give the $g_1$-estimate, whose Lipschitz seminorm involves $f''$, and the $g_2$-estimate.
Likewise $\|\bar{\chi}_\delta[w_0]-\bar{\chi}_0[w_0]\|_\infty +\mathrm{Lip}\bigl(\bar{\chi}_\delta[w_0]-\bar{\chi}_0[w_0]\bigr) \le C\delta\,\|w_0\|_\infty$, with $(\bar{\chi}_\delta[w_0]-\bar{\chi}_0[w_0])(z_j)=0$, the kernel of Equation~\eqref{eq:rep-chi} being Lipschitz in $\delta$.
The regular parts of $(\mathcal B_\delta-\mathcal B_0)[w_0]$ are the prefactor difference $\alpha(V_\delta-V_0)E=-\mathrm{i}\delta\alpha E$, the terms $-E(f_\delta\bar{\chi}_\delta[w_0]-f_0\bar{\chi}_0[w_0])/U'$, and the $g_2$-integrals.
Together they contribute $O(\delta)$ in $C^0(I_j)$.

The singular part must be compared through the logarithmic decomposition, not by bounding the kernel difference in absolute value.
When $z$ lies on the far side of $z_j$, the integration interval crosses the pole, where $|1/V_\delta-1/V_0|=\delta/(|\tau||\tau-\mathrm{i}\delta|)$ is not integrable, and the $\delta=0$ integral exists only as the boundary value in Equation~\eqref{eq:branch}.
Write $\psi_\delta:=g_{1,\delta}\bar{\chi}_\delta[w_0]$, so that $\|\psi_\delta-\psi_0\|_{C^{0,1}}\le C\delta$ by Equation~\eqref{eq:coef-delta} while $\|\psi_0\|_{C^{0,1}}\le C(|\alpha|+|\beta|)$, and apply the decomposition in Equation~\eqref{eq:logextract} at the parameter $\delta$ and at $0$ separately.
The difference of the two singular terms splits into the following four pieces.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)},leftmargin=2.2em]
\item The logarithmic part with the common residue $\psi_0(z_j)/U'(z_j)$, namely $E\,\tfrac{\psi_0(z_j)}{U'(z_j)} \bigl[V_\delta\operatorname{Log}(\tau-\mathrm{i}\delta) -V_0\operatorname{Log}(\tau-\mathrm{i}0)\bigr]$, is bounded by $C\delta(1+\log(1/\delta))$ through the model estimate in Equation~\eqref{eq:log-model} of \cref{app:proofs}.
\item The part from the change of residue, $E\,\tfrac{(\psi_\delta-\psi_0)(z_j)}{U'(z_j)}\, V_\delta\operatorname{Log}(\tau-\mathrm{i}\delta)$, is bounded by $C\delta\cdot\varsigma\log(1/\varsigma)$, since $\sup_{I_j}|V_\delta|\bigl(1+|\log|V_\delta||\bigr)\le C\varsigma\log(1/\varsigma)$.
\item The endpoint terms at $z_j+\varsigma$ differ by $O(\delta)$.
With $\tau_+:=\tau(z_j+\varsigma)$, which satisfies $|\tau_+|\ge m_U\varsigma$, we have $|\operatorname{Log}(\tau_+-\mathrm{i}\delta)-\operatorname{Log}(\tau_+-\mathrm{i}0)| \le\delta/|\tau_+|$ and $|V_\delta-V_0|=\delta$.
\item The remainders are $(V_\delta-V_0)E\,\mathcal R_\delta[\psi_\delta] +V_0E\bigl(\mathcal R_\delta[\psi_\delta]-\mathcal R_\delta[\psi_0]\bigr) +V_0E\bigl(\mathcal R_\delta[\psi_0]-\mathcal R_0[\psi_0]\bigr)$.
The first is $O(\delta\varsigma)$ by the remainder bound of Equation~\eqref{eq:logextract}.
The second is $O(\varsigma\delta)$ by the same bound applied to $\psi_\delta-\psi_0$.
The third is $O(\delta\log(1/\delta))$ by the comparison estimate in Equation~\eqref{eq:remainder-compare} of \cref{app:proofs}, in which the Lipschitz factor $|\mathfrak g(t)-\mathfrak g(0)|\le \mathrm{Lip}(\mathfrak g)|t|$, with $\mathfrak g=(\psi/U')\circ\tau^{-1}$ the function $\psi/U'$ written in the variable $\tau$, cancels the nonintegrable part of the kernel difference.
\end{enumerate}
Collecting, $\|(\mathcal B_\delta-\mathcal B_0)[w_0]\|_\infty\le C(|\alpha|+|\beta|)\,\delta\log(1/\delta)$, and Equation~\eqref{eq:delta-rate} follows.
This proves \ref{it:conv-fixed}.

For \ref{it:conv-matrices}, the columns of $\mathcal E^{\pm}_{\delta}$ are the values at $z_j\pm\varsigma$ of the basis fixed points with $(\alpha,\beta)=(1,0)$ and $(0,1)$, so the bound and convergence follow from \cref{lem:fixedpoint} and \ref{it:conv-fixed}.
For the determinant, the identity $\Xi'=(V'/V)\,\Xi$ from \cref{prop:local}\ref{it:basis-wronskian} uses only the system in Equation~\eqref{eq:wchi-recall} and holds for every $\delta\in[0,\delta_1]$.
For $\delta>0$ it holds on all of $I_j$, because $V_\delta$ has no zero there.
For $\delta=0$ it holds on $I_j\setminus\{z_j\}$, where $\Xi_0/V_0$ is constant on each side of $z_j$, and the two one-sided constants coincide because both equal $\lim_{z\to z_j}\Xi_0/V_0=\varkappa_0$ by \cref{prop:local}\ref{it:basis-wronskian}.
Hence $\Xi_\delta=\varkappa_\delta V_\delta$ on $I_j$ with $\varkappa_\delta$ constant, and $\det\mathcal E^{\pm}_{\delta}=\Xi_\delta(z_j\pm\varsigma)$ because the columns of $\mathcal E^{\pm}_{\delta}$ are the values of the two basis solutions at $z_j\pm\varsigma$.
The endpoint computation in the proof of \cref{prop:local}\ref{it:basis-wronskian} uses only the representation in Equation~\eqref{eq:rep}, which the fixed points satisfy for every $\delta\in[0,\delta_1]$.
For the fixed point $(w^{(2)}_\delta,\bar{\chi}^{(2)}_\delta)$ with $(\alpha,\beta)=(0,1)$, it gives $\varkappa_\delta=1+\int_{z_j}^{z_j+\varsigma}E\,\bar{\rho}_0 k^2V_\delta\,w^{(2)}_\delta\,\,\mathrm{d} s$.
Since $|V_\delta|\le|\tau|+\delta\le(\max_{I_j}|U'|+m_U)\,\varsigma$ on $I_j$ by $\delta_1\le m_U\varsigma$, and $|w^{(2)}_\delta|\le C_\ast$ by \cref{lem:fixedpoint}, this gives $|\varkappa_\delta-1|\le C\varsigma^2$.
Writing $\varkappa_\delta=\Xi_\delta(z_j+\varsigma)/V_\delta(z_j+\varsigma)$ and using $|V_\delta(z_j+\varsigma)|\ge|\tau(z_j+\varsigma)|\ge m_U\varsigma$, $|V_\delta-V_0|=\delta$, and the endpoint convergence of the basis solutions from \ref{it:conv-fixed} gives $|\varkappa_\delta-\varkappa_0|\le C\delta(1+|\log(\delta/V_\ast)|)/(m_U\varsigma)$.
For $\varsigma$ small, $C\varsigma^2\le\tfrac12$, so $|\varkappa_\delta|\ge\tfrac12$ for all $\delta\in[0,\delta_1]$, $|\det\mathcal E^{\pm}_{\delta}|\ge\tfrac12 m_U\varsigma$, and the inverse bound follows from the formula for the inverse of a $2\times2$ matrix, which swaps the diagonal entries, changes the sign of the other two entries, and divides by the determinant.

For \ref{it:conv-transfer}, recall that for $\delta>0$ the fixed points are the solutions of Equation~\eqref{eq:wchi-recall} (\cref{lem:representation}), so $T_\delta$ carries the values at $z_j+\varsigma$ to the values at $z_j-\varsigma$.
For every $\delta\in[0,\delta_1]$ it is defined and bounded with bounded inverse by \ref{it:conv-matrices}.
Convergence follows from
\begin{gather*}
T_\delta-T_0
=(\mathcal E^{-}_{\delta}-\mathcal E^{-}_{0})(\mathcal E^{+}_{\delta})^{-1}
+\mathcal E^{-}_{0}
\bigl[(\mathcal E^{+}_{\delta})^{-1}-(\mathcal E^{+}_{0})^{-1}\bigr],\\
(\mathcal E^{+}_{\delta})^{-1}-(\mathcal E^{+}_{0})^{-1}
=(\mathcal E^{+}_{\delta})^{-1}
(\mathcal E^{+}_{0}-\mathcal E^{+}_{\delta})
(\mathcal E^{+}_{0})^{-1}.
\end{gather*}
The $C^1$-dependence of $T_0$ and $w_0(z_j)$ on $c_r$ needs a change of variables that follows the critical level.
The critical level $z_j(c_r)$ and the interval $I_j(c_r)$ move as $c_r$ changes, and differentiating $\tau\operatorname{Log}(\tau-\mathrm{i}0)$ with respect to $c_r$ at fixed $z$ gives a term that is singular at $z_j$.
We therefore write $z=z_j(c_r)+x$ and differentiate at fixed $x$.
In this variable, $\tilde\tau(x)=U(z_j+x)-c_r$ satisfies $\partial_{c_r}\tilde\tau=U'(z_j+x)/U'(z_j)-1=O(x)$, which cancels the singularity.
The details are in \cref{lem:pullback} of \cref{app:proofs}.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- proposition-8: body_md -->
\label{prop:limit-solutions}
Let $c_r$ be a regular value as above and define the \emph{limit problem} on $[0,h_{\mathrm{a}}]$ as the system in Equation~\eqref{eq:wchi-recall} with $\delta=0$ on $[0,h_{\mathrm{a}}]\setminus\{z_1,\dots,z_m\}$, continued across each $z_j$ by the representation in Equation~\eqref{eq:rep}, equivalently by the basis of \cref{prop:local}.
