The endpoint value $\operatorname{Log}(\tilde\tau(\varsigma)-\mathrm{i}\delta)$ is $C^1$ in $c_r$ because $|\tilde\tau(\varsigma)|\ge m_U\varsigma$.
Since the factors $\tilde E$ and $\psi(0)/\tilde U'(0)$ are $C^1$ in $c_r$, the logarithmic term of $\mathcal B_{c_r,\delta}[w]$ is $C^1$ in $c_r$ as a map into $C^0(I)$, with uniformly bounded, uniformly continuous derivative.

For the remainder integral, we differentiate under the integral sign and use
\begin{equation}
\partial_{c_r}\Bigl[\frac{\mathfrak h}{\tilde V}\Bigr]
=\frac{\partial_{c_r}\mathfrak h}{\tilde V}
-\frac{\mathfrak h\,\theta}{\tilde V^{2}} .
\end{equation}
For the second piece, $|\mathfrak h|\le C|x'|$, $|\theta|\le C|x'|$, and $|\tilde V|\ge|\tilde\tau|\ge m_U|x'|$ give the bound $C/m_U^2$.
For the first piece,
\begin{equation}
\partial_{c_r}\mathfrak h
=\partial_{c_r}\psi
-\partial_{c_r}\psi(0)\,\frac{\tilde U'}{\tilde U'(0)}
-\psi(0)\,\partial_{c_r}\Bigl[\frac{\tilde U'(x')}{\tilde
U'(0)}\Bigr],
\end{equation}
which vanishes at $x'=0$, because the first two terms cancel there, and $\partial_{c_r}[\tilde U'(x')/\tilde U'(0)] =\bigl[U''(z_j+x')\,U'(z_j)-U'(z_j+x')\,U''(z_j)\bigr] z_j'/U'(z_j)^{2}$ vanishes at $x'=0$ as well.
Since $\partial_{c_r}\mathfrak h$ is Lipschitz in $x'$ uniformly, $|\partial_{c_r}\mathfrak h(x')|\le C|x'|$, and the first piece is bounded by $C/m_U$.
The mean value theorem therefore bounds the $c_r$ difference quotients by an integrable function that does not depend on the increment of $c_r$, uniformly in $\delta$.
The dominated convergence theorem yields
\begin{equation}
\partial_{c_r}\int_\varsigma^x\frac{\mathfrak h}{\tilde V}\,\,\mathrm{d} x'
=\int_\varsigma^x\partial_{c_r}
\Bigl[\frac{\mathfrak h}{\tilde V}\Bigr]\,\mathrm{d} x',
\end{equation}
which is continuous in $(x,c_r)$ and bounded by $C\varsigma$, uniformly in $\delta\in[0,\delta_1]$.
No endpoint or base-point terms arise because both are stationary in the $x$-coordinate.

The remaining terms of $\mathcal B_{c_r,\delta}[w]$, namely $\alpha\tilde V\tilde E$, $-\tilde E\tilde f\tilde\chi[w]/\tilde U'$, and the $\tilde g_2$-integral, are $C^1$ in $c_r$ as maps into $C^0(I)$ by the preceding coefficient estimates.
The logarithmic and remainder estimates show that, for each $w$, $c_r\mapsto\mathcal B_{c_r,\delta}[w]\in C^0(I)$ is $C^1$ and
\begin{equation}
\|\partial_{c_r}\mathcal B_{c_r,\delta}[w]\|_{C^0(I)}
\le C\bigl(|\alpha|+|\beta|+\|w\|_\infty\bigr),
\end{equation}
and $\partial_{c_r}\mathcal B_{c_r,\delta}[w]$ is continuous in $c_r$ uniformly in $\delta$.
Since $\mathcal B_{c_r,\delta}$ is affine in $w$, we write $\mathcal B_{c_r,\delta}[w]=\mathcal A_{c_r,\delta}w+\mathcal B_{c_r,\delta}[0]$, and the linear part $\mathcal A_{c_r,\delta}w$ is $\mathcal B_{c_r,\delta}[w]$ with $\alpha=\beta=0$.
In this case $\psi(0)=0$, so the logarithmic term is absent, and each term of the derivative computed above is an integral operator applied to $w$, whose integrand is bounded by the integrable functions above and depends continuously on $c_r$.
The dominated convergence theorem then shows that the same statements hold for $\mathcal A_{c_r,\delta}$ in the operator norm on $C^0(I)$.
Here the operator norm $\|\mathcal G\|_{C^0\to C^0}$ of a linear map $\mathcal G$ from $C^0(I)$ to itself is the supremum of $\|\mathcal Gw\|_{C^0(I)}$ over the $w$ with $\|w\|_{C^0(I)}\le1$.
The uniform continuity of the derivative on the compact set $K$ strengthens differentiability to the uniform Taylor estimate
\begin{equation}
\bigl\|\mathcal A_{c_r+h,\delta}-\mathcal A_{c_r,\delta}
-h\,\partial_{c_r}\mathcal A_{c_r,\delta}\bigr\|_{C^0\to C^0}=o(h),
\end{equation}
uniformly in $\delta\in[0,\delta_1]$ and $c_r\in K$, and likewise for the affine parts.
Here $o(h)$ denotes a quantity whose ratio to $h$ tends to zero as $h\to0$.
This proves \ref{it:pb-map}.

For \ref{it:pb-fixed}, we use the decomposition $\mathcal B_{c_r,\delta}[w]=\mathcal A_{c_r,\delta}w+\mathcal B_{c_r,\delta}[0]$ of the affine map.
Both $\mathcal A_{c_r,\delta}$ and $\mathcal B_{c_r,\delta}[0]$ are $C^1$ in $c_r$ by \ref{it:pb-map}, and we denote their derivatives with respect to $c_r$ by $\dot{\mathcal A}_{c_r,\delta}$ and $\dot{\mathcal B}_{c_r,\delta}[0]$.
The fixed point satisfies $w_{c_r,\delta}=(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal A_{c_r,\delta})^{-1}\mathcal B_{c_r,\delta}[0]$, where $\mathrm{Id}$ is the identity map of $C^0(I)$, and $\|(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal A_{c_r,\delta})^{-1}\|_{C^0\to C^0}\le2$ by the uniform $\tfrac12$-contraction.
Define the candidate derivative $\dot w:=(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal A_{c_r,\delta})^{-1} \bigl(\dot{\mathcal A}_{c_r,\delta}\,w_{c_r,\delta}+\dot{\mathcal B}_{c_r,\delta}[0] \bigr)$.
We subtract the fixed-point identities at $c_r+h$ and $c_r$, write $\mathcal A_{c_r+h,\delta}w_{c_r+h,\delta}-\mathcal A_{c_r,\delta}w_{c_r,\delta} =(\mathcal A_{c_r+h,\delta}-\mathcal A_{c_r,\delta})w_{c_r+h,\delta} +\mathcal A_{c_r,\delta}(w_{c_r+h,\delta}-w_{c_r,\delta})$, and obtain
\begin{multline*}
(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal A_{c_r,\delta})
\bigl(w_{c_r+h,\delta}-w_{c_r,\delta}-h\,\dot w\bigr)
=\bigl(\mathcal A_{c_r+h,\delta}-\mathcal A_{c_r,\delta}-h\dot{\mathcal A}_{c_r,\delta}\bigr)
w_{c_r+h,\delta}\\
+\bigl(\mathcal B_{c_r+h,\delta}[0]-\mathcal B_{c_r,\delta}[0]-h\dot{\mathcal B}_{c_r,\delta}[0]\bigr)
+h\,\dot{\mathcal A}_{c_r,\delta}\bigl(w_{c_r+h,\delta}-w_{c_r,\delta}\bigr).
\end{multline*}
Its norm in $C^0(I)$ is $o(h)$, the first two brackets by the uniform Taylor estimates above and the last because $\|\dot{\mathcal A}_{c_r,\delta}\|_{C^0\to C^0}$ is bounded and $\|w_{c_r+h,\delta}-w_{c_r,\delta}\|_{C^0(I)}\le Ch$, which follows from the same identity without the terms containing $\dot w$, $\dot{\mathcal A}$, and $\dot{\mathcal B}$.
Hence $\partial_{c_r}w_{c_r,\delta}=\dot w$ exists, is continuous in $c_r$, and is bounded uniformly in $\delta$.
Let $(w_1,\tilde\chi_1)$ and $(w_2,\tilde\chi_2)$ denote the fixed points with $(\alpha,\beta)=(1,0)$ and $(0,1)$.
The endpoint matrices $\mathcal E^{\pm}$ have the columns $(w_1,\tilde\chi_1)(\pm\varsigma)$ and $(w_2,\tilde\chi_2)(\pm\varsigma)$, and evaluation at the points $x=\pm\varsigma$ and $x=0$, which do not move, is a bounded linear functional on $C^0(I)$.
Hence $\mathcal E^{\pm}$ and the critical values $w(z_j(c_r))=w_{c_r,\delta}(0)$ are $C^1$ in $c_r$ with uniformly bounded derivatives, which proves \ref{it:pb-fixed}.

For \ref{it:pb-inverse}, we first bound $\det\mathcal E^{+}$ from below.
We evaluate the representation in Equation~\eqref{eq:rep} at the endpoint $x=\varsigma$, where the singular integral is empty.
The cross terms containing $\tilde E\tilde f/\tilde U'$ cancel in the determinant, and
\begin{equation}
\det\mathcal E^{+}
=\tilde V(\varsigma)\,\tilde E(\varsigma)\,
\tilde\chi_2(\varsigma),
\qquad
\tilde E(\varsigma)\,\tilde\chi_2(\varsigma)
=1+O(\varsigma^{2})
\end{equation}
by Equation~\eqref{eq:rep-chi} and the fixed-point bounds, so $|\det\mathcal E^{+}|\ge\tfrac12|\tilde V(\varsigma)| \ge\tfrac12m_U\varsigma$ for $\varsigma$ small, uniformly in $(c_r,\delta)$.
To transfer this bound to $\mathcal E^{-}$, we use the first-order-system identities, under which the fixed points solve the system in Equation~\eqref{eq:wchi-recall} by \S\ref{subsec:B-equiv}.
The coefficient matrix of this system has trace $V'/V$, so the modified Wronskian $\Xi=w_1\tilde\chi_2-w_2\tilde\chi_1$ of the two fixed points, as in \cref{prop:local}\ref{it:basis-wronskian}, satisfies $\Xi=\varkappa\tilde V$ with $\varkappa$ constant on each interval where $\tilde V\neq0$.
For $\delta>0$ the interval is connected and one constant applies.
At $\delta=0$, the representation in Equation~\eqref{eq:rep} identifies the two one-sided constants, since the cross terms containing $\tilde E\tilde f/\tilde U'$ cancel in $\Xi$, leaving
\begin{equation}
\frac{\Xi}{\tilde V}
=\tilde E\bigl[(1+\mathscr I_1)\tilde\chi_2
-\mathscr I_2\tilde\chi_1\bigr],
\qquad
\mathscr I_\ell(x):=\int_{\varsigma}^{x}
\Bigl(\frac{\tilde g_1\tilde\chi_\ell}{\tilde V}
+\tilde g_2w_\ell\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d} x' ,
\end{equation}
in which $\tilde\chi_1=O(x^2)$ by Equation~\eqref{eq:rep-chi} with $\beta=0$ and $|\tilde V|=O(|x|)$.
Since also $|\tilde V|\ge m_U|x|$, the integrand of $\mathscr I_1$ is bounded and $\mathscr I_1$ is continuous across $x=0$, while $\mathscr I_2=O(|\log|x||)$ and $\mathscr I_2\tilde\chi_1=O(x^2|\log|x||)\to0$.
Hence, using $\tilde E(0)=\tilde\chi_2(0)=1$, $\Xi/\tilde V$ extends continuously through $x=0$ with the common value $1+\mathscr I_1(0)$.
Consequently $|\det\mathcal E^{-}| =|\det\mathcal E^{+}|\, |\tilde V(-\varsigma)|/|\tilde V(\varsigma)| \ge\tfrac12|\tilde V(-\varsigma)|\ge\tfrac12m_U\varsigma$ in either case.
With these lower bounds and the $C^1$ dependence of $\mathcal E^{\pm}$ from \ref{it:pb-fixed}, the identity $\partial_{c_r}(\mathcal E^{\pm})^{-1} =-(\mathcal E^{\pm})^{-1}(\partial_{c_r}\mathcal E^{\pm}) (\mathcal E^{\pm})^{-1}$ gives the claimed $C^1$ bounds for the inverses, and the maps $T=\mathcal E^{-}(\mathcal E^{+})^{-1}$ inherit them.
At $\delta=0$ this proves the $C^1$ statement in \cref{prop:convergence}.
All bounds are uniform for $\delta\in[0,\delta_1]$.
<!-- end proof-33 -->
<!-- corollary-5: body_md -->
\label{cor:uniformM}
Assume the hypotheses of \cref{thm:lowmach}, and let $\mathfrak a_M$, $M\in[0,M_0]$, be the coefficient tuples of the atmospheres in Equation~\eqref{eq:M-family}, and write $\mathfrak a^{\mathrm{inc}}$ for the tuple with $M=0$, which belongs to the limiting problem, as in the proof of \cref{thm:lowmach}.
Since these tuples are generated by atmospheres, they satisfy the first-order-system identities, and $\delta$ enters them only through $V^2$.
By Equation~\eqref{eq:coef-lip}, $\|\mathfrak a_M-\mathfrak a^{\mathrm{inc}}\|_{\mathcal X}\le CM^2$.
Then there are $M_1\in(0,M_0]$, $r_0>0$, $\delta_1>0$, and $C$ such that the following hold uniformly in $M\le M_1$, with $\mathbb D^+_{r_0}=\{c\in\mathbb{C}:|c-c_k|\le r_0,\ \operatorname{Im} c\ge0\}$ as in \cref{prop:Zhalfdisc}.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item\label{it:uM-transfer} For $\delta\in[0,\delta_1]$ and $c_r\in[c_k-r_0,c_k+r_0]$, the maps $T_\delta$ across the critical neighborhoods and their inverses are bounded and differ from their incompressible counterparts by $O(M^2)$.
\item\label{it:uM-trace} With $m_0:=\min_{|c_r-c_k|\le r_0} |w_\ast^{\mathrm{inc}}(0;c_r)|>0$,
  \begin{equation}
  \inf_{M\le M_1}\ \min_{|c_r-c_k|\le r_0}
  \bigl|w_\ast^{M}(0;c_r)\bigr|\ \ge\ \tfrac12 m_0,
  \qquad
  \inf_{M\le M_1}\ \min_{c\in\mathbb D^+_{r_0}}
  \bigl|w^{M}(0;k,c)\bigr|\ \ge\ \tfrac14 m_0 ,
  \end{equation}
  so each $Z_{\mathrm{a}}^{M}(k,\cdot)$ is holomorphic in the interior of $\mathbb D^+_{r_0}$, and the denominator in Equation~\eqref{eq:Za-formula} does not vanish on $\mathbb D^+_{r_0}$.
\item\label{it:uM-rate} For $|c_r-c_k|\le r_0$ and $0<\delta\le\delta_1$, $|Z_{\mathrm{a}}^{M}(k,c_r+\mathrm{i}\delta)-Z_{\mathrm{a}}^{+,M}(k,c_r)| \le C\delta(1+|\log(\delta/V_\ast)|)$, and $|\partial_{c_r}Z_{\mathrm{a}}^{+,M}(k,c_r)|\le C$.
\item\label{it:uM-modulus} Consequently, for all $c\in\mathbb D^+_{r_0}$,
  \begin{equation}
  \bigl|Z_{\mathrm{a}}^{M}(k,c)-Z_{\mathrm{a}}^{+,M}(k,c_k)\bigr|
  \le\omega\bigl(|c-c_k|\bigr),
  \qquad
  \omega(r):=C\,r\bigl(1+|\log(r/V_\ast)|\bigr),
  \end{equation}
  that is, \cref{prop:Zhalfdisc}\ref{it:Z-modulus} holds with constants uniform in $M\le M_1$.
  If, in addition, $\operatorname{Im} c_1(M)\ge\tfrac12\operatorname{Im} c_1(0)>0$, then the constants $(\varepsilon_0,R,C_0)$ in the proof of \cref{thm:main} can be chosen uniformly in $M\le M_1$.
\item\label{it:uM-complex} The pressure response is $O(M^2)$-close to its incompressible counterpart,
  \begin{equation}
  \sup_{c\in\mathbb D^+_{r_0}}
  \bigl|Z_{\mathrm{a}}^{M}(k,c)-Z_{\mathrm{a}}^{\mathrm{inc}}(k,c)\bigr|\ \le\ C\,M^{2},
  \end{equation}
  where $Z_{\mathrm{a}}^{\mathrm{inc}}(k,\cdot)$ is the pressure response of the limiting problem in Equation~\eqref{eq:rayleigh-limit}.
  On the real interval $[c_k-r_0,c_k+r_0]$, $Z_{\mathrm{a}}^{M}$ and $Z_{\mathrm{a}}^{\mathrm{inc}}$ denote their boundary values.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end corollary-5 -->
<!-- proof-34: body_md -->
We first choose $r_0>0$ as in the proof of \cref{prop:Zhalfdisc}, so that every $c_r\in[c_k-r_0,c_k+r_0]$ is a regular value of $U$ with the same number of critical levels and the incompressible problem satisfies Hypothesis \ref{hyp:nonres} for these $c_r$.
The smaller values of $r_0$ chosen below keep these properties.
Part \ref{it:uM-transfer} follows from \cref{lem:coef-perturb}\ref{it:cp-transfer}, applied on each critical neighborhood to the tuples $\mathfrak a_M$ and $\mathfrak a^{\mathrm{inc}}$.
On the intervals between the critical neighborhoods, the coefficients and the values at $z=h_{\mathrm{a}}$ given in Equation~\eqref{eq:canonical} differ by $O(M^2)$, so Gr\"onwall's inequality gives the same bound for the solution maps.

For \ref{it:uM-trace}, $m_0>0$ by Hypothesis \ref{hyp:nonres} for the incompressible problem and the continuity of $w_\ast^{\mathrm{inc}}(0;\cdot)$ on the compact interval $[c_k-r_0,c_k+r_0]$.
When we compose the maps of \ref{it:uM-transfer} and the solution maps between the critical neighborhoods from $z=h_{\mathrm{a}}$ down to $z=0$, we obtain $|w_\ast^{M}(0;c_r)-w_\ast^{\mathrm{inc}}(0;c_r)|\le CM^2$ uniformly on this interval.
Choose $M_1$ with $CM_1^2\le\tfrac12 m_0$.
By \cref{lem:uniformconst}, the constants of \cref{prop:convergence,prop:limit-solutions} are uniform in $M$, and by \cref{lem:pullback} they are uniform in $c_r$, so $|w^{M}(0;k,c_r+\mathrm{i}\delta)-w_\ast^{M}(0;c_r)|\le C\delta(1+|\log(\delta/V_\ast)|)$ for $\delta\in(0,\delta_1]$, with a constant independent of $M$.
We shrink $r_0$ so that $r_0\le\delta_1$ and $Cr_0(1+|\log(r_0/V_\ast)|)\le\tfrac14 m_0$.
This gives the second bound, because every $c\in\mathbb D^+_{r_0}$ has $\operatorname{Im} c\le r_0\le\delta_1\le V_\ast$ and $\delta\mapsto\delta(1+|\log(\delta/V_\ast)|)$ is increasing on $(0,V_\ast]$.
Since the poles of $Z_{\mathrm{a}}^{M}(k,\cdot)$ are the zeros of $w^{M}(0;k,\cdot)$ (\cref{lem:regular-solve}), this proves \ref{it:uM-trace}.

For \ref{it:uM-rate}, we compose the estimates of \cref{prop:convergence} from $z=h_{\mathrm{a}}$ down to $z=0$.
\cref{lem:uniformconst} makes their constants uniform in $M$, and \ref{it:uM-trace} bounds the denominator of the quotient in Equation~\eqref{eq:Za-formula} from below.
The derivative estimate follows from \cref{lem:pullback}, applied uniformly to the tuples $\mathfrak a_M$, and the same lower bound.

For \ref{it:uM-modulus}, let $c=c_r+\mathrm{i}\delta\in\mathbb D^+_{r_0}$.
We split $|Z_{\mathrm{a}}^{M}(k,c)-Z_{\mathrm{a}}^{+,M}(k,c_k)| \le|Z_{\mathrm{a}}^{M}(k,c)-Z_{\mathrm{a}}^{+,M}(k,c_r)|+|Z_{\mathrm{a}}^{+,M}(k,c_r)-Z_{\mathrm{a}}^{+,M}(k,c_k)|$ and apply \ref{it:uM-rate} with $\delta\le|c-c_k|$ and $|c_r-c_k|\le|c-c_k|$.
The map $r\mapsto r(1+|\log(r/V_\ast)|)$ is increasing for $r\le V_\ast$, so both terms are dominated by $\omega(|c-c_k|)$ after shrinking $r_0\le V_\ast$.
In the proof of \cref{thm:main}, Equation~\eqref{eq:D-ell} uses this modulus of continuity, and the lower bounds for the affine function $\ell$ of that proof use $\operatorname{Im} c_1(M)\ge\tfrac12\operatorname{Im} c_1(0)$ and $c_k$.
The same composition as in \ref{it:uM-trace} bounds the values $(w^{M},\bar{\chi}^{M})(0;k,c)$ uniformly on $\mathbb D^+_{r_0}$, while \ref{it:uM-trace} bounds the denominator in Equation~\eqref{eq:Za-formula} away from zero.
Hence the localization bound and all constants in the proof are uniform for $M\le M_1$.

For \ref{it:uM-complex}, let $c=c_r+\mathrm{i}\delta\in\mathbb D^+_{r_0}$.
Then $\delta\le r_0\le\delta_1$, so \cref{lem:coef-perturb}\ref{it:cp-fixed} and~\ref{it:cp-transfer} apply at $(c_r,\delta)$ to the tuples $\mathfrak a_M$ and $\mathfrak a^{\mathrm{inc}}$.
Hence the maps $T_\delta$ across the critical neighborhoods and the critical values differ by $O(M^2)$, uniformly in $(c_r,\delta)$.
On the intervals containing no critical level, the coefficients of the system in Equation~\eqref{eq:wchi-recall} and the values at $z=h_{\mathrm{a}}$ given in Equation~\eqref{eq:canonical} are $O(M^2)$-close uniformly on $\mathbb D^+_{r_0}$.
Gr\"onwall's inequality gives the same bound for the solution operators.
Composing from $z=h_{\mathrm{a}}$ down to $z=0$ yields $|w^{M}(0;k,c)-w^{\mathrm{inc}}(0;k,c)| +|\bar{\chi}^{M}(0;k,c)-\bar{\chi}^{\mathrm{inc}}(0;k,c)|\le CM^2$ on $\mathbb D^+_{r_0}$.
With the denominators bounded below by \ref{it:uM-trace}, including its case $M=0$, the quotient formula in Equation~\eqref{eq:Za-formula} gives \ref{it:uM-complex}.
<!-- end proof-34 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{lem:composition}
Let $\mathcal S$ be one of the strips above or the circle $\mathbb{T}_{k_0}$, and let $m=2$ or $m=3$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item\label{it:comp-algebra} $H^m(\mathcal S)$ is a Banach algebra, that is, $\|uv\|_{H^m}\le C\|u\|_{H^m}\|v\|_{H^m}$, and the multiplier inequality $\|uv\|_{H^{m-1}}\le C\|u\|_{H^{m}}\|v\|_{H^{m-1}}$ holds.
On $\mathbb{T}_{k_0}$, the space $H^s(\mathbb{T}_{k_0})$ is a Banach algebra for every real $s>\frac12$.
\item\label{it:comp-nemytskii} Let $W$ be an open subset of $\mathbb{R}$ or of $\mathbb{R}^2$, and let $F\in C^{m+2}(W)$.
The Nemytskii map $u\mapsto F(u)$ is the composition with $F$, taken at each point, so that $F(u)(x,\tilde z):=F\bigl(u(x,\tilde z)\bigr)$ on a strip and $F(u)(x):=F\bigl(u(x)\bigr)$ on $\mathbb{T}_{k_0}$.
It is defined and $C^1$ on the set of the $u\in H^m(\mathcal S)$, or $u\in H^m(\mathcal S)^2$ when $W\subset\mathbb{R}^2$, with $\operatorname{ran}(u)\subset W$, where $\operatorname{ran}(u)$ is the set of values of $u$ on $\overline{\mathcal S}$.
This set of fields $u$ is open, because $\operatorname{ran}(u)$, the range of a continuous field on the compact set $\overline{\mathcal S}$, is a compact subset of $W$ and so has a positive distance to $\partial W$, and because the embedding inequality $\|h\|_{L^\infty}\le C_{\mathrm{emb}}\|h\|_{H^m}$ gives $\operatorname{ran}(u+h)\subset W$ when $C_{\mathrm{emb}}\|h\|_{H^m}$ is smaller than this distance.
For $y\in W$ and a vector $h$ with as many components as $y$, we write $\mathrm dF(y)[h]:=\sum_i\partial_iF(y)\,h_i$ and $\mathrm d^2F(y)[h,h]:=\sum_{i,j}\partial_{ij}F(y)\,h_ih_j$ for the first and second derivatives of $F$, where the sums run over the components $y_i$ of $y$, $\partial_i$ is the partial derivative with respect to $y_i$, and $\partial_{ij}:=\partial_i\partial_j$.
For fields $u$ and $h$ on $\mathcal S$, the functions $\mathrm dF(u)[h]$ and $\mathrm d^2F(u)[h,h]$ are given by these formulas at each point.
The Fr\'echet derivative of the Nemytskii map at $u$ is $h\mapsto \mathrm dF(u)[h]$, and in the multivariable Taylor formula
\begin{equation}
F(u+h)-F(u)-\mathrm dF(u)[h]
=\int_0^1(1-t)\,\mathrm d^2F(u+th)[h,h]\,\,\mathrm{d} t
\end{equation}
the right side has $H^m$ norm at most $C\|h\|_{H^m}^{2}$.
For every compact set $K_1\subset W$ and every $R>0$, this constant is uniform over the $u$ with $\|u\|_{H^m}\le R$ and $\operatorname{ran}(u)\subset K_1$ and the $h$ with $\|h\|_{H^m}\le r(K_1)$.
For the same $u$, the norm $\|F(u)\|_{H^m}$ is bounded, and $\|F(u_1)-F(u_2)\|_{H^m}\le C\|u_1-u_2\|_{H^m}$ whenever $\|u_1-u_2\|_{H^m}\le r(K_1)$.
\item\label{it:comp-inverse} If, in addition, $F$ has no zero in $W$, then the same holds for $u\mapsto1/F(u)$.
In particular, $\tilde\rho\mapsto1/\tilde\rho$ is $C^1$ on $\{\operatorname{ran}(\tilde\rho,\tilde S)\subset\mathscr U_{d_0}\}$, where $\tilde\rho\ge\rho_->0$.
\item\label{it:comp-extension} Let $\mathcal S$ be a strip, and let $\operatorname{ext}$ denote the extension that is constant in $\tilde z$, $(\operatorname{ext}f)(x,\tilde z):=f(x)$.
Then
\begin{equation}
\|\operatorname{ext}f\|_{H^m(\mathcal S)}\le C\,\|f\|_{H^m(\mathbb{T}_{k_0})},
\qquad
\|(\operatorname{ext}f)\,u\|_{H^m(\mathcal S)}
\le C\,\|f\|_{H^m(\mathbb{T}_{k_0})}\,\|u\|_{H^m(\mathcal S)},
\end{equation}
the first because $\operatorname{ext}f$ has no vertical derivatives and the strip has finite height, the second by \ref{it:comp-algebra}.
For the use below, $\|\operatorname{ext}\mathcal K_\eta\|_{H^2(\mathcal S)}\le C\|\mathcal K_\eta\|_{H^2(\mathbb{T}_{k_0})}\le C\|\mathcal K_\eta\|_{H^{5/2}(\mathbb{T}_{k_0})}$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- proof-35: body_md -->
For \ref{it:comp-algebra}, we use the Sobolev embedding inequalities $\|u\|_{L^\infty}\le C\|u\|_{H^2}$ and $\|u\|_{L^4}\le C\|u\|_{H^1}$, which hold on the two-dimensional strips and on $\mathbb{T}_{k_0}$.
We write $\partial^j$ for any partial derivative of order $j$ with respect to $(x,\tilde z)$, or with respect to $x$ on $\mathbb{T}_{k_0}$, and $\partial:=\partial^1$.
For $m=2$, expand $\partial^2(uv)$ by the Leibniz rule and estimate the three types of terms by $\|u\|_{L^\infty}\|\partial^2v\|_{L^2}$, $\|\partial u\|_{L^4}\|\partial v\|_{L^4}$, and $\|\partial^2u\|_{L^2}\|v\|_{L^\infty}$.
For $m=3$, the additional terms $\partial^3u\,v$ and $\partial^2u\,\partial v$, and those with $u$ and $v$ exchanged, are estimated in the same way by $\|\partial^3u\|_{L^2}\|v\|_{L^\infty}$ and $\|\partial^2u\|_{L^4}\|\partial v\|_{L^4}$.
The multiplier inequality follows the same way, placing the factor from $H^{m-1}$ in $L^2$ or $L^4$ and the factor from $H^m$ in $L^\infty$ or $L^4$, and for $m=3$ it uses that the first derivative of the factor in $H^3$ lies in $H^2$ and is therefore bounded.
On $\mathbb{T}_{k_0}$, if $u$ and $v$ have the Fourier coefficients $\hat u_n$ and $\hat v_n$, then $uv$ has the coefficients $\sum_{n_1+n_2=n}\hat u_{n_1}\hat v_{n_2}$, the convolution of the two sequences.
The algebra property for real $s>\frac12$ then follows from the Fourier norm, the bound $(1+n^2k_0^2)^{s/2}\le2^s\bigl[(1+n_1^2k_0^2)^{s/2}+(1+n_2^2k_0^2)^{s/2}\bigr]$ for $n=n_1+n_2$, and Young's inequality for sequences, which states that the sum of the squared absolute values of a convolution is at most the sum of the squared absolute values of one sequence times the square of the sum of the absolute values of the other.
The last sum is finite for the Fourier coefficients of a function in $H^s(\mathbb{T}_{k_0})$ by the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality, because $\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(1+n^2k_0^2)^{-s}<\infty$.

For \ref{it:comp-nemytskii}, the function $F(u)$ is bounded, and bounded functions lie in $L^2$ because $\mathcal S$ has finite area or length.
By the chain rule, every term of $\partial^{j}[F(u)]$, $1\le j\le m$, is a partial derivative of $F$ of some order $i$ with $1\le i\le j$, evaluated at $u$ and bounded since $F\in C^m$ on a compact set, times a product of $i$ derivatives of components of $u$ whose orders add up to $j$.
For $m=2$, this product is a single derivative of order at most two, which lies in $L^2$, or the product of two first derivatives, which lies in $L^2$ because first derivatives lie in $L^4$ by the embedding inequality $\|u\|_{L^4}\le C\|u\|_{H^1}$.
For $m=3$, the first derivatives lie in $H^2$ and are therefore bounded.
Since the orders add up to at most three, at most one factor has order two or three, and this factor lies in $L^2$, so the product is bounded or lies in $L^2$.
This uses only $F\in C^m$.
Let $u_1$ and $u_2$ have ranges in $K_1$ and $H^m$ norms at most $R$, with $\|u_1-u_2\|_{H^m}\le r(K_1)$.
Then $\|u_1-u_2\|_{L^\infty}\le\frac12\min\{1,\operatorname{dist}(K_1,\partial W)\}$, so the ranges of $u_2+t(u_1-u_2)$, $t\in[0,1]$, lie in the compact set $K_2$ of points whose distance to $K_1$ is at most this number, and $K_2\subset W$.
The Lipschitz bound follows by writing $F(u_1)-F(u_2)=\int_0^1\mathrm dF\bigl(u_2+t(u_1-u_2)\bigr)[u_1-u_2]\,\,\mathrm{d} t$ and applying \ref{it:comp-algebra} together with the boundedness statement for the partial derivatives $\partial_iF\in C^{m+1}$ on $K_2$.
This uses only $F\in C^{m+1}$.
For the $C^1$ statement, the multivariable Taylor formula displayed in the statement holds pointwise.
By definition, $\mathrm d^2F(u+th)[h,h]$ is the sum of the products $h_ih_j\,\partial_{ij}F(u+th)$, and the ranges of $u+th$ lie in $K_2$ for $\|h\|_{H^m}\le r(K_1)$ by the same argument.
Hence \ref{it:comp-algebra} and the boundedness statement applied to $\partial_{ij}F\in C^{m}$, which is where $F\in C^{m+2}$ enters, bound the remainder by $C\|h\|_{H^m}^2$, uniformly for $t\in[0,1]$.
Continuity of $u\mapsto\mathrm dF(u)$ in the operator norm of linear maps from $H^m(\mathcal S)$, or from $H^m(\mathcal S)^2$, to $H^m(\mathcal S)$ follows from the Lipschitz bound for the partial derivatives $\partial_iF$ and \ref{it:comp-algebra}.
The openness of the domain was shown in the statement.
Item \ref{it:comp-inverse} is \ref{it:comp-nemytskii} applied to $1/F\in C^{m+2}(W)$.
Item \ref{it:comp-extension} was proved in the statement.
<!-- end proof-35 -->
<!-- proposition-12: body_md -->
\label{prop:residual}
The residual $\mathcal N=(\mathcal N_{\mathrm{dyn}},\mathcal N_{\mathrm{con}})$ is a well-defined map $\mathcal O\to\mathbb Y$, and it is continuously Fr\'echet differentiable.
<!-- end proposition-12 -->
<!-- proof-36: body_md -->
By Equations~\eqref{eq:eta-operators} and \eqref{eq:Ndyn-mass}, each component of $\mathcal N_{\mathrm{dyn}}$ on a strip is a finite sum of products of factors of the following kinds.
The first kind is an unknown or a first derivative of an unknown, which lies in $H^2$.
The second kind is one of the functions $\phi\eta_x$ and $\phi'\eta$, which lie in $H^3$ of the strip, since $\eta\in H^4(\mathbb{T}_{k_0})$ and $\phi$ is smooth.
The third kind is $1/J$ with $J=1+\phi'\eta$, or the inverse density $1/\tilde\rho$ from the momentum components.
Both lie in $H^2$ by \cref{lem:composition}\ref{it:comp-inverse} with $m=2$, since $J\ge\tfrac34$ and $\tilde\rho\ge\rho_->0$ on $\mathcal O$.
The fourth kind is one of the compositions $P_\rho(\tilde\rho,\tilde S)$ and $P_S(\tilde\rho,\tilde S)$, which appear when the chain rule is applied to $\partial_x^{\eta}\tilde p_{\mathrm a}$ and $\partial_z^{\eta}\tilde p_{\mathrm a}$.
They lie in $H^2$ by \cref{lem:composition}\ref{it:comp-nemytskii} with $m=2$ and $W=\mathscr U$, because $P_\rho,P_S\in C^4$ by $P\in C^5$.
The fifth kind is $\operatorname{ext}\mathcal K_\eta(\mathbb U)$, from the last term of $\mathfrak M^{\eta}$.
The function $\mathcal K_\eta(\mathbb U)$ of Equation~\eqref{eq:etat-sub} is built from the traces of $\tilde u$ and $\tilde w$ at $\tilde z=0$, which lie in $H^{5/2}(\mathbb{T}_{k_0})$ because the trace map $H^3(\mathcal S_{\mathrm a})\to H^{5/2}(\mathbb{T}_{k_0})$ is bounded, and from $\eta_x\in H^3(\mathbb{T}_{k_0})$, multiplied in the algebra $H^{5/2}(\mathbb{T}_{k_0})$.
By \cref{lem:composition}\ref{it:comp-extension}, multiplication by its extension is a bounded map on $H^2$ of the strip.
Every product of two $H^2$ factors lies in $H^2$ by \cref{lem:composition}\ref{it:comp-algebra}, so each component of $\mathcal N_{\mathrm{dyn}}$ on a strip maps $\mathcal O$ into $H^2$, and the component $[\mathcal N_{\mathrm{dyn}}]_\eta$ maps $\mathcal O$ into $H^{5/2}(\mathbb{T}_{k_0})$.
Each factor is a $C^1$ function of $\mathbb U$ with values in $H^2$, either because it is linear in $\mathbb U$ or by \cref{lem:composition}, and finite sums and products of $C^1$ maps into a Banach algebra are $C^1$.
The components of $\mathcal N_{\mathrm{con}}$ are treated in the same way, since the traces are bounded linear maps $H^3(\mathcal S)\to H^{5/2}(\mathbb{T}_{k_0})$ and products on $\mathbb{T}_{k_0}$ use the algebra $H^{5/2}(\mathbb{T}_{k_0})$.
In the dynamic condition, $P(\tilde\rho,\tilde S)$ lies in $H^3(\mathcal S_{\mathrm a})$ by \cref{lem:composition}\ref{it:comp-nemytskii} with $m=3$, since $P\in C^5$.
The curvature $\kappa(\eta)=-\eta_{xx}(1+\eta_x^2)^{-3/2}$ is the product of $\eta_{xx}\in H^2(\mathbb{T}_{k_0})$ and $(1+\eta_x^2)^{-3/2}$.
The second factor lies in $H^3(\mathbb{T}_{k_0})$ by \cref{lem:composition}\ref{it:comp-nemytskii} with $m=3$ and the smooth function $t\mapsto(1+t^2)^{-3/2}$, since $\eta_x\in H^3(\mathbb{T}_{k_0})$.
Hence $\kappa(\eta)\in H^2(\mathbb{T}_{k_0})$ by the multiplier inequality of \cref{lem:composition}\ref{it:comp-algebra}, and $\kappa$ is $C^1$ in $\eta$ for the same reasons.
Therefore $\mathcal N$ maps $\mathcal O$ into the space $\mathbb Y$ of Equation~\eqref{eq:Yspace} and is $C^1$.
<!-- end proof-36 -->
<!-- proposition-13: body_md -->
\label{prop:goodunknown}
Let $\mathbb U_0\in\mathcal O$ be the laminar state of \S\ref{subsec:flattening}.
Define the \emph{good-unknown map} $\mathcal T\in\mathcal L(\mathbb X)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:goodunknown}
\mathcal T\,(r,u,w,s;\,v_x,v_z,q_{\mathrm w};\,\zeta)
:=\bigl(r+\phi\zeta\rho_0',\ u+\phi\zeta U',\ w,\ s;\
v_x,\ v_z,\ q_{\mathrm w}+\phi\zeta p_{\mathrm w,0}';\ \zeta\bigr),
\end{equation}
a bounded linear map of $\mathbb X$ onto itself with a bounded inverse, which replaces $\zeta$ by $-\zeta$ in the correction terms.
Let $\mathcal T_{\mathrm{ev}}$ be the map induced by $\mathcal T$ on the components $(r,u,w,s,v_x,v_z,\zeta)$ with time derivatives, which adds $\phi\zeta\rho_0'$ to $r$ and $\phi\zeta U'$ to $u$.
Then the following hold for every $\mathbb V\in\mathbb X$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item\label{it:gu-dyn} For the evolution equations,
\begin{equation}
\mathrm d\mathcal N_{\mathrm{dyn}}(\mathbb U_0)\bigl[\mathcal T\mathbb
V\bigr]
=\mathcal T_{\mathrm{ev}}\bigl[\mathbb A\mathbb V\bigr].
\end{equation}
Thus the right side adds $\phi\,[\mathbb A\mathbb V]_\zeta\,\rho_0'$ to the $r$-component and $\phi\,[\mathbb A\mathbb V]_\zeta\,U'$ to the $u$-component of $\mathbb A\mathbb V$.
\item\label{it:gu-con} For the constraint equations,
\begin{multline*}
\mathrm d\mathcal N_{\mathrm{con}}(\mathbb U_0)\bigl[\mathcal T\mathbb
V\bigr]
=\Bigl(\nabla\cdot\mathbf v;\ \
w|_{z=0}-v_z|_{z=0};\\
\bigl(q_{\mathrm w}|_{z=0}-\rho_{\mathrm{w}} g\zeta\bigr)
-\bigl(q|_{z=0}-\rho_0(0)g\zeta\bigr)+\sigma\zeta_{xx};\ \
w|_{z=h_{\mathrm{a}}};\ v_z|_{z=-h_{\mathrm{w}}}\Bigr),
\end{multline*}
with $\mathbf v=(v_x,v_z)$ and $q=a^2r+P_Ss$ the Eulerian pressure variation of \S\ref{sec:linearization}.
\end{enumerate}
Consequently a $C^1$ curve $t\mapsto\mathbb V(t)\in\mathbb X$ solves the linearized system of \cref{prop:linearized} if and only if $t\mapsto\mathcal T\mathbb V(t)$ solves the linearization at $\mathbb U_0$ of the flattened system in Equation~\eqref{eq:flat-system}.
In other words, after the change of variables $\mathcal T$, the Fr\'echet derivative $\mathrm d\mathcal N(\mathbb U_0)$ is the linearized system of \cref{prop:linearized}.
<!-- end proposition-13 -->
<!-- proof-37: body_md -->
Write $(\tilde r,\tilde u^1,\tilde w^1,\tilde s;\,\tilde{\mathbf v}^1;\,\tilde q_{\mathrm w};\,\zeta):=\mathcal T\mathbb V$, so that $\tilde r=r+\phi\zeta\rho_0'$, $\tilde u^1=u+\phi\zeta U'$, $\tilde w^1=w$, $\tilde s=s$, $\tilde{\mathbf v}^1=\mathbf v$, and $\tilde q_{\mathrm w}=q_{\mathrm w}+\phi\zeta p_{\mathrm w,0}'$.
These are the chain-rule relations between the variation of a pullback and the Eulerian variation at fixed points.
For example, for a family of densities $\rho(\vartheta)$ with $\rho(0)=\rho_0$ and $\partial_\vartheta\rho|_{\vartheta=0}=r$, one has $\partial_\vartheta\bigl[\rho(\vartheta)\circ\Phi_{\vartheta\zeta}\bigr] \big|_{\vartheta=0}=r+\phi\zeta\,\rho_0'$.
The superscript $1$ marks a variation whose letter alone would denote a field of $\mathbb U$, as for $\tilde u^1$, $\tilde w^1$, and $\tilde{\mathbf v}^1$, while $\tilde r$, $\tilde s$, and $\tilde q_{\mathrm w}$ need no mark because the corresponding fields of $\mathbb U$ are $\tilde\rho$, $\tilde S$, and $\tilde p_{\mathrm w}$.
Since $\mathcal N$ is $C^1$ by \cref{prop:residual}, its derivative in the direction $\mathcal T\mathbb V$ can be computed term by term.
We display the mass and momentum components of the air, in which the cancellations occur, and indicate the remaining components.

At $\mathbb U_0$, we have $\tilde\rho=\rho_0(\tilde z)$, $\tilde u=U(\tilde z)$, $\tilde w=0$, $\eta=0$, $J=1$, and $\mathcal K_\eta(\mathbb U_0)=0$.
The derivative of $\mathcal K_\eta(\mathbb U)$ at $\mathbb U_0$ in the direction $\mathcal T\mathbb V$, which we denote by $\mathcal K^1$, is $\mathcal K^1=\tilde w^1|_{\tilde z=0}-U(0)\zeta_x=w|_{z=0}$, because $U(0)=0$.
For the mass equation, the derivative of the component $[\mathcal N_{\mathrm{dyn}}]_{\tilde\rho}$ of Equation~\eqref{eq:Ndyn-mass} at $\mathbb U_0$ in the direction $\mathcal T\mathbb V$ is
\begin{equation}
\mathrm d\bigl[\mathcal N_{\mathrm{dyn}}\bigr]_{\tilde\rho}
\bigl[\mathcal T\mathbb V\bigr]
=\phi\rho_0'\,w|_{z=0}
-U\partial_x\tilde r+U\phi\zeta_x\rho_0'
-\tilde w^1\rho_0'
-\rho_0\bigl(\partial_x\tilde u^1-\phi\zeta_xU'
+\partial_{\tilde z}\tilde w^1\bigr).
\end{equation}
When we substitute $\tilde r=r+\phi\zeta\rho_0'$ and $\tilde u^1=u+\phi\zeta U'$, the terms $-U\partial_x(\phi\zeta\rho_0')=-U\phi\zeta_x\rho_0'$ and $-\rho_0\partial_x(\phi\zeta U')=-\rho_0\phi\zeta_xU'$ cancel the two terms containing $\zeta_x$, and we obtain
\begin{equation}
\mathrm d\bigl[\mathcal N_{\mathrm{dyn}}\bigr]_{\tilde\rho}
\bigl[\mathcal T\mathbb V\bigr]
=\phi\rho_0'\,w|_{z=0}
+\Bigl[-U\partial_xr-\rho_0'w-\rho_0(\partial_xu+\partial_zw)\Bigr].
\end{equation}
The bracket is $[\mathbb A\mathbb V]_r$, the right-hand side of Equation~\eqref{eq:lin-cont} solved for $\partial_tr$, and the first term is $\phi\rho_0'[\mathbb A\mathbb V]_\zeta$.
Together they form the $r$-component of $\mathcal T_{\mathrm{ev}}[\mathbb A\mathbb V]$ in \ref{it:gu-dyn}.

For vertical momentum, we differentiate the component $[\mathcal N_{\mathrm{dyn}}]_{\tilde w}$ of Equation~\eqref{eq:Ndyn-mass}.
At $\mathbb U_0$, the factor $\tilde w=0$ removes every coupling of $\mathfrak M^{\eta}\tilde w$ to $\eta$, so this term contributes $-U\partial_x\tilde w^1$.
The variation of the pressure $\tilde p_{\mathrm a}=P(\tilde\rho,\tilde S)$ in the direction $\mathcal T\mathbb V$ is the flattened counterpart $\tilde q:=a^2\tilde r+P_S\tilde s$ of $q=a^2r+P_Ss$, and $\tilde q=q+\phi\zeta\,a^2\rho_0'=q+\phi\zeta\,p_0'$ by $S_0'=0$ and Equation~\eqref{eq:hydrostatic}.
Hence
\begin{equation}
\mathrm d\bigl[\mathcal N_{\mathrm{dyn}}\bigr]_{\tilde w}
\bigl[\mathcal T\mathbb V\bigr]
=-U\partial_x\tilde w^1
-\frac1{\rho_0}\Bigl[\partial_{\tilde z}\bigl(q+\phi\zeta p_0'\bigr)
-\phi'\zeta\,p_0'
+\tilde r\,g\Bigr].
\end{equation}
The term $-\phi'\zeta p_0'$ is the variation of $1/J$ against the base-state gradient $\partial_{\tilde z}p_0=p_0'$, and the term $\tilde rg$ is the variation of $1/\tilde\rho$ against $\partial_{\tilde z}p_0=-\rho_0g$.
Now $\partial_{\tilde z}(\phi\zeta p_0')-\phi'\zeta p_0' =\phi\zeta p_0''$ and $\tilde rg=rg+\phi\zeta\rho_0'g$, and hydrostatic balance gives $p_0''+g\rho_0'=(-\rho_0g)'+g\rho_0'=0$.
So the corrections containing $\zeta$ cancel because the base state solves the equations, and this component equals $-U\partial_xw-(\partial_zq+gr)/\rho_0=[\mathbb A\mathbb V]_w$, the right-hand side of Equation~\eqref{eq:lin-zmom} solved for $\partial_tw$.
Since $\mathcal T$ leaves the $\tilde w$-component unchanged, this is the corresponding component of the identity in \ref{it:gu-dyn}.

For horizontal momentum, we differentiate the component $[\mathcal N_{\mathrm{dyn}}]_{\tilde u}$ of Equation~\eqref{eq:Ndyn-mass}.
The variation of $1/\tilde\rho$ multiplies $\partial_x^{\eta}p_0$, which vanishes at the laminar state because the base pressure $p_0$ does not depend on $x$ and $\eta=0$, so no terms with $1/\rho_0^2$ remain.
This gives
\begin{equation}
\mathrm d\bigl[\mathcal N_{\mathrm{dyn}}\bigr]_{\tilde u}
\bigl[\mathcal T\mathbb V\bigr]
=\phi\,U'\,\mathcal K^1
-U\partial_x\tilde u^1+U\phi\zeta_xU'
-\tilde w^1U'
-\frac1{\rho_0}\bigl[\partial_x(q+\phi\zeta p_0')
-\phi\zeta_x\,p_0'\bigr],
\end{equation}
with $\mathcal K^1=w|_{z=0}$.
The mesh term of $\mathfrak M^{\eta}$ enters with the plus sign, since it is subtracted inside $\mathfrak M^{\eta}$.
When we substitute $\tilde u^1=u+\phi\zeta U'$, the term $-U\partial_x(\phi\zeta U')=-U\phi\zeta_xU'$ cancels $+U\phi\zeta_xU'$, and $\partial_x(\phi\zeta p_0')=\phi\zeta_xp_0'$ cancels the term $-\phi\zeta_xp_0'$ in the last bracket, which leaves
\begin{equation}
\mathrm d\bigl[\mathcal N_{\mathrm{dyn}}\bigr]_{\tilde u}
\bigl[\mathcal T\mathbb V\bigr]
=\Bigl[-U\partial_xu-U'w-\frac1{\rho_0}\partial_xq\Bigr]
+\phi\,U'\,w|_{z=0}
=[\mathbb A\mathbb V]_u+\phi\,U'\,[\mathbb A\mathbb V]_\zeta .
\end{equation}
This is the $u$-component of $\mathcal T_{\mathrm{ev}}[\mathbb A\mathbb V]$, and the bracket is Equation~\eqref{eq:lin-xmom} solved for $\partial_tu$.
Thus the mesh velocity supplies the good-unknown correction to the time derivative of $u$.
The entropy component is pure transport, because $S_0'=0$.
The water momentum components repeat these computations with $\rho_{\mathrm{w}}$ in place of $\rho_0$, zero base velocity, and $p_{\mathrm w,0}''=0$ in place of hydrostatic balance, which gives Equation~\eqref{eq:linear-water}.
The divergence constraint has no terms containing $\eta$ at $\mathbb U_0$, because these terms multiply derivatives of the base water velocity, which is zero.

For the constraint equations in Equation~\eqref{eq:Ncon}, the kinematic compatibility condition linearizes to $w|_{z=0}-U(0)\zeta_x-v_z|_{z=0}=w|_{z=0}-v_z|_{z=0}$ by Hypothesis \ref{hyp:noslip}.
The component $[\mathcal N_{\mathrm{dyn}}]_\eta$ linearizes to $\mathcal K^1=w|_{z=0}$, which together with the compatibility condition reproduces Equation~\eqref{eq:lin-kinematic}.
The dynamic condition linearizes to $\tilde q_{\mathrm w}|_{\tilde z=0}-\tilde q|_{\tilde z=0}-\sigma \mathrm d\kappa(0)[\zeta]$, where $\mathrm d\kappa(0)$ is the Fr\'echet derivative of the curvature at $\eta=0$.
Here $\tilde q_{\mathrm w}|_{\tilde z=0}$ and $\tilde q|_{\tilde z=0}$ are the traces at $\tilde z=0$ of the variations of $\tilde p_{\mathrm w}$ and $P(\tilde\rho,\tilde S)$, which equal the variations of their traces because the trace map is linear and bounded.
Since $\phi(0)=1$, these traces are $\tilde q_{\mathrm w}|_{\tilde z=0}=q_{\mathrm w}|_{z=0}+\zeta p_{\mathrm w,0}'(0)=q_{\mathrm w}|_{z=0}-\rho_{\mathrm{w}} g\zeta$ and $\tilde q|_{\tilde z=0}=q|_{z=0}+\zeta p_0'(0) =q|_{z=0}-\rho_0(0)g\zeta$, and $\mathrm d\kappa(0)[\zeta]=-\zeta_{xx}$, which gives the Lagrangian combination of Equation~\eqref{eq:lin-dynamic}.
Thus, in terms of the good unknown, the variations of the flattened pressures at $\tilde z=0$ are the Lagrangian pressure variations.
The impermeability conditions at the top and bottom are unchanged by $\mathcal T$ because $\phi$ vanishes there.
The final equivalence statement follows because $\mathcal T$ is invertible, with a bounded inverse, and does not depend on time.
<!-- end proof-37 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
\label{def:modal-operator}
Let $k>0$ and $\varepsilon>0$, with the density scaling of \S\ref{subsec:eps-family}, so that $\rho_0(0)=\varepsilon\rho_{\mathrm{w}}$.
Set
\begin{equation}
\mathcal V_k:=\bigl\{\hat{\mathbf v}=(\hat v_x,\hat v_z)\in
L^2(-h_{\mathrm{w}},0)^2:\ \mathrm{i} k\hat v_x+\hat v_z'=0,\ \hat v_z(-h_{\mathrm{w}})=0\bigr\},
\end{equation}
where the divergence condition holds in the sense of distributions, that is, $\int_{-h_{\mathrm{w}}}^0(\mathrm{i} k\hat v_x\psi-\hat v_z\psi')\,\,\mathrm{d} z=0$ for every $\psi\in C_c^\infty(-h_{\mathrm{w}},0)$.
Here $C_c^\infty(-h_{\mathrm{w}},0)$ is the space of smooth functions with compact support in $(-h_{\mathrm{w}},0)$, the smooth functions on $(-h_{\mathrm{w}},0)$ that vanish near $-h_{\mathrm{w}}$ and near $0$.
For such $\hat{\mathbf v}$, $\hat v_z'=-\mathrm{i} k\hat v_x\in L^2$, so $\hat v_z\in H^1$ and its value at $z=-h_{\mathrm{w}}$ is defined.
The set $\mathcal V_k$ is a closed subspace of $L^2(-h_{\mathrm{w}},0)^2$, because the divergence condition passes to limits in $L^2$, and then $\hat v_z$ converges in $H^1$.
Set
\begin{gather*}
\mathcal H_k:=L^2(0,h_{\mathrm{a}})^{4}\times\mathcal V_k\times\mathbb{C}\ \ni\
\hat{\mathbb U}=(\hat u,\hat w,\hat q,\hat s,\hat{\mathbf v},\hat\zeta),\\
\mathcal D(\mathbb{L}_k):=\bigl\{\hat{\mathbb U}\in\mathcal H_k:\
\hat w,\hat q\in H^1(0,h_{\mathrm{a}}),\ \hat w(h_{\mathrm{a}})=0,\
\hat w(0)=\hat v_z(0)\bigr\},
\end{gather*}
where $\mathcal H_k$ carries the norm given by $\|\hat{\mathbb U}\|_{\mathcal H_k}^2:=\|\hat u\|_{L^2}^2+\|\hat w\|_{L^2}^2+\|\hat q\|_{L^2}^2+\|\hat s\|_{L^2}^2+\|\hat{\mathbf v}\|_{L^2}^2+|\hat\zeta|^2$.
For $\hat{\mathbb U}\in\mathcal D(\mathbb{L}_k)$, define $\hat r:=(\hat q-P_S\hat s)/a^2$ and the water pressure amplitude
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qw-solve}
\hat q_{\mathrm w}(z):=\bigl[\hat q(0)+G_\sigma\hat\zeta\bigr]\,
\frac{\cosh k(z+h_{\mathrm{w}})}{\cosh kh_{\mathrm{w}}},
\qquad
G_\sigma:=\bigl(\rho_{\mathrm{w}}-\rho_0(0)\bigr)g+\sigma k^2 .
\end{equation}
It is the unique solution of $\hat q_{\mathrm w}''=k^2\hat q_{\mathrm w}$ on $(-h_{\mathrm{w}},0)$ with $\hat q_{\mathrm w}'(-h_{\mathrm{w}})=0$ and $\hat q_{\mathrm w}(0)=\hat q(0)+G_\sigma\hat\zeta$, which is the linearized dynamic condition in Equation~\eqref{eq:lin-dynamic} for these amplitudes.
Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Lop-def}
\mathbb{L}_k\hat{\mathbb U}:=
\begin{pmatrix}
-\mathrm{i} kU\hat u-U'\hat w-\mathrm{i} k\hat q/\rho_0\\[2pt]
-\mathrm{i} kU\hat w-\bigl(\hat q'+g\hat r\bigr)/\rho_0\\[2pt]
-\mathrm{i} kU\hat q+\rho_0g\hat w-\rho_0a^2\bigl(\mathrm{i} k\hat u+\hat w'\bigr)\\[2pt]
-\mathrm{i} kU\hat s\\[2pt]
-\bigl(\mathrm{i} k\hat q_{\mathrm w},\ \hat q_{\mathrm w}'\bigr)/\rho_{\mathrm{w}}\\[2pt]
\hat v_z(0)
\end{pmatrix}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
\label{lem:closed}
The operator $\mathbb{L}_k$ is densely defined and closed on $\mathcal H_k$.
That is, $\mathcal D(\mathbb{L}_k)$ is dense in $\mathcal H_k$, and the graph $\{(\hat{\mathbb U},\mathbb{L}_k\hat{\mathbb U}):\hat{\mathbb U}\in\mathcal D(\mathbb{L}_k)\}$ is closed in $\mathcal H_k\times\mathcal H_k$.
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-38: body_md -->
To prove density, let $\hat{\mathbb U}\in\mathcal H_k$ and approximate $\hat u,\hat q,\hat s$ in $L^2$ by smooth functions.
We keep $\hat{\mathbf v}$ and $\hat\zeta$, and we approximate $\hat w$ by $\hat w_n:=\check w_n+\hat v_z(0)\,\theta_n$.
Here $\check w_n\in C_c^\infty(0,h_{\mathrm{a}})$ with $\check w_n\to\hat w$ in $L^2$, which is possible because $C_c^\infty(0,h_{\mathrm{a}})$ is dense in $L^2(0,h_{\mathrm{a}})$, and $\theta_n(z):=\max\{0,1-nz/h_{\mathrm{a}}\}$ is a piecewise linear function with $\theta_n(0)=1$, $\theta_n=0$ on $[h_{\mathrm{a}}/n,h_{\mathrm{a}}]$, and $\|\theta_n\|_{L^2}\to0$.
Then $\hat w_n\in H^1$, $\hat w_n(0)=\hat v_z(0)$, $\hat w_n(h_{\mathrm{a}})=0$, and $\hat w_n\to\hat w$ in $L^2$, so the approximants lie in $\mathcal D(\mathbb{L}_k)$ and converge to $\hat{\mathbb U}$ in $\mathcal H_k$.

For closedness, let $\hat{\mathbb U}_n\in\mathcal D(\mathbb{L}_k)$ with $\hat{\mathbb U}_n\to\hat{\mathbb U}$ and $\mathbb{L}_k\hat{\mathbb U}_n\to\mathbb F$ in $\mathcal H_k$.
By the $\hat q$-component of Equation~\eqref{eq:Lop-def}, $\rho_0a^2\hat w_n'=-[\mathbb{L}_k\hat{\mathbb U}_n]_{\hat q} -\mathrm{i} kU\hat q_n+\rho_0g\hat w_n-\mathrm{i} k\rho_0a^2\hat u_n$ converges in $L^2$.
Since $\rho_0a^2$ is continuous and positive on $[0,h_{\mathrm{a}}]$, $\hat w_n'$ converges in $L^2$, so $\hat w\in H^1$, $\hat w_n\to\hat w$ in $H^1$, and the values $\hat w_n(0)$ and $\hat w_n(h_{\mathrm{a}})$ converge.
By the $\hat w$-component, $\hat q_n'=-\rho_0[\mathbb{L}_k\hat{\mathbb U}_n]_{\hat w} -\mathrm{i} k\rho_0U\hat w_n-g\hat r_n$ converges in $L^2$, because $\hat r_n=(\hat q_n-P_S\hat s_n)/a^2$ converges in $L^2$, as $1/a^2$ and $P_S/a^2$ are bounded.
Hence $\hat q\in H^1$ and $\hat q_n(0)\to\hat q(0)$.
On the water side, $\hat v_{z,n}'=-\mathrm{i} k\hat v_{x,n}$ converges in $L^2$, so $\hat v_{z,n}\to\hat v_z$ in $H^1$ and $\hat v_{z,n}(0)\to\hat v_z(0)$.
The boundary conditions $\hat w(h_{\mathrm{a}})=0$ and $\hat w(0)=\hat v_z(0)$ pass to the limit, so $\hat{\mathbb U}\in\mathcal D(\mathbb{L}_k)$.
The water pressure in Equation~\eqref{eq:qw-solve} is $\hat q(0)+G_\sigma\hat\zeta$ times a smooth function of $z$ that does not depend on $n$, so it converges with all its derivatives, uniformly on $[-h_{\mathrm{w}},0]$, when $(\hat q_n(0),\hat\zeta_n)\to(\hat q(0),\hat\zeta)$.
Hence $\mathbb{L}_k\hat{\mathbb U}_n$ converges to $\mathbb{L}_k\hat{\mathbb U}$ in $\mathcal H_k$, component by component, and $\mathbb F=\mathbb{L}_k\hat{\mathbb U}$.
<!-- end proof-38 -->
<!-- theorem-7: body_md -->
\label{thm:pointspec}
Let $k>0$, $\varepsilon>0$, and $c\in\mathbb{C}$ with $\operatorname{Im} c\neq0$, and set $\lambda:=-\mathrm{i} kc$.
Then $\lambda$ is an eigenvalue of $\mathbb{L}_k$ if and only if $\mathfrak{E}(k,c,\varepsilon)=0$.
In that case the eigenspace $\ker(\mathbb{L}_k-\lambda)$, the set of $\hat{\mathbb U}\in\mathcal D(\mathbb{L}_k)$ with $\mathbb{L}_k\hat{\mathbb U}=\lambda\hat{\mathbb U}$, is one-dimensional, and it is spanned by the amplitudes $(\hat u,\hat w,\hat q,\hat s,\hat{\mathbf v},\hat\zeta)$ of the normal mode of \cref{prop:reduction}.
In particular every root $c$ of the dispersion relation in Equation~\eqref{eq:dispersion} with $\operatorname{Im} c>0$ gives the eigenvalue $\lambda=-\mathrm{i} kc$ of $\mathbb{L}_k$ with $\operatorname{Re}\lambda=k\operatorname{Im} c>0$.
<!-- end theorem-7 -->
<!-- proof-39: body_md -->
Let $\hat{\mathbb U}\in\mathcal D(\mathbb{L}_k)$, $\hat{\mathbb U}\neq0$, satisfy $\mathbb{L}_k\hat{\mathbb U}=\lambda\hat{\mathbb U}$.
The $\hat s$-component of this equation gives $\mathrm{i} k(c-U)\hat s=0$, and $V=U-c\neq0$, so $\hat s\equiv0$ and $\hat r=\hat q/a^2$.
The $\hat u$-component contains no derivative, $\mathrm{i} kV\hat u=-(U'\hat w+\mathrm{i} k\hat q/\rho_0)$, and determines $\hat u\in L^2$ from $(\hat w,\hat q)$.
When we substitute this expression into the $\hat q$-component, multiply by $V$, and rearrange, we obtain
\begin{equation}
\mathrm{i} k\hat q
=\frac{\rho_0a^2}{a^2-V^2}\bigl[V\hat w'-(U'+bV)\hat w\bigr]
=A\bigl[V\hat w'-(U'+bV)\hat w\bigr],
\end{equation}
where the division by $a^2-V^2$ is permitted for $\operatorname{Im} c\neq0$ by \cref{lem:regular-solve}, and $A$ is defined in Equation~\eqref{eq:A-def}.
Thus $\mathrm{i} k\hat q=\chi$ with $\chi$ as in Equation~\eqref{eq:A-def}, and the $\hat w$-component with $\hat r=\hat q/a^2$ becomes $\chi'=-b\chi+\rho_0k^2V\hat w$.
Hence the pair $(\hat w,\bar{\chi})$ with $\bar{\chi}=\mathrm{i} k\hat q/(\varepsilon\rho_{\mathrm{w}})$ solves the system in Equation~\eqref{eq:wchi-recall}.
The coefficients of this system are continuous, so $\hat w,\hat q\in C^1$.
The condition $\hat w(h_{\mathrm{a}})=0$ makes the air component a constant multiple $\mu\,(w,\bar{\chi})(\cdot;k,c)$ of the top-normalized solution (\cref{lem:regular-solve}).
On the water side, $\lambda\neq0$, and the water component gives $\hat{\mathbf v}=-\nabla_k\hat q_{\mathrm w}/(\lambda\rho_{\mathrm{w}})$ with $\nabla_k:=(\mathrm{i} k,\partial_z)$.
Hence $\hat v_z(0)=-\hat q_{\mathrm w}'(0)/(\lambda\rho_{\mathrm{w}}) =-k\tanh(kh_{\mathrm{w}})\,\hat q_{\mathrm w}(0)/(\lambda\rho_{\mathrm{w}})$ by Equation~\eqref{eq:qw-solve}.
The $\hat\zeta$-component $\lambda\hat\zeta=\hat v_z(0)$ and the domain constraint $\hat w(0)=\hat v_z(0)$ then give $\hat w(0)=\lambda\hat\zeta=\mathrm{i} kV(0)\hat\zeta$ and
\begin{equation}
\lambda^2\rho_{\mathrm{w}}\,\hat\zeta=-k\tanh(kh_{\mathrm{w}})\,\hat q_{\mathrm w}(0)
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
\rho_{\mathrm{w}}\Bigl[kc^2\coth(kh_{\mathrm{w}})-g-\frac{\sigma k^2}{\rho_{\mathrm{w}}}\Bigr]\hat\zeta
=\hat q(0)-\rho_0(0)g\,\hat\zeta ,
\end{equation}
where the equivalence uses $\lambda^2=-k^2c^2$ and Equation~\eqref{eq:qw-solve}.
The second relation is the dispersion relation in Equation~\eqref{eq:dispersion0}, written for general $\hat\zeta$.
If $\hat\zeta=0$, this relation and Equation~\eqref{eq:qw-solve} force $\hat q_{\mathrm w}(0)=\hat q(0)=0$, and $\hat w(0)=\lambda\hat\zeta=0$.
Then $(\hat w,\bar{\chi})(0)=(0,0)$, so $\mu=0$ by uniqueness for the system in Equation~\eqref{eq:wchi-recall}, since the top-normalized solution is not identically zero.
Consequently $\hat q_{\mathrm w}\equiv0$, $\hat{\mathbf v}=0$, and $\hat{\mathbb U}=0$, a contradiction.
This is the form at a single wavenumber of the exclusion of modes with $\hat\zeta=0$ in \cref{prop:reduction}.
So $\hat\zeta\neq0$, and, as in the proof of \cref{lem:determinant}\ref{it:det-modes}, the displayed dispersion relation with $\hat\zeta=\mu w(0;k,c)/(\mathrm{i} kV(0))$ and $\hat q(0)=\varepsilon\rho_{\mathrm{w}}\,\mu\,\bar{\chi}(0;k,c)/(\mathrm{i} k)$ is equivalent to $\mathfrak{E}(k,c,\varepsilon)=0$.

Conversely, if $\mathfrak{E}(k,c,\varepsilon)=0$, then $w(0;k,c)\neq0$ by \cref{lem:determinant}\ref{it:det-nonvanish}.
Let $\hat w:=w(\cdot;k,c)$ and $\hat q:=\varepsilon\rho_{\mathrm{w}}\bar{\chi}(\cdot;k,c)/(\mathrm{i} k)$, and set $\hat s:=0$, $\hat u:=-(U'\hat w+\mathrm{i} k\hat q/\rho_0)/(\mathrm{i} kV)$, $\hat\zeta:=\hat w(0)/(\mathrm{i} kV(0))\neq0$, $\hat q_{\mathrm w}$ by Equation~\eqref{eq:qw-solve}, and $\hat{\mathbf v}:=-\nabla_k\hat q_{\mathrm w}/(\lambda\rho_{\mathrm{w}})$.
All regularity and domain conditions hold.
Indeed, $\hat w,\hat q\in C^1$, the field $\hat{\mathbf v}$ is smooth and divergence-free with $\hat v_z(-h_{\mathrm{w}})=0$, and $\hat w(0)=\hat v_z(0)$ and the $\hat\zeta$-component of $\mathbb{L}_k\hat{\mathbb U}=\lambda\hat{\mathbb U}$ hold because $\mathfrak{E}=0$ gives the displayed relation, read in the reverse direction.
Finally $(\mathbb{L}_k-\lambda)\hat{\mathbb U}=0$ by construction.

For the dimension, the argument above shows that every eigenfunction has the air component $\mu\,(w,\bar{\chi})(\cdot;k,c)$ for some $\mu\in\mathbb{C}$, and that its other components are then determined by the formulas above.
Hence the eigenspace is spanned by the eigenfunction with $\mu=1$.
<!-- end proof-39 -->
<!-- proposition-14: body_md -->
\label{prop:realization}
Let $k>0$.
Then the following hold.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item\label{it:real-split} The map $\mathcal C_k:\mathbb X_k\to\mathbb Y_{\mathrm{con},k}$ is split surjective, that is, it is bounded, its range is all of $\mathbb Y_{\mathrm{con},k}$, and it has a bounded linear right inverse $\mathcal C_k^{\dagger}:\mathbb Y_{\mathrm{con},k}\to\mathbb X_k$, so that $\mathcal C_k\mathcal C_k^{\dagger}=\mathrm{Id}$, the identity map of $\mathbb Y_{\mathrm{con},k}$.
Hence $\mathfrak T_k=\ker\mathcal C_k$ is a closed complemented subspace of $\mathbb X_k$, that is, every element of $\mathbb X_k$ is, in exactly one way, the sum of an element of $\mathfrak T_k$ and an element of the closed subspace $\operatorname{ran}\mathcal C_k^{\dagger}$, the range of $\mathcal C_k^{\dagger}$.
A right inverse is obtained as follows.
Given $(\hat f,\mathfrak c_1,\mathfrak c_2,\mathfrak c_3,\mathfrak c_4)\in\mathbb Y_{\mathrm{con},k}$, set $\hat\zeta:=0$, $\hat r:=\hat s:=\hat u:=0$, and
\begin{equation}
\hat v_z(z):=\mathfrak c_4+\int_{-h_{\mathrm{w}}}^{z}\hat f(z')\,\,\mathrm{d} z',
\qquad
\hat v_x:=0,
\end{equation}
so that $\mathrm{i} k\hat v_x+\hat v_z'=\hat f$ and $\hat v_z(-h_{\mathrm{w}})=\mathfrak c_4$ exactly.
Choose $\hat w\in H^3$ with $\hat w(0)=\mathfrak c_1+\hat v_z(0)$ and $\hat w(h_{\mathrm{a}})=\mathfrak c_3$, and $\hat q_{\mathrm w}\in H^3$ with $\hat q_{\mathrm w}(0)=\mathfrak c_2$.
Every step is a bounded linear map of $(\hat f,\mathfrak c_1,\dots,\mathfrak c_4)$.
No compatibility condition arises, because the value $\hat v_z(0)$ is not prescribed.
\item\label{it:real-traj} Let $t_1>0$, and let $t\mapsto\hat{\mathbb V}(t)\in\mathbb X_k$ be continuously differentiable on $[0,t_1]$.
Then the following two statements are equivalent.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
\item $\hat{\mathbb V}(t)\in\mathfrak T_k$ for all $t$, and $\hat{\mathbb V}$ solves the Eulerian evolution $\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d} t}\Pi_{\mathrm{ev}}\hat{\mathbb V} =\mathbb A_k\hat{\mathbb V}$ of Equation~\eqref{eq:euler-evolution}.
Equivalently, by \cref{prop:goodunknown}, the flattened variation $\mathcal T\hat{\mathbb V}$ solves the linearized flattened system.
\item The curve $\hat{\mathbb U}(t):=\mathcal Q_k\Pi_{\mathrm{ev}} \hat{\mathbb V}(t)$ satisfies $\hat{\mathbb U}\in C([0,t_1];\mathcal D(\mathbb{L}_k))\cap C^1([0,t_1];\mathcal H_k)$ and $\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d} t}\hat{\mathbb U}=\mathbb{L}_k\hat{\mathbb U}$, and the water pressure of $\hat{\mathbb V}(t)$ is $\mathcal P_k(\hat q(0),\hat\zeta)$ for every $t$.
Here $C([0,t_1];\mathcal D(\mathbb{L}_k))$ is the space of maps from $[0,t_1]$ into $\mathcal D(\mathbb{L}_k)$ that are continuous in the graph norm, and $C^1([0,t_1];\mathcal H_k)$ is the space of continuously differentiable maps from $[0,t_1]$ into $\mathcal H_k$, so $\hat{\mathbb U}$ is a classical trajectory of $\mathbb{L}_k$ exactly when it lies in both spaces and satisfies $\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d} t}\hat{\mathbb U}=\mathbb{L}_k\hat{\mathbb U}$.
\end{enumerate}
\item\label{it:real-core} On the $H^3$ core,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:realization-identity}
\mathbb{L}_k\,\mathcal Q_k\Pi_{\mathrm{ev}}\hat{\mathbb V}
=\mathcal Q_k\,
\mathbb A_k^{\mathrm{red}}\hat{\mathbb V},
\qquad
\hat{\mathbb V}\in\mathfrak D_k,
\qquad
\mathcal Q_k\Pi_{\mathrm{ev}}\mathfrak D_k\subset\mathcal D(\mathbb{L}_k),
\end{equation}
and $\mathcal Q_k\Pi_{\mathrm{ev}}\mathfrak D_k$ is a core for $\mathbb{L}_k$, that is, a subspace of the domain on which the closure of the restriction of $\mathbb{L}_k$ is $\mathbb{L}_k$ itself.
In other words, $\mathbb{L}_k$ is the graph closure of $\mathbb A_k^{\mathrm{red}}$ on the $H^3$ core $\mathfrak D_k$, after the change of variables $\mathcal Q_k$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end proposition-14 -->
<!-- proof-40: body_md -->
For \ref{it:real-split}, the displayed choices give $\mathrm{i} k\hat v_x+\hat v_z'=\hat f$, and $\hat v_z\in H^3$ because an antiderivative of an $H^2$ function on an interval lies in $H^3$.
The functions $\hat w$ and $\hat q_{\mathrm w}$ can be taken polynomial in $z$, with coefficients linear in the prescribed values.
Applying $\mathcal C_k$ to the constructed tuple returns $(\hat f,\mathfrak c_1,\mathfrak c_2,\mathfrak c_3,\mathfrak c_4)$, the third component because $\hat q=0$ and $\hat\zeta=0$, and each step is bounded and linear.
With $\mathrm{Id}$ now the identity map of $\mathbb X_k$, the map $\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal C_k^{\dagger}\mathcal C_k$ is a projection, that is, it equals its own square, because $\mathcal C_k\mathcal C_k^{\dagger}$ is the identity map of $\mathbb Y_{\mathrm{con},k}$.
It maps $\mathbb X_k$ onto $\ker\mathcal C_k$ and vanishes on $\operatorname{ran}\mathcal C_k^{\dagger}$, which is closed because $\mathcal C_k$ is a bounded left inverse of $\mathcal C_k^{\dagger}$.

For \ref{it:real-traj}, we first prove that (a) implies (b).
Membership in $\mathfrak T_k$ gives, for every $t$, that $\mathrm{i} k\hat v_x+\hat v_z'=0$ and $\hat v_z(-h_{\mathrm{w}})=0$, so $(\hat v_x,\hat v_z)\in\mathcal V_k$, that $\hat w(h_{\mathrm{a}})=0$ and $\hat w(0)=\hat v_z(0)$, so $\mathcal Q_k\Pi_{\mathrm{ev}}\hat{\mathbb V}(t)\in \mathcal D(\mathbb{L}_k)$, and that the dynamic condition gives the interface value of the water pressure in Equation~\eqref{eq:qw-solve}.
For the interior profile, we differentiate the divergence and bottom constraints in time and insert the water components of $\mathbb A_k$, which gives
\begin{equation}
0=\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d} t}\bigl(\mathrm{i} k\hat v_x+\hat v_z'\bigr)
=-\frac1{\rho_{\mathrm{w}}}\bigl(\mathrm{i} k(\mathrm{i} k\hat q_{\mathrm w})
+\hat q_{\mathrm w}''\bigr)
=\frac1{\rho_{\mathrm{w}}}\bigl(k^2\hat q_{\mathrm w}
-\hat q_{\mathrm w}''\bigr),
\qquad
0=\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d} t}\hat v_z(-h_{\mathrm{w}})
=-\frac{\hat q_{\mathrm w}'(-h_{\mathrm{w}})}{\rho_{\mathrm{w}}}.
\end{equation}
So $\hat q_{\mathrm w}$ solves $\hat q_{\mathrm w}''=k^2\hat q_{\mathrm w}$ with $\hat q_{\mathrm w}'(-h_{\mathrm{w}})=0$ and the interface value above, and therefore $\hat q_{\mathrm w}=\mathcal P_k(\hat q(0),\hat\zeta)$.
It remains to compare the components of $\mathbb A_k$ with those of $\mathbb{L}_k$ in Equation~\eqref{eq:Lop-def}.
Under $\mathcal Q_k$, the $\hat q$-component is $a^2$ times the $\hat r$-component plus $P_S$ times the $\hat s$-component of $\mathbb A_k$, which is the third component of Equation~\eqref{eq:Lop-def} by $p_0'=a^2\rho_0'$, as in the computation after \cref{def:modal-operator}.
The $\hat u$-, $\hat w$-, and $\hat s$-components are identical.
The water and $\hat\zeta$-components are identical once $\hat q_{\mathrm w}=\mathcal P_k(\hat q(0),\hat\zeta)$.
Hence $\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d} t}\hat{\mathbb U}=\mathbb{L}_k\hat{\mathbb U}$.
Since $\hat{\mathbb V}$ is $C^1$ with values in $\mathbb X_k$, the curve $\hat{\mathbb U}$ is $C^1$ with values in $\mathcal H_k$, and $\mathbb{L}_k\hat{\mathbb U}$ is continuous in $\mathcal H_k$ by the formulas in Equation~\eqref{eq:Lop-def}, so $\hat{\mathbb U}$ is continuous in the graph norm.
This is (b).
For the converse, let (b) hold.
The constraint equations hold for every $t$, because the divergence and bottom conditions are built into $\mathcal V_k$, the top and kinematic conditions into $\mathcal D(\mathbb{L}_k)$, and the dynamic condition is the interface value in Equation~\eqref{eq:qw-solve}.
Since $\hat r=(\hat q-P_S\hat s)/a^2$ by the definition of $\mathcal Q_k$, the evolution equations in Equation~\eqref{eq:euler-evolution} are Equation~\eqref{eq:Lop-def} read backward through $\mathcal Q_k$, which gives (a).

For \ref{it:real-core}, the identity in Equation~\eqref{eq:realization-identity} is the comparison of the components just performed, now stated for single states, so that no time derivative enters.
For $\hat{\mathbb V}\in\mathfrak D_k$, we have $\hat q_{\mathrm w}=\mathcal P_k(\hat q(0),\hat\zeta)$ by definition.
Hence $\mathbb A_k^{\mathrm{red}}\hat{\mathbb V}=\mathbb A_k\hat{\mathbb V}$, and each component of $\mathcal Q_k\mathbb A_k\hat{\mathbb V}$ equals the corresponding component of $\mathbb{L}_k\mathcal Q_k\Pi_{\mathrm{ev}}\hat{\mathbb V}$.
The domain membership was checked in the proof of \ref{it:real-traj}.

For the core property, let $\hat{\mathbb U}=(\hat u,\hat w,\hat q,\hat s,\hat{\mathbf v}, \hat\zeta)\in\mathcal D(\mathbb{L}_k)$.
We approximate $\hat{\mathbb U}$ in the graph norm by elements of $\mathcal Q_k\Pi_{\mathrm{ev}}\mathfrak D_k$.
Since $\hat v_x=\mathrm{i}\hat v_z'/k$ on $\mathcal V_k$, the water component is determined by $\hat v_z\in H^1$ with $\hat v_z(-h_{\mathrm{w}})=0$.
Choose smooth $\check u_n\to\hat u$ and $\check s_n\to\hat s$ in $L^2$, and smooth $\check q_n\to\hat q$, $\check w_n\to\hat w$, and $\check v_{z,n}\to\hat v_z$ in $H^1$.
Choose smooth functions $\psi_0,\psi_1$ on $[0,h_{\mathrm{a}}]$ with $\psi_0(0)=1$, $\psi_0(h_{\mathrm{a}})=0$, $\psi_1(0)=0$, $\psi_1(h_{\mathrm{a}})=1$, and $\psi_2$ on $[-h_{\mathrm{w}},0]$ with $\psi_2(-h_{\mathrm{w}})=1$, $\psi_2(0)=0$.
We correct the boundary values by adding multiples of these functions,
\begin{equation}
\hat v_{z,n}:=\check v_{z,n}-\check v_{z,n}(-h_{\mathrm{w}})\,\psi_2,
\qquad
\hat w_n:=\check w_n-\check w_n(h_{\mathrm{a}})\,\psi_1
-\bigl[\check w_n(0)-\hat v_{z,n}(0)\bigr]\,\psi_0 .
\end{equation}
Then $\hat w_n(h_{\mathrm{a}})=0$, $\hat v_{z,n}(-h_{\mathrm{w}})=0$, and $\hat w_n(0)=\hat v_{z,n}(0)$ hold exactly.
Every coefficient of $\psi_0$, $\psi_1$, and $\psi_2$ tends to $0$, because values at the endpoints converge under $H^1$ convergence and the limits satisfy $\hat v_z(-h_{\mathrm{w}})=0$, $\hat w(h_{\mathrm{a}})=0$, and $\hat w(0)=\hat v_z(0)$.
Hence $\hat w_n\to\hat w$ and $\hat v_{z,n}\to\hat v_z$ in $H^1$.
We complete the water component by
\begin{equation}
\hat v_{x,n}:=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{k}\,\hat v_{z,n}',
\qquad
\hat{\mathbf v}_n:=(\hat v_{x,n},\hat v_{z,n}),
\end{equation}
so that $\mathrm{i} k\hat v_{x,n}+\hat v_{z,n}'=0$ exactly and $\hat v_{x,n}\to\hat v_x=\mathrm{i}\hat v_z'/k$ in $L^2$.
The approximating tuple is then
\begin{equation}
\hat{\mathbb V}_n
:=\Bigl(\frac{\check q_n-P_S\check s_n}{a^2},\
\check u_n,\ \hat w_n,\ \check s_n,\
\frac{\mathrm{i}}{k}\hat v_{z,n}',\ \hat v_{z,n},\
\mathcal P_k\bigl(\check q_n(0),\hat\zeta\bigr),\
\hat\zeta\Bigr) .
\end{equation}
It lies in $\mathfrak D_k$, because the divergence and bottom conditions hold exactly by the construction of $\hat{\mathbf v}_n$, the kinematic and top conditions by the corrected boundary values, and the dynamic condition and the harmonic profile by the definition of $\mathcal P_k$.
Its image $\hat{\mathbb U}_n:=\mathcal Q_k\Pi_{\mathrm{ev}}\hat{\mathbb V}_n=(\check u_n,\hat w_n,\check q_n,\check s_n,\hat{\mathbf v}_n,\hat\zeta)$ lies in $\mathcal D(\mathbb{L}_k)$.
The components of Equation~\eqref{eq:Lop-def} involve $\hat w$ and $\hat q$ through at most one derivative and the values at the endpoints, $\hat u$ and $\hat s$ through multiplication by bounded coefficients, and $\hat{\mathbf v}$ through the water pressure and the value $\hat v_z(0)$.
The water pressure $\mathcal P_k(\check q_n(0),\hat\zeta)$ converges to $\mathcal P_k(\hat q(0),\hat\zeta)$ with all its derivatives, uniformly on $[-h_{\mathrm{w}},0]$, because $\check q_n(0)\to\hat q(0)$.
Since $\hat w_n$, $\check q_n$, and $\hat v_{z,n}$ converge in $H^1$ and $\check u_n$ and $\check s_n$ converge in $L^2$, the images $\hat{\mathbb U}_n$ converge to $\hat{\mathbb U}$ in the graph norm of $\mathbb{L}_k$.
<!-- end proof-40 -->
<!-- remark-16: body_md -->
\label{rem:realisation-scope}
\cref{prop:realization} identifies $\mathbb{L}_k$ as the graph closure of $\mathbb A_k^{\mathrm{red}}$ on the $H^3$ core, not of the full evolution $\mathbb A_k$.
On a state that satisfies the constraints, the constraint equations determine only the interface value of the water pressure, and the harmonic profile is selected by the requirement that the evolution preserve the divergence and bottom constraints, as in the differential-algebraic structure of Equation~\eqref{eq:flat-system}.
The image $\mathbb A_k^{\mathrm{red}}\hat{\mathbb V}$ of a state $\hat{\mathbb V}\in\mathfrak D_k$ need not itself satisfy the constraints.
The conditions that the image also satisfies the constraints are called higher compatibility conditions, and they concern the time derivative along a classical trajectory.
They are part of the definition of the domains of the powers of $\mathbb{L}_k$, since a state lies in the domain of $\mathbb{L}_k^2$ exactly when it lies in $\mathcal D(\mathbb{L}_k)$ and its image under $\mathbb{L}_k$ lies in $\mathcal D(\mathbb{L}_k)$ again.
Together with \cref{prop:goodunknown}, \cref{prop:realization} proves \cref{thm:operator}\ref{it:op-realized}.
<!-- end remark-16 -->
<!-- proposition-15: body_md -->
\label{prop:resolvent}
Let $k>0$, $\varepsilon>0$, and a compact set $\Sigma\subset\{c\in\mathbb{C}:\operatorname{Im} c\neq0\}$ be given.
There is a constant $C$, which depends on $\Sigma$, such that for every $c\in\Sigma$ with $\mathfrak{E}(k,c,\varepsilon)\neq0$, the operator $\lambda-\mathbb{L}_k$ with $\lambda=-\mathrm{i} kc$ is a bijection of $\mathcal D(\mathbb{L}_k)$ onto $\mathcal H_k$, so $\lambda$ lies in the resolvent set of $\mathbb{L}_k$, and
\begin{equation}
\bigl\|(\lambda-\mathbb{L}_k)^{-1}\bigr\|_{\mathcal H_k\to\mathcal H_k}
\ \le\ C\Bigl(1+\frac1{|\mathfrak{E}(k,c,\varepsilon)|}\Bigr).
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-15 -->
<!-- proof-41: body_md -->
Since $\mathfrak{E}(k,c,\varepsilon)\neq0$, \cref{thm:pointspec} shows that $\lambda$ is not an eigenvalue of $\mathbb{L}_k$, so $(\lambda-\mathbb{L}_k)\hat{\mathbb U}=0$ only for $\hat{\mathbb U}=0$, and the equation $(\lambda-\mathbb{L}_k)\hat{\mathbb U}=\mathbb F$ has at most one solution.
To construct a solution and bound it, let $\mathbb F\in\mathcal H_k$ be given, with the components $\hat F_u$, $\hat F_w$, $\hat F_q$, $\hat F_s$, $\hat{\mathbf F}_v=(\hat F_{v,x},\hat F_{v,z})\in\mathcal V_k$, and $\hat F_\zeta$, in the order of the components of $\hat{\mathbb U}$.
We solve $(\lambda-\mathbb{L}_k)\hat{\mathbb U}=\mathbb F$ by the elimination in the proof of \cref{thm:pointspec}, now with these source terms.
The $\hat s$-component of this equation is $\mathrm{i} kV\hat s=\hat F_s$, so $\hat s=\hat F_s/(\mathrm{i} kV)\in L^2$.
The $\hat u$-component gives $\mathrm{i} kV\hat u=\hat F_u-U'\hat w-\mathrm{i} k\hat q/\rho_0$.
When we substitute these expressions into the $\hat q$- and $\hat w$-components as in the proof of \cref{thm:pointspec}, the pair $(\hat w,\bar{\chi})$ with $\bar{\chi}=\mathrm{i} k\hat q/(\varepsilon\rho_{\mathrm{w}})$ satisfies the inhomogeneous system
\begin{equation}\label{eq:inhomog}
\hat w'=\Bigl(\frac{U'}{V}+b\Bigr)\hat w
+\frac{\bar{\chi}}{\bar{A} V}+\mathfrak s_w,
\qquad
\bar{\chi}'=-b\,\bar{\chi}+\bar{\rho}_0 k^2V\hat w+\mathfrak s_\chi,
\end{equation}
with the source terms
\begin{equation}
\mathfrak s_w
:=\frac{\hat F_q}{\rho_0a^2}-\frac{\hat F_u}{V},
\qquad
\mathfrak s_\chi
:=\frac{\mathrm{i} k\,\rho_0\hat F_w}{\varepsilon\rho_{\mathrm{w}}}
+\frac{b\,P_S\hat F_s}{\varepsilon\rho_{\mathrm{w}}\,V},
\end{equation}
which are linear in $\mathbb F$.
The first comes from the elimination of $\hat u$ in the $\hat q$-component, and the second from the $\hat w$-component with $\hat r=(\hat q-P_S\hat s)/a^2$ and the value of $\hat s$.
On $\Sigma$, all denominators are bounded below, because $|V(z)|=|U(z)-c|\ge|\operatorname{Im} c|\ge\min_{c'\in\Sigma}|\operatorname{Im} c'|>0$, and $|a^2-V^2|$ is bounded below on the compact set $\Sigma$ by the argument of \cref{lem:regular-solve}.
Hence $\|\mathfrak s_w\|_{L^2}+\|\mathfrak s_\chi\|_{L^2}\le C\|\mathbb F\|_{\mathcal H_k}$ uniformly on $\Sigma$.
Let $Y:=(w,\bar{\chi})(\cdot;k,c)$ be the top-normalized solution, and let $\breve Y=(\breve w,\breve\chi)$ be the solution of the homogeneous system with the values $(\breve w,\breve\chi)(h_{\mathrm{a}})=(1,0)$, where $\breve\chi$ is the component that corresponds to $\bar{\chi}$.
Their modified Wronskian, as in \cref{prop:local}\ref{it:basis-wronskian}, is $\Xi=w\breve\chi-\breve w\bar{\chi}=\varkappa_{\mathrm{top}}V$ with the constant $\varkappa_{\mathrm{top}}=\Xi(h_{\mathrm{a}})/V(h_{\mathrm{a}})=-\bar{A}(h_{\mathrm{a}})\neq0$, by the computation in the proof of \cref{prop:convergence}\ref{it:conv-matrices}, which is valid for $\operatorname{Im} c\neq0$.
Variation of constants gives the particular solution that vanishes at $z=h_{\mathrm{a}}$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:vop}
Y_p(z):=Y(z)\int_{h_{\mathrm{a}}}^{z}
\frac{\breve\chi\,\mathfrak s_w-\breve w\,\mathfrak s_\chi}
{\varkappa_{\mathrm{top}}V}\,\,\mathrm{d} z'
\;+\;\breve Y(z)\int_{h_{\mathrm{a}}}^{z}
\frac{w\,\mathfrak s_\chi-\bar{\chi}\,\mathfrak s_w}
{\varkappa_{\mathrm{top}}V}\,\,\mathrm{d} z' .
\end{equation}
Every solution of Equation~\eqref{eq:inhomog} has the form $\mu Y+\breve\mu\breve Y+Y_p$ with constants $\mu$ and $\breve\mu$, and the condition $\hat w(h_{\mathrm{a}})=0$ gives $\breve\mu=0$, because $w(h_{\mathrm{a}})=0$, $\breve w(h_{\mathrm{a}})=1$, and $Y_p(h_{\mathrm{a}})=0$.
On $\Sigma$, the particular solution obeys
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Xp-bound}
\|Y_p\|_{H^1(0,h_{\mathrm{a}})\times H^1(0,h_{\mathrm{a}})}\ \le\
C\,\|\mathbb F\|_{\mathcal H_k},
\end{equation}
since the homogeneous solutions and $1/(\varkappa_{\mathrm{top}}V)$ are bounded on the compact set $\Sigma$, the integrals are bounded by the $L^1$ norms of the source terms, which are at most $C\|\mathbb F\|_{\mathcal H_k}$, and the derivatives of $Y_p$ are recovered from Equation~\eqref{eq:inhomog} itself.

On the water side, the water component gives $\hat{\mathbf v}=(\hat{\mathbf F}_v -\nabla_k\hat q_{\mathrm w}/\rho_{\mathrm{w}})/\lambda$, which lies in $\mathcal V_k$ because $\hat{\mathbf F}_v$ does and $\hat q_{\mathrm w}$ solves $\hat q_{\mathrm w}''=k^2\hat q_{\mathrm w}$ with $\hat q_{\mathrm w}'(-h_{\mathrm{w}})=0$.
The $\hat\zeta$-component and the domain constraint $\hat w(0)=\hat v_z(0)$ give $\hat\zeta=(\hat w(0)+\hat F_\zeta)/\lambda$.
When we combine these two components, the domain constraint, and the interface value $\hat q_{\mathrm w}(0)=\hat q(0)+G_\sigma\hat\zeta$ from Equation~\eqref{eq:qw-solve}, as in the proof of \cref{thm:pointspec}, we obtain the single relation
\begin{equation}\label{eq:interface-functional}
\mathcal I(\hat w,\hat q)
:=\bigl[\lambda^2\rho_{\mathrm{w}}+k\tanh(kh_{\mathrm{w}})\,G_\sigma\bigr]\hat w(0)
+\lambda\,k\tanh(kh_{\mathrm{w}})\,\hat q(0)
=\mathfrak d(\mathbb F),
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
\mathfrak d(\mathbb F)
:=\lambda\rho_{\mathrm{w}}\,\hat F_{v,z}(0)-k\tanh(kh_{\mathrm{w}})\,G_\sigma\,
\hat F_\zeta
\end{equation}
is a bounded linear functional of $\mathbb F$, that is, a bounded linear map from $\mathcal H_k$ to $\mathbb{C}$.
It is bounded because $\hat F_{v,z}'=-\mathrm{i} k\hat F_{v,x}\in L^2$ on $\mathcal V_k$, so $\hat F_{v,z}\in H^1$ and its value at $z=0$ is bounded by its $H^1$ norm.
The same substitutions as in the proof of \cref{lem:determinant} give the identity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ell-Edet}
\mathcal I\bigl(w(\cdot;k,c),\ \varepsilon\rho_{\mathrm{w}}\bar{\chi}(\cdot;k,c)/(\mathrm{i} k)\bigr)
=-k\tanh(kh_{\mathrm{w}})\,\rho_{\mathrm{w}}\;\mathfrak{E}(k,c,\varepsilon).
\end{equation}
Let $\mathcal I(Y_p)$ denote the value of $\mathcal I$ at the first component of $Y_p$ and at $\varepsilon\rho_{\mathrm{w}}/(\mathrm{i} k)$ times its second component.
Imposing Equation~\eqref{eq:interface-functional} on $\mu Y+Y_p$ then determines
\begin{equation}
\mu=\frac{\mathfrak d(\mathbb F)-\mathcal I(Y_p)}
{-k\tanh(kh_{\mathrm{w}})\,\rho_{\mathrm{w}}\,\mathfrak{E}(k,c,\varepsilon)} .
\end{equation}
The bounds now follow in order,
\begin{equation}
|\mathcal I(Y_p)|+|\mathfrak d(\mathbb F)|\le C\,\|\mathbb F\|_{\mathcal H_k},
\qquad
|\mu|\le\frac{C}{|\mathfrak{E}(k,c,\varepsilon)|}\,\|\mathbb F\|_{\mathcal H_k} ,
\end{equation}
the first by Equation~\eqref{eq:Xp-bound}, the bound on the value at $z=0$ of an $H^1$ function, and the boundedness of the coefficients.
We reconstruct $\hat q,\hat u,\hat s,\hat\zeta,\hat q_{\mathrm w},\hat{\mathbf v}$ from $\mu Y+Y_p$ and the formulas above, and obtain
\begin{equation}
\|\hat w\|_{H^1}+\|\hat q\|_{H^1}
+\|\hat u\|_{L^2}+\|\hat s\|_{L^2}
+\|\hat{\mathbf v}\|_{L^2}+|\hat\zeta|
\ \le\ C\Bigl(1+\frac1{|\mathfrak{E}(k,c,\varepsilon)|}\Bigr)\|\mathbb F\|_{\mathcal H_k} .
\end{equation}
The bounds for $\hat w$ and $\hat q$ come from $\mu Y+Y_p$ and Equation~\eqref{eq:Xp-bound}, those for $\hat u$ and $\hat s$ from the formulas for them above, with $|V|$ bounded below, and those for $\hat\zeta$, $\hat q_{\mathrm w}$, and $\hat{\mathbf v}$ from the interface formulas with $|\lambda|=k|c|\ge k|\operatorname{Im} c|$ bounded below on $\Sigma$.
This yields $\hat{\mathbb U}\in\mathcal D(\mathbb{L}_k)$ with $(\lambda-\mathbb{L}_k)\hat{\mathbb U}=\mathbb F$, each component holding by construction, and the stated bound on the resolvent.
<!-- end proof-41 -->
<!-- corollary-6: body_md -->
\label{cor:algsimple}
Let $c_{\mathrm u}$ with $\operatorname{Im} c_{\mathrm u}>0$ be a zero of $\mathfrak{E}(k,\cdot,\varepsilon)$ with $\partial_c\mathfrak{E}(k,c_{\mathrm u},\varepsilon)\neq0$, equivalently, by Equation~\eqref{eq:Edet-factor}, a simple root of $D(k,\cdot,\varepsilon)$ at which $w(0;k,c_{\mathrm u})\neq0$.
Then $\lambda_{\mathrm u}=-\mathrm{i} kc_{\mathrm u}$ is an isolated point of the spectrum of $\mathbb{L}_k$, and the resolvent has a pole of first order there, that is, $\|(\lambda-\mathbb{L}_k)^{-1}\|_{\mathcal H_k\to\mathcal H_k}\le C/|\lambda-\lambda_{\mathrm u}|$ for $\lambda\neq\lambda_{\mathrm u}$ near $\lambda_{\mathrm u}$.
For every sufficiently small $r>0$, the Riesz projection
\begin{equation}
\mathbb P:=\frac1{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|\lambda-\lambda_{\mathrm u}|=r}
(\lambda-\mathbb{L}_k)^{-1}\,\,\mathrm{d}\lambda ,
\end{equation}
which is the spectral projection onto the eigenvalue $\lambda_{\mathrm u}$, has rank one.
Hence the eigenvalue is algebraically simple, and $\ker\bigl((\mathbb{L}_k-\lambda_{\mathrm u})^{m}\bigr)=\ker(\mathbb{L}_k-\lambda_{\mathrm u})$ is one-dimensional for every integer $m\ge1$.
In particular, under the assumptions of \cref{thm:main}, the unstable root $c_\varepsilon$ of \cref{thm:main} is a simple zero of $\mathfrak{E}(k,\cdot,\varepsilon)$ for every $\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_0)$ by \cref{thm:main}\ref{it:main-exist} and Equation~\eqref{eq:Edet-factor}, so the unstable eigenvalue $\lambda_\varepsilon=-\mathrm{i} kc_\varepsilon$ is algebraically simple for every $\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_0)$.
<!-- end corollary-6 -->
<!-- proof-42: body_md -->
The function $\mathfrak{E}(k,\cdot,\varepsilon)$ is holomorphic near $c_{\mathrm u}$ (\cref{lem:determinant}) and has a simple zero there.
By Taylor's theorem, there is $r_1\in(0,\operatorname{Im} c_{\mathrm u})$ with $|\mathfrak{E}(k,c,\varepsilon)|\ge\tfrac12|\partial_c\mathfrak{E}(k,c_{\mathrm u},\varepsilon)|\,|c-c_{\mathrm u}|$ for $|c-c_{\mathrm u}|\le r_1$, and these $c$ lie in the upper half-plane.
We apply \cref{prop:resolvent} with $\Sigma=\{c\in\mathbb{C}:|c-c_{\mathrm u}|\le r_1\}$ and use $|\lambda-\lambda_{\mathrm u}|=k|c-c_{\mathrm u}|$.
Then every $\lambda\neq\lambda_{\mathrm u}$ with $|\lambda-\lambda_{\mathrm u}|\le kr_1$ lies in the resolvent set of $\mathbb{L}_k$, and $\|(\lambda-\mathbb{L}_k)^{-1}\|\le C/|\lambda-\lambda_{\mathrm u}|$.
Since $\lambda_{\mathrm u}$ is an eigenvalue by \cref{thm:pointspec}, it is an isolated point of the spectrum.
The resolvent is holomorphic for $0<|\lambda-\lambda_{\mathrm u}|\le kr_1$, and the coefficients of the negative powers $(\lambda-\lambda_{\mathrm u})^{-n}$, $n\ge1$, in its Laurent expansion about $\lambda_{\mathrm u}$, the expansion in positive and negative powers of $\lambda-\lambda_{\mathrm u}$, are
\begin{equation}
\mathbb P_n:=\frac1{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|\lambda-\lambda_{\mathrm u}|=r}
(\lambda-\lambda_{\mathrm u})^{n-1}(\lambda-\mathbb{L}_k)^{-1}\,\,\mathrm{d}\lambda,
\qquad 0<r\le kr_1,
\end{equation}
so that $\mathbb P=\mathbb P_1$.
The circle has length $2\pi r$, the factor $|\lambda-\lambda_{\mathrm u}|^{n-1}$ equals $r^{n-1}$ on it, and the operator norm of the resolvent is at most $C/r$ on it, so $\|\mathbb P_n\|\le Cr^{\,n-1}$ for every such $r$.
Letting $r\to0$ gives $\mathbb P_n=0$ for $n\ge2$, so the pole has order one.
For a closed operator with an isolated spectral point, $\mathbb P$ is a projection that commutes with $\mathbb{L}_k$ on $\mathcal D(\mathbb{L}_k)$ \cite[Ch.~III, \S6.5]{Kato1995}.
A first-order pole makes $\lambda_{\mathrm u}$ semisimple, that is, every generalized eigenvector, a vector $\hat{\mathbb U}$ with $(\mathbb{L}_k-\lambda_{\mathrm u})^m\hat{\mathbb U}=0$ for some $m\ge1$, is an eigenvector.
We verify this and the rank directly.
For the resolvent $(\lambda-\mathbb{L}_k)^{-1}$, the Laurent coefficients satisfy $(\mathbb{L}_k-\lambda_{\mathrm u})\mathbb P_n=\mathbb P_{n+1}$, so $(\mathbb{L}_k-\lambda_{\mathrm u})\mathbb P=\mathbb P_2=0$ \cite[Ch.~III, \S6.5]{Kato1995}.
Hence the range $\operatorname{ran}\mathbb P$ of $\mathbb P$ is contained in $\ker(\mathbb{L}_k-\lambda_{\mathrm u})$, which is one-dimensional by \cref{thm:pointspec}.
The projection $\mathbb P$ acts as the identity on this null space, because every $\hat{\mathbb U}\in\ker(\mathbb{L}_k-\lambda_{\mathrm u})$ satisfies $(\lambda-\mathbb{L}_k)^{-1}\hat{\mathbb U}=(\lambda-\lambda_{\mathrm u})^{-1}\hat{\mathbb U}$, so $\mathbb P\hat{\mathbb U}=\hat{\mathbb U}$.
Hence $\operatorname{ran}\mathbb P=\ker(\mathbb{L}_k-\lambda_{\mathrm u})$, and $\mathbb P$ has rank one.
It remains to exclude generalized eigenvectors that are not eigenvectors.
In particular, the domain $\mathcal D\bigl((\mathbb{L}_k-\lambda_{\mathrm u})^2\bigr)$ of the square is the set of $\hat{\mathbb U}\in\mathcal D(\mathbb{L}_k)$ with $(\mathbb{L}_k-\lambda_{\mathrm u})\hat{\mathbb U}\in\mathcal D(\mathbb{L}_k)$.
If $\hat{\mathbb U}$ lies in this set and $(\mathbb{L}_k-\lambda_{\mathrm u})^2\hat{\mathbb U}=0$, put $\hat{\mathbb W}:=(\mathbb{L}_k-\lambda_{\mathrm u})\hat{\mathbb U}\in\ker(\mathbb{L}_k-\lambda_{\mathrm u})$.
Then $\hat{\mathbb W}=\mathbb P\hat{\mathbb W}=\mathbb P(\mathbb{L}_k-\lambda_{\mathrm u})\hat{\mathbb U}=(\mathbb{L}_k-\lambda_{\mathrm u})\mathbb P\hat{\mathbb U}=0$, because $\mathbb P$ commutes with $\mathbb{L}_k$ on its domain and $(\mathbb{L}_k-\lambda_{\mathrm u})\mathbb P=0$.
So $\ker\bigl((\mathbb{L}_k-\lambda_{\mathrm u})^2\bigr)=\ker(\mathbb{L}_k-\lambda_{\mathrm u})$, and by induction the same holds for every power.
<!-- end proof-42 -->
<!-- remark-17: body_md -->
\label{rem:fulloperator}
The linearized system on $\mathbb{T}_{k_0}$ decomposes over the Fourier modes $k=nk_0$, $n\in\mathbb{Z}$, and the components with $n<0$ are given by the same formulas.
The mean-flow component $n=0$ is not constructed in this paper.
An operator for the full linearized system, which acts on each Fourier mode separately, can be assembled from these components once the component $n=0$ is defined on its space of states that satisfy the constraints.
For every such operator, the eigenfunction of $\mathbb{L}_k$ constructed above, multiplied by $e^{\mathrm{i} kx}$, is an $L^2$ eigenfunction, and under the assumptions of \cref{thm:main} it has the eigenvalue $\lambda_\varepsilon=-\mathrm{i} kc_\varepsilon$ with $\operatorname{Re}\lambda_\varepsilon>0$ (\cref{cor:spectral}).
<!-- end remark-17 -->
<!-- remark-18: body_md -->
\label{rem:generation}
The resolvent is constructed only for $\operatorname{Im} c\neq0$, that is, for $\operatorname{Re}\lambda\neq0$.
The set $\{-\mathrm{i} kU(z):0\le z\leh_{\mathrm{a}}\}$ of values of the coefficient $-\mathrm{i} kU$ in $\mathbb{L}_k$, which corresponds to the real phase speeds in the range of $U$, is the candidate for the continuous spectrum, the set of $\lambda$ that are not eigenvalues and for which the equation $(\lambda-\mathbb{L}_k)\hat{\mathbb U}=\mathbb F$ can be solved for a dense set of $\mathbb F$ but not for every $\mathbb F$.
A limiting-absorption estimate for $(\lambda-\mathbb{L}_k)^{-1}$ as $\lambda$ approaches this set would require an inhomogeneous version of \cref{thm:limabs} and is not established here.
Semigroup generation is not addressed (\cref{rem:spaces}).
<!-- end remark-18 -->
